A generalized coupling approach for the weak approximation of stochastic functional differential equations
Yushi Hamaguchi, and, Dai Taguchi

Abstract
In this paper, we study functional type weak approximation of weak solutions of stochastic functional differential equations by means of the Euler–Maruyama scheme. Under mild assumptions on the coefficients, we provide a quantitative error estimate for the weak approximation in terms of the Lévy–Prokhorov metric of probability laws on the path space. The weak error estimate obtained in this paper is sharp in the topological and quantitative senses in some special cases. We apply our main result to ten concrete examples appearing in a wide range of science and obtain a weak error estimate for each model. The proof of the main result is based on the so-called generalized coupling of probability measures.



Keywords:
Stochastic functional differential equation; weak approximation; generalized coupling; Euler–Maruyama scheme.

2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
: 34K50; 65C30; 60F17; 65C20.

1
Introduction

In this paper, we consider the following stochastic functional differential equation (SFDE) driven by Brownian motion
W
:

\mathrm{d}X(t)=b(t,X)\,\mathrm{d}t+\sigma(t,X)\,\mathrm{d}W(t),\ \ t\in[0,\infty),

(1.1)

where the coefficients
b(t,X)
and
\sigma(t,X)
are allowed to depend on the path of the solution
X=(X(s))_{s\in[0,\infty)}
up to time
t
. The aim of this paper is to show a functional type weak approximation of a weak solution of the SFDE (
1.1
) by means of the Euler–Maruyama scheme, providing a quantitative error estimate for the weak convergence. More precisely, given a weak solution
(X,W,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
to the SFDE (
1.1
), for each partition
\pi=(t_{k})^{m}_{k=0}
of a bounded interval
[0,T]
such that
0=t_{0}<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{m}=T
, we provide an estimate of

d_{\mathrm{LP}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T}),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}])\big)

(1.2)

in terms of the mesh size
|\pi|:=\max_{k\in\{0,\dots,m-1\}}(t_{k+1}-t_{k})
. Here,
d_{\mathrm{LP}}(\cdot,\cdot)
denotes the so-called Lévy–Prokhorov metric between probability measures on the space of continuous functions stopped at time
T
,
\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T})
denotes the law of the stopped path
X_{T}:=X(\cdot\wedge T)=(X(s\wedge T))_{s\in[0,\infty)}
of the given weak solution,
X^{\pi}=(X^{\pi}(t_{k}))^{m}_{k=0}
denotes the Euler–Maruyama scheme defined on a probability space
(\Omega^{\pi},\mathcal{F}^{\pi},\mathbb{P}^{\pi})
, and
\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}])
denotes the law of the linear interpolation
\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}]
of
X^{\pi}
at each point of the partition
\pi
. The convergence to zero of the Lévy–Prokhorov metric (
1.2
) as
|\pi|\downarrow 0
is equivalent to the weak convergence of
\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}]
to
X_{T}
on the path space. Precise definitions of the weak solution, Euler–Maruyama scheme and Lévy–Prokhorov metric are summarized in
Section

2
below. Under mild assumptions on the coefficients, we obtain an error estimate for (
1.2
) by using the “generalized coupling approach”, which has been used in the literature of the ergodicity of infinite dimensional Markovian systems.

In the literature of the weak approximations of numerical schemes for stochastic differential equations (SDEs), most existing works such as
[
9
,
19
,
28
,
36
,
47
,
56
]
consider the convergence order of the weak error measured by

\big|\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[f(X(T))]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}[f(X^{\pi}(T))]\big|

(1.3)

for some fixed
\mathbb{R}
-valued test function
f
on the Euclidean space and fixed time
T\in(0,\infty)
. This kind of convergence analysis does not provide a weak convergence on the path space. In contrast, the weak error estimate in terms of the Lévy–Prokhorov metric (
1.2
) is related to the estimate of

\big|\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[f(X_{T})]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}[f(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}])]\big|

for bounded and Lipschitz continuous test functions
f
on the path space; see
Section

2
for more details. Alfonsi, Jourdain and Kohatsu-Higa
[
4
]
study a functional type weak error estimate for one-dimensional Markovian SDEs in terms of the (
L^{1}
-)Wasserstein metric:

\mathcal{W}_{1}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T}),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\widetilde{X}^{\pi}_{T})\big),

(1.4)

where
\widetilde{X}^{\pi}
is the continuous time extension of the Euler–Maruyama scheme
X^{\pi}=(X^{\pi}(t_{k}))^{m}_{k=0}
by means of the interpolation by the Brownian path. By the Kantorovich–Rubinstein theorem (cf.
[
25
, Theorem 11.8.2]
), the error estimate with respect to the Wasserstein metric (
1.4
) is related to the estimate of

\big|\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[f(X_{T})]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}[f(\widetilde{X}^{\pi}_{T})]\big|

(1.5)

in terms of (not necessarily bounded) Lipschitz continuous test functions
f
on the path space. Assuming that the coefficients are bounded, sufficiently smooth (with bounded derivatives) and uniformly elliptic, they obtain in
[
4
, Theorem 3.2]
a convergence order of almost
|\pi|^{3/4}
for the error (
1.4
), which is faster than the order
\sqrt{|\pi|}
obtained by a direct application of the well-known results
[
42
,
46
]
on the strong error analysis. Also, Ngo and Taguchi
[
59
]
study the error in the form of (
1.5
) for Markovian SDEs with irregular drift and constant diffusion. It is worth mentioning that, besides the choices of the metrics, there is a significant difference in the ways of continuous time extensions of the Euler–Maruyama scheme
X^{\pi}
between the objective (
1.2
) in the present paper and the ones (
1.4
) and (
1.5
) in
[
4
,
59
]
. Our error analysis using the linear interpolation of the Euler–Maruyama scheme is in the spirit of the Donsker-type functional central limit theorem (cf.
[
44
, Chapter 2, Theorem 4.20]
), and the results in
[
4
,
59
]
using the interpolation by the Brownian path do not provide an error estimate for such a purpose. There are only a few works on the Donsker-type functional central limit theorem for SDEs. Ankirchner, Kruse and Urusov
[
8
]
show the functional central limit theorem for one-dimensional Markovian SDEs without drift and with irregular diffusion coefficient, but they do not provide a quantitative error estimate. Lototsky
[
53
]
provides a quantitative error estimate for the functional central limit theorem for Brownian motion in terms of the linear interpolation of Gaussian random walks and applies it to one-dimensional Markovian SDEs with Lipschitz continuous drift coefficient and constant diffusion coefficient. In the latter paper
[
53
]
, it is shown that the sharp convergence order in terms of the Wasserstein metric (which is stronger than the Lévy–Prokhorov metric) is
\sqrt{|\pi|\log\frac{1}{|\pi|}}
. Similarly, it turns out that the weak convergence speed of linear interpolations of any random variables (with respect to the uniform partitions
\pi
of
[0,T]
) to Brownian motion cannot be faster than
\sqrt{|\pi|\log\frac{1}{|\pi|}}
in terms of the Lévy–Prokhorov metric; see
Proposition

4.2
. Hence, the order
\sqrt{|\pi|\log\frac{1}{|\pi|}}
can be seen as a benchmark for the convergence order of the objective (
1.2
) in this paper.

The above mentioned works on the weak convergence of numerical methods require the coefficients to be either Markovian, bounded or smooth with bounded derivatives. In addition, they typically require the uniform ellipticity for the diffusion coefficient. However, these assumptions are restrictive in view of applications. Most of stochastic models appearing in science have unbounded or even super-linearly growing coefficients together with non-uniformly elliptic diffusion coefficient, and they are typically defined only on a proper subset of the Euclidean space. Gyöngy and Krylov
[
30
]
show the convergence in probability of the Euler–Maruyama scheme under a setting including these situations. However, they are still restricted to the Markovian case, and do not provide a convergence rate when the coefficients are super-linearly growing. There are many important non-Markovian models involving strongly nonlinear coefficients as well as path-dependent structures such as the time-delay of the system. This motivates us to study SFDEs (
1.1
) defined only on a domain
D
under mild assumptions on the coefficients. Specifically, the framework in the present paper allows the coefficients to be path-dependent, locally bounded, locally Hölder continuous and non-uniformly elliptic; see
Assumption

1
below for more details. In particular, our framework includes not only the case of super-linearly growing coefficients but also the case of the coefficients involving strongly nonlinear terms such as
x^{-1}
. Under this quite general setting, in our main result (
Theorem

3.1
), we provide a quantitative estimate for the functional type weak approximation in terms of the Lévy–Prokhorov metric (
1.2
). Furthermore, as a by-product of the weak convergence result, we obtain a novel result on uniqueness in law for the SFDE (
1.1
). Our main result can be applied to many examples appearing in science which are beyond the existing works on numerical approximations. In
Section

6
, we apply
Theorem

3.1
to ten concrete examples of Markovian and non-Markovian models appearing in physics, chemistry, mathematical finance, economics, population biology, and so on. Interestingly, the path-dependence of the coefficients enables us to apply our result not only to SDEs with delay but also to some non-standard Markovian SDEs such as reflected SDEs and stochastic oscillator models after appropriate transformations; see
Section

4.4
for more details.

The significance of the main result of this paper lies not only in the generality of the framework as mentioned above but also in the sharpness of the error estimate. Indeed, in a special case of
Theorem

3.1
, we reach the optimal convergence order
\sqrt{|\pi|\log\frac{1}{|\pi|}}
for the Lévy–Prokhorov metric (
1.2
); see
Corollary

3.3
and
Proposition

4.2
. Furthermore, it is worth mentioning that the topology of the weak convergence (which is nothing but the topology generated by the Lévy–Prokhorov metric) obtained in
Theorem

3.1
cannot be improved to the topology generated by the Wasserstein metric in general; see
Example

4.1
for such an example. Indeed, Hutzenthaler, Jentzen and Kloeden
[
38
,
40
]
show that, for any one-dimensional, non-degenerate and Markovian SDEs (defined on the whole space
\mathbb{R}
) with super-linearly growing coefficients, the
p
-th moment
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}[|X^{\pi}(T)|^{p}]
of the (standard) Euler–Maruyama scheme
X^{\pi}
diverges as
|\pi|\downarrow 0
for any
p\in(0,\infty)
and
T\in(0,\infty)
. This implies the divergence of the error in terms of the Wasserstein metric even for the one-dimensional distribution at each time
T\in(0,\infty)
. Recently, due to this difficulty in the strongly nonlinear coefficients case, several authors have considered modified versions of the Euler–Maruyama scheme and showed their strong convergence. For example, in the Markovian setting,
[
39
,
65
]
consider the tamed Euler–Maruyama scheme,
[
3
,
34
,
58
]
consider the backward Euler–Maruyama scheme, and
[
18
]
consider the truncated Euler–Maruyama scheme. Moreover, Guo, Mao and Yue
[
29
]
apply the truncated Euler–Maruyama scheme to the case of SDEs with delay. Unlike this recent trend, in the present paper, we do not consider any modifications of the Euler–Maruyama scheme but focus on the standard one. Even for the case of multi-dimensional, path-dependent and super-linearly growing coefficients case, our results ensure the functional type weak convergence of the standard Euler–Maruyama scheme together with its quantitative error estimate.

Typical methods adopted in the literature to establish quantitative weak error estimates for the Euler–Maruyama scheme are based on the “PDE approach”, which relies on the analysis of the corresponding Kolmogorov equation; see, for example,
[
9
,
28
,
47
,
56
]
. Such methods fundamentally rely on the Markovian structure of SDEs. Recently, Holland
[
36
]
combined the PDE approach with the stochastic sewing lemma of Lê
[
50
]
to improve the weak convergence rate; see also
[
14
,
22
]
for applications of the stochastic sewing lemma to strong convergence analysis. Although the stochastic sewing lemma itself does not rely on the Markovian structure, Holland’s weak error analysis still relies on the Feynman–Kac formula and the corresponding Kolmogorov equation. Extending this approach to the path-dependent SFDE (
1.1
) would require the analysis of an infinite-dimensional Kolmogorov equation and is therefore quite difficult. In the case of SDEs with delay, Clément, Kohatsu-Higa and Lamberton
[
19
]
develop the so-called “duality approach” based on Malliavin calculus, for which smoothness of the coefficients is crucial. Ngo and Taguchi
[
59
]
adopt the “Girsanov transform approach” for Markovian SDEs with an irregular drift coefficient and a constant diffusion coefficient, and Bao and Shao
[
10
]
extend this approach to SDEs with delayed drift coefficients, still assuming a constant diffusion coefficient. These approaches mainly concern weak convergence of finite-dimensional distributions, measured by (
1.3
) at each fixed time
T
. They require the coefficients to be path-independent, bounded, or sufficiently smooth. Moreover, in all the above-mentioned works, boundedness and uniform ellipticity of the diffusion coefficient play crucial roles. The present paper aims to substantially weaken these restrictive assumptions.

In this paper, inspired by the work by Kulik and Scheutzow
[
48
]
on weak well-posedness and ergodicity of SFDEs, we demonstrate a “
generalized coupling approach
”, which is a stochastic control-type approach based on the analysis of the so-called
generalized coupling
of probability measures. A (true) coupling of probability measures is defined as a probability measure on the product space with prescribed marginal laws. It is known that the Lévy–Prokhorov metric has a dual representation by means of couplings; see (
2.2
) in
Section

2.2
below. Hence, it is important to construct a reasonable true coupling in order to give a good estimate for the Lévy–Prokhorov metric (
1.2
). However, the construction and estimate of a reasonable true coupling are quite difficult in general. In contrast, a generalized coupling allows the marginal to have a mild deviation bound instead of the exact coincidence with respect to the prescribed probability measure, and the construction of the generalized coupling satisfying a desired property is typically easier than the construction of the true coupling. The general idea of the generalized coupling approach, which is also known as the Control-and-Reimburse strategy, is “
to apply a stochastic control in order to improve the system, and then to take into account the impact of the control
”
[
48
]
. In the present paper, we introduce a
controlled Euler–Maruyama scheme

\widehat{X}^{\pi}
which “improves” the (true) Euler–Maruyama scheme
X^{\pi}
using additional control parameters. The joint distributions of the pair of
X_{T}
and (the linear interpolation of)
\widehat{X}^{\pi}
can be seen as a generalized coupling between
\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T})
and
\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}])
. The error between a (given) weak solution
X
and the controlled Euler–Maruyama scheme
\widehat{X}^{\pi}
can be estimated by means of the control parameters. Then, taking into account the deviation from the true Euler–Maruyama scheme
X^{\pi}
to the controlled one
\widehat{X}^{\pi}
, which corresponds to the “impact of the control”, we can formulate a “stochastic control problem”. Solving the control problem, we can obtain a desired weak error estimate for the Lévy–Prokhorov metric (
1.2
), showing our main result (
Theorem

3.1
). See
Section

5
for more detailed idea of the generalized coupling approach, construction of the controlled Euler–Maruyama scheme
\widehat{X}^{\pi}
and the proof of
Theorem

3.1
. The idea of the generalized coupling investigated in
[
48
]
originates from Hairer’s work
[
31
]
on exponential mixing properties of stochastic partial differential equations and has been used in ergodic theory of Markov processes; for example, it is used in
[
32
]
to show a general form of Harris’ theorem, in
[
15
]
to show ergodicity of various infinite dimensional Markov processes that may lack the strong Feller property, in
[
74
]
to show the dimension-free Harnack inequality for SDEs, and in
[
11
]
to show the asymptotic log-Harnack inequality for SDEs with infinite delay, among others. To the best of our knowledge, the present paper is the first to apply the generalized coupling approach to theory of numerical approximations for stochastic processes.

The rest of this paper is organized as follows:
Section

2
is a preliminary section, where we introduce the notations which we use throughout the paper, summarize basic properties of the Lévy–Prokhorov metric and define the concepts of weak solutions of SFDEs as well as the Euler–Maruyama scheme. In
Section

3
, we state the main result (
Theorem

3.1
) and show its immediate consequence (
Corollary

3.3
). In
Section

4
, we make remarks on the main result. In particular, we show in
Proposition

4.2
a lower bound for the weak approximation in terms of the Lévy–Prokhorov metric and discuss in
Section

4.4
some special classes of SFDEs included in our framework.
Section

5
is devoted to the proof of
Theorem

3.1
. In the introductory part of this section, we demonstrate the idea of the generalized coupling approach in detail and introduce the controlled Euler–Maruyama scheme. In
Section

6
, we apply our main result to ten concrete examples appearing in a wide range of scientific fields. In
Appendix
, we prove some auxiliary results used in this paper. In particular, the results in
Appendix

D
provide some fundamental estimates for stochastic processes, which are important in their own right.

2
Preliminaries

In this section, we summarize the notations which we use throughout this paper, recall the definition and well-known properties of the Lévy–Prokhorov metric, define the concept of weak solutions of SFDEs and introduce the Euler–Maruyama scheme.

2.1
Notation

We denote by
|\cdot|
the standard Euclidean norm on the space
\mathbb{R}^{n}
of
n
-dimensional (column) vectors or the Frobenius norm on the space
\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
of
n\times d
-matrices for each
n,d\in\mathbb{N}
.
I_{n\times n}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
denotes the identity matrix. For each
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we define
\mathrm{dist}(\xi,A):=\inf_{\eta\in A}|\xi-\eta|
, where we set
\mathrm{dist}(\xi,\emptyset):=\infty
. For each set
A
,
\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{A}
is the corresponding indicator function, and
A^{\complement}
is the complement relative to a given set which is clear from the context.

For a measurable space
(S,\mathcal{S})
, we denote by
\mathcal{P}(S)
the set of all probability measures on
(S,\mathcal{S})
. When
S
is a topological space, we always choose
\mathcal{S}
as the corresponding Borel
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{B}(S)
, and we denote by
\mathrm{supp}\,\mu
the support of a Borel probability measure
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(S)
. For two probability measures
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}(S)
, we denote by
\mathscr{C}(\mu,\nu)\subset\mathcal{P}(S\times S)
the set of all couplings between
\mu
and
\nu
, that is, probability measures
\mathfrak{m}
on the product measurable space
(S\times S,\mathcal{S}\otimes\mathcal{S})
such that
\mathfrak{m}(\cdot\times S)=\mu
and
\mathfrak{m}(S\times\cdot)=\nu
. For each
s\in S
,
\delta_{s}\in\mathcal{P}(S)
denotes the Dirac measure at the point
s
. For each random variable
\xi
on a probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
with values in a measurable space
(S,\mathcal{S})
, we denote by
\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(\xi):=\mathbb{P}\circ\xi^{-1}\in\mathcal{P}(S)
the law of
\xi
under the probability measure
\mathbb{P}
. The expectation under a probability measure
\mathbb{P}
is denoted by
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[\cdot]
; we sometimes denote it by
\mathbb{E}[\cdot]
when the underlying probability
\mathbb{P}
is clear from the context.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. We denote by
\mathcal{C}:=C([0,\infty);\mathbb{R}^{n})
the set of continuous functions from
[0,\infty)
to
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. For each
x\in\mathcal{C}
and
t\in[0,\infty)
,
x(t)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
denotes the value of
x\in\mathcal{C}
at time
t\in[0,\infty)
, and
x_{t}\in\mathcal{C}
denotes the stopped function defined by
x_{t}(s):=x(t\wedge s)
for each
s\in[0,\infty)
. We denote by
\mathcal{C}_{t}
the set of all functions
x\in\mathcal{C}
such that
x=x_{t}
. The set
\mathcal{C}_{t}
becomes a separable Banach space with the norm
\|x\|_{\infty}:=\sup_{s\in[0,t]}|x(s)|
. It is known that the Borel
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{C}_{t})
coincides with the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the family of evaluation maps
\mathcal{C}_{t}\ni x\mapsto x(s)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
s\in[0,t]
. The family
\{\mathcal{G}_{t}\}_{t\in[0,\infty)}
of
\sigma
-algebras
\mathcal{G}_{t}:=\{\{x\in\mathcal{C}\,|\,x_{t}\in A\}\,|\,A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{C}_{t})\}
with
t\in[0,\infty)
defines a filtration on
\mathcal{C}
. We denote
\Lambda:=[0,\infty)\times\mathcal{C}
. We say that a set
\Gamma\subset\Lambda
is progressively measurable if
\Gamma\cap([0,t]\times\mathcal{C})
is
\mathcal{B}([0,t])\otimes\mathcal{G}_{t}
-measurable for any
t\in[0,\infty)
. Furthermore, we say that a map
\varphi:\Gamma\to S
from a progressively measurable set
\Gamma\subset\Lambda
to a measurable space
(S,\mathcal{S})
is progressively measurable if the set
\{(t,x)\in\Gamma\,|\,\varphi(t,x)\in A\}
is progressively measurable for any
A\in\mathcal{S}
. If
\varphi:\Gamma\to S
is progressively measurable and
\mathcal{S}
separates points of
S
, then

\varphi(t,x)=\varphi(t,x_{t})\ \ \text{for all $(t,x)\in\Gamma$}.

Note that a set
\Gamma\subset\Lambda
is progressively measurable if and only if the map
\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{\Gamma}:\Lambda\to\mathbb{R}
is progressively measurable. The family of progressively measurable sets is a
\sigma
-algebra on
\Lambda
.

Let
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
be two subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. We define

\Lambda[D_{1},D_{2}]:=\left\{(t,x)\in\Lambda\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}x(0)\in D_{1}\ \text{and}\ x(s)\in D_{2}\ \text{for all $s\in[0,t]$}\right\}.

Clearly, if
(t,x)\in\Lambda[D_{1},D_{2}]
and
s\in[0,t]
, then
(s,x)\in\Lambda[D_{1},D_{2}]
. Furthermore, if
D_{1}
is a Borel subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and if
D_{2}
is an open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, then the set
\Lambda[D_{1},D_{2}]\subset\Lambda
is progressively measurable. This follows from the right-continuity of
[0,\infty)\ni t\mapsto\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{\Lambda[D_{1},D_{2}]}(t,x)
for each
x\in\mathcal{C}
and the
\mathcal{G}_{t}
-measurability of
\mathcal{C}\ni x\mapsto\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{\Lambda[D_{1},D_{2}]}(t,x)
for each
t\in[0,\infty)
. For simplicity of notation, we abbreviate
\Lambda[D,D]
to
\Lambda[D]
when
D_{1}=D_{2}=D\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Note that
\Lambda[\mathbb{R}^{n}]=\Lambda[\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n}]=\Lambda
.

For each
(t,x)\in\Lambda
and
\delta\in[0,\infty)
, we denote by
\varpi(t,x;\delta)
the modulus of continuity of
x\in\mathcal{C}
on
[0,t]
with length
\delta
, that is,

\varpi(t,x;\delta):=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq r\leq s\leq t\\
s-r\leq\delta\end{subarray}}|x(s)-x(r)|.

Clearly,
\varpi(t,x;0)=\varpi(0,x;\delta)=0
, and the function
\delta\mapsto\varpi(t,x;\delta)
is finite, non-decreasing and continuous. For each
t\in[0,\infty)
and
\delta\in[0,\infty)
, the map
\mathcal{C}\ni x\mapsto\varpi(t,x;\delta)
is
\mathcal{G}_{t}
-measurable. Also, for each
x\in\mathcal{C}
, and
\delta\in[0,\infty)
, it holds that

0\leq\varpi(t,x;\delta)-\varpi(s,x;\delta)\leq\varpi\big(t,x;(t-s)\wedge\delta\big),\ \ 0\leq s\leq t<\infty,

and hence
t\mapsto\varpi(t,x;\delta)
is non-decreasing and continuous. In particular, the map
\Lambda\ni(t,x)\mapsto\varpi(t,x;\delta)
is progressively measurable for each
\delta\in[0,\infty)
.

For each
n
-dimensional vector
\xi=(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n})^{\top}\in(0,\infty)^{n}
, we define
\xi^{-1}:=(\xi_{1}^{-1},\dots,\xi_{n}^{-1})^{\top}\in(0,\infty)^{n}
. Also, for each
(t,x)\in\Lambda[(0,\infty)^{n}]
, we define
x_{t}^{-1}\in\mathcal{C}_{t}
by
x_{t}^{-1}(s):=x_{t}(s)^{-1}=x(t\wedge s)^{-1}\in(0,\infty)^{n}
for each
s\in[0,\infty)
.

Throughout the paper, the natural numbers
n
and
d
typically represent the dimensions of the solution of the SFDE (
1.1
) and Brownian motion, respectively.

2.2
Basic properties of the Lévy–Prokhorov metric

Let
(S,d_{S})
be a complete separable metric space. The Lévy–Prokhorov metric
d_{\mathrm{LP}}(\mu,\nu)
between two probability measures
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}(S)
is defined by

d_{\mathrm{LP}}(\mu,\nu):=\inf\left\{\varepsilon>0\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\mu(A)\leq\nu(A^{\varepsilon})+\varepsilon\ \text{and}\ \nu(A)\leq\mu(A^{\varepsilon})+\varepsilon\ \text{for all}\ A\in\mathcal{B}(S)\right\},

(2.1)

where

A^{\varepsilon}:=\left\{x\in S\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\text{there exists $y\in A$ such that $d_{S}(x,y)<\varepsilon$}\right\}.

It is well-known that
(\mathcal{P}(S),d_{\mathrm{LP}})
is a metric space (cf.
[
25
, Theorems 11.3.1]
), and the weak convergence of probability measures on
(S,\mathcal{B}(S))
is equivalent to the convergence with respect to the Lévy–Prokhorov metric
d_{\mathrm{LP}}
(cf.
[
25
, Theorem 11.3.3]
). Also, for each
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}(S)
, the Lévy–Prokhorov metric
d_{\mathrm{LP}}(\mu,\nu)
can be represented by means of couplings between
\mu
and
\nu
as follows:

d_{\mathrm{LP}}(\mu,\nu)=\inf_{\mathfrak{m}\in\mathscr{C}(\mu,\nu)}\inf\left\{\varepsilon>0\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\mathfrak{m}\big(\left\{(x,y)\in S\times S\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}d_{S}(x,y)>\varepsilon\right\}\big)<\varepsilon\right\}

(2.2)

(cf.
[
25
, Corollary 11.6.4]
). In terms of
S
-valued random variables
\xi
and
\eta
defined on a (common) probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
, the above relation yields

d_{\mathrm{LP}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(\xi),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(\eta)\big)\leq\inf\left\{\varepsilon>0\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\mathbb{P}\big(d_{S}(\xi,\eta)>\varepsilon\big)<\varepsilon\right\}.

(2.3)

The right-hand side above is called the Ky Fan metric between
\xi
and
\eta
, which induces the topology of convergence in probability of
S
-valued random variables on the prescribed probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
(cf.
[
25
, Theorem 9.2.2]
).

In the literature of numerical analysis of SDEs, the weak rate of convergence of an approximation scheme is often measured by means of a functional with respect to a class
\Xi
of measurable test functions
f:S\to\mathbb{R}
. For such a purpose, it is natural to introduce the following quantity:

d_{\Xi}(\mu,\nu):=\sup_{f\in\Xi}\left|\int_{S}f(x)\,\mu(\mathrm{d}x)-\int_{S}f(x)\,\nu(\mathrm{d}x)\right|

for each
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}(S)
such that
\int_{S}|f(x)|\mu(\mathrm{d}x)<\infty
and
\int_{S}|f(x)|\nu(\mathrm{d}x)<\infty
for any
f\in\Xi
. In terms of
S
-valued random variables
\xi
and
\eta
defined on a probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
such that
\mathbb{E}[|f(\xi)|]<\infty
and
\mathbb{E}[|f(\eta)|]<\infty
for any
f\in\Xi
, we have

d_{\Xi}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(\xi),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(\eta)\big)=\sup_{f\in\Xi}\left|\mathbb{E}\big[f(\xi)\big]-\mathbb{E}\big[f(\eta)\big]\right|,

where
\mathbb{E}[\cdot]
denotes the expectation with respect to
\mathbb{P}
. The Lévy–Prokhorov metric is related to the metric
d_{\Xi}
induced by the class
\Xi=\mathrm{BL}
of bounded and Lipschitz continuous functions, that is,

\mathrm{BL}:=\left\{f:S\to\mathbb{R}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\sup_{x\in S}|f(x)|+\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y\in S\\
x\neq y\end{subarray}}\frac{|f(x)-f(y)|}{d_{S}(x,y)}\leq 1\right\}.

(2.4)

Indeed, it is known that

\frac{2d_{\mathrm{LP}}(\mu,\nu)^{2}}{2+d_{\mathrm{LP}}(\mu,\nu)}\leq d_{\mathrm{BL}}(\mu,\nu)\leq 2d_{\mathrm{LP}}(\mu,\nu)\ \ \text{for any $\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}(S)$},

(2.5)

and both the above two inequalities are sharp (cf.
[
25
, Sections 11.3 and 11.6]
).
The above relations show that the topologies induced by the metrics
d_{\mathrm{BL}}
and
d_{\mathrm{LP}}
are the same, which coincide with the weak convergence topology. However, we should be careful when we treat the order of weak convergence. The above relations mean that, for each
\mu,\mu^{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{P}(S)
with
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
and
r\in(0,\infty)
, as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
,

•

the convergence order
d_{\mathrm{LP}}(\mu,\mu^{\varepsilon})=O(\varepsilon^{r})
with respect to the Lévy–Prokhorov metric implies the same convergence order
d_{\mathrm{BL}}(\mu,\mu^{\varepsilon})=O(\varepsilon^{r})
with respect to the metric induced by the class
\mathrm{BL}
of test functions, while

•

the convergence order
d_{\mathrm{BL}}(\mu,\mu^{\varepsilon})=O(\varepsilon^{r})
implies only the convergence order
d_{\mathrm{LP}}(\mu,\mu^{\varepsilon})=O(\varepsilon^{r/2})
in general.

We refer the reader to
Appendix

A
for additional properties of the Lévy–Prokhorov metric that will be utilized in this paper.

In this paper, we are mainly interested in the convergence order with respect to the Lévy–Prokhorov metric
d_{\mathrm{LP}}
associated with the metric space
(S,d_{S})=(\mathcal{C}_{T},\|\cdot-\cdot\|_{\infty})
. From the above observations, we see that such quantitative results imply the same convergence order with respect to
d_{\mathrm{BL}}
corresponding to
(S,d_{S})=(\mathcal{C}_{T},\|\cdot-\cdot\|_{\infty})
.

Remark 2.1
.

It is important to keep in mind that the quantitative results concerning the Lévy–Prokhorov metric in this paper depend not only on the topology of
S
but also on the choice of the metric
d_{S}
itself.

2.3
Weak solutions of SFDEs

We are concerned with the SFDE (
1.1
) and its weak solution taking values on a subset of the Euclidean space. First, we provide a precise notion of the
data
of the SFDE (
1.1
).

Definition 2.2
.

By
data
of the SFDE (
1.1
), we mean a tuple
(D,\mu_{0},b,\sigma)
consisting of:

•

a non-empty open set
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, called a
domain
;

•

a probability measure
\mu_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
\mu_{0}(D)=1
, called an
initial distribution
;

•

progressively measurable maps
b:\Lambda[\mathrm{supp}\,\mu_{0},D]\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\sigma:\Lambda[\mathrm{supp}\,\mu_{0},D]\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, called
coefficients
.

We define the concepts of weak solutions and uniqueness in law for the SFDE (
1.1
) as follows.

Definition 2.3
.

We say that a tuple
(X,W,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
is a
weak solution
of the SFDE (
1.1
) associated with data
(D,\mu_{0},b,\sigma)
if the following hold:

•

(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
is a complete probability space, and
\mathbb{F}=(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is a filtration satisfying the usual conditions;

•

W
is an
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued Brownian motion on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
relative to
\mathbb{F}
;

•

X
is an
\mathbb{R}^{n}
-valued continuous
\mathbb{F}
-adapted process on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
such that
\mathbb{P}\circ X(0)^{-1}=\mu_{0}
,

\mathbb{P}\left(\text{$X(t)\in D$ for any $t\in[0,\infty)$}\right)=1,

and

\mathbb{P}\left(\text{$\int^{T}_{0}\big\{|b(t,X)|+|\sigma(t,X)|^{2}\big\}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty$ for any $T\in[0,\infty)$}\right)=1;

•

\mathbb{P}
-a.s., it holds that

X(t)=X(0)+\int^{t}_{0}b(s,X)\,\mathrm{d}s+\int^{t}_{0}\sigma(s,X)\,\mathrm{d}W(s)

for any
t\in[0,\infty)
.

We say that
uniqueness in law
holds for the SFDE (
1.1
) associated with data
(D,\mu_{0},b,\sigma)
if, for any two weak solutions
(X^{i},W^{i},\Omega^{i},\mathcal{F}^{i},\mathbb{F}^{i},\mathbb{P}^{i})
,
i=1,2
, of the SFDE (
1.1
) associated with data
(D,\mu_{0},b,\sigma)
, it holds that

\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(X^{1})=\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(X^{2}).

Here, for
i=1,2
,
\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{i}}(X^{i})
denotes the law of
X^{i}
on
\mathcal{C}
under the probability measure
\mathbb{P}^{i}
.

Remark 2.4
.

We emphasize that the above definition requires a weak solution
of the SFDE (
1.1
) associated with data
(D,\mu_{0},b,\sigma)
to satisfy

\mathbb{P}(\text{$X(t)\in D$ for any $t\in[0,\infty)$})=1
. The concept of the uniqueness in law is also considered in this manner. Hence, the case where
X
takes values in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus D
with positive probability is a priori excluded from the definition. Also, notice that uniqueness in law holds if there is no weak solution. In this paper, we focus on the weak approximation of a given weak solution as well as uniqueness in law of the SFDE (
1.1
) associated with data
(D,\mu_{0},b,\sigma)
satisfying mild growth, continuity and ellipticity conditions specified in
Assumption

1
below.

2.4
Euler–Maruyama scheme

For each
T\in(0,\infty)
, let
\Pi_{T}
be the collection of all partitions of
[0,T]
, that is,

\Pi_{T}:=\left\{\pi=(t_{k})^{m}_{k=0}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}m\in\mathbb{N},\ t_{0}=0,\ t_{k}<t_{k+1}\ \text{for any $k\in\{0,\dots,m-1\}$, and}\ t_{m}=T\right\}.

For each
\pi=(t_{k})^{m}_{k=0}\in\Pi_{T}
, denote
|\pi|:=\max_{k\in\{0,\dots,m-1\}}(t_{k+1}-t_{k})
. Furthermore, for each vector
x=(x(t_{k}))^{m}_{k=0}\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{m+1}
, define
\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[x]\in\mathcal{C}_{T}
as the linear interpolation of
(x(t_{k}))^{m}_{k=0}
with respect to
\pi
, that is,

\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[x](t):=\sum^{m-1}_{k=0}\left\{\frac{t_{k+1}-t}{t_{k+1}-t_{k}}x(t_{k})+\frac{t-t_{k}}{t_{k+1}-t_{k}}x(t_{k+1})\right\}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[t_{k},t_{k+1})}(t)+x(t_{m})\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[t_{m},\infty)}(t)

(2.6)

for
t\in[0,\infty)
. Clearly, the map
\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}:(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{m+1}\to\mathcal{C}_{T}
is continuous and linear. Notice that, for each
k\in\{0,\dots,m-1\}
, the stopped function
\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[x]_{t_{k}}\in\mathcal{C}_{t_{k}}
is given by

\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[x]_{t_{k}}(t)=\sum^{k-1}_{j=0}\left\{\frac{t_{j+1}-t}{t_{j+1}-t_{j}}x(t_{j})+\frac{t-t_{j}}{t_{j+1}-t_{j}}x(t_{j+1})\right\}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[t_{j},t_{j+1})}(t)+x(t_{k})\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[t_{k},\infty)}(t)

(2.7)

for
t\in[0,\infty)
, which depends only on
(x(t_{j}))^{k}_{j=0}
.
Note also that, for each Borel set
D_{1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
and open and convex set
D_{2}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
(t_{k},\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[x])\in\Lambda[D_{1},D_{2}]
if and only if
x(t_{0})\in D_{1}
and
x(t_{j})\in D_{2}
for all
j\in\{0,\dots,k\}
.

Let data
(D,\mu_{0},b,\sigma)
be given. We extend the domain
\Lambda[\mathrm{supp}\,\mu_{0},D]
of the coefficients
b
and
\sigma
to the whole space
\Lambda
by setting
b(t,x)=0
and
\sigma(t,x)=0
for each
(t,x)\in\Lambda\setminus\Lambda[\mathrm{supp}\,\mu_{0},D]
. Then,
b
and
\sigma
can be seen as progressively measurable maps from
\Lambda
to
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, respectively. For each
T\in(0,\infty)
and
\pi=(t_{k})^{m}_{k=0}\in\Pi_{T}
, let
(\Omega^{\pi},\mathcal{F}^{\pi},\mathbb{P}^{\pi})
be a complete probability space supporting independent random variables
\xi^{\pi}
and
Z^{\pi}_{k}
for
k\in\{0,\dots,m-1\}
, where
\xi^{\pi}
is an
\mathbb{R}^{n}
-valued random variable distributed according to the given initial distribution
\mu_{0}
, and each
Z^{\pi}_{k}
is a
d
-dimensional random variable distributed according to the Gaussian distribution
N(0,(t_{k+1}-t_{k})I_{d\times d})
with mean zero and covariance matrix
(t_{k+1}-t_{k})I_{d\times d}
. Without loss of generality, we may assume that
\xi^{\pi}(\omega)\in\mathrm{supp}\,\mu_{0}
for all
\omega\in\Omega^{\pi}
. Under this setting, define the Euler–Maruyama scheme
X^{\pi}=(X^{\pi}(t_{k}))^{m}_{k=0}
inductively as follows:

\begin{cases}X^{\pi}(t_{0})=\xi^{\pi},\\
X^{\pi}(t_{k+1})=X^{\pi}(t_{k})+b\big(t_{k},\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}]\big)(t_{k+1}-t_{k})+\sigma\big(t_{k},\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}]\big)Z^{\pi}_{k},\ k\in\{0,\dots,m-1\}.\end{cases}

(2.8)

Thanks to (
2.7
) and
\varphi(t_{k},\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}])=\varphi(t_{k},\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}]_{t_{k}})
for
\varphi\in\{b,\sigma\}
, the Euler–Maruyama scheme (
2.8
) is an explicit scheme, and we have
X^{\pi}=\Psi^{\pi}(\xi^{\pi},Z^{\pi}_{0},\dots,Z^{\pi}_{m-1})
for some measurable map
\Psi^{\pi}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{m}\to(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{m+1}
.

Remark 2.5
.

If, for any
\bar{x}\in D
,
\sigma(0,\bar{x}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[0,\infty)}(\cdot))\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
is non-degenerate in the sense that all eigenvalues of
\sigma(0,\bar{x}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[0,\infty)}(\cdot))\sigma(0,\bar{x}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[0,\infty)}(\cdot))^{\top}
are positive, then
\sigma(0,\bar{x}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[0,\infty)}(\cdot))Z^{\pi}_{0}
is a non-degenerate Gaussian random variable on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
for each
\bar{x}\in D
. Thus, when
D
is a proper subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, even if
X^{\pi}(t_{0})=\xi^{\pi}\in D
a.s., the random variable
X^{\pi}(t_{1})
defined by the first step of the Euler–Maruyama scheme (
2.8
) takes values in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus D
with positive probability. This is why we need to enlarge the domain
\Lambda[\mathrm{supp}\,\mu_{0},D]
of
b
and
\sigma
to the whole space
\Lambda
. Since
D
is assumed to be convex and
X^{\pi}(t_{0})=\xi^{\pi}\in\mathrm{supp}\,\mu_{0}
, we have
(t_{k},\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}])\in\Lambda[\mathrm{supp}\,\mu_{0},D]
if and only if
X^{\pi}(t_{j})\in D
for all
j\in\{0,\dots,k\}
. By virtue of the convention that
b(t,x)=0
and
\sigma(t,x)=0
for each
(t,x)\in\Lambda\setminus\Lambda[\mathrm{supp}\,\mu_{0},D]
, we have
X^{\pi}(t_{\ell})=X^{\pi}(t_{k})
for any
\ell\in\{k+1,\dots,m\}
on the event
\{X^{\pi}(t_{k})\notin D\}
for each
k\in\{0,\dots,m-1\}
.

The purpose of this paper is to give an error estimate for the weak approximation of SFDE (
1.1
) by the Euler–Maruyama scheme (
2.8
) under mild regularity conditions for the (path-dependent) coefficients
b
and
\sigma
. More precisely, for each
T\in(0,\infty)
and
\pi\in\Pi_{T}
, we estimate the Lévy–Prokhorov metric
d_{\mathrm{LP}}(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T}),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}]))
between the law of the path of the weak solution
(X,W,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
of the SFDE associated with data
(D,\mu_{0},b,\sigma)
(if any) and the law of the linear interpolation of the Euler–Maruyama scheme
X^{\pi}=(X^{\pi}(t_{k}))^{m}_{k=0}
having the same initial distribution as
X
.

3
Main result: Weak approximation of SFDEs

We consider the following assumption on the data
(D,\mu_{0},b,\sigma)
of the SFDE (
1.1
).

Assumption 1
.

The domain
D
is a non-empty, open and convex subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and the coefficients
b:\Lambda[\mathrm{supp}\,\mu_{0},D]\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\sigma:\Lambda[\mathrm{supp}\,\mu_{0},D]\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
satisfy the following conditions:

(G)

(The growth condition.)
For any
T\in(0,\infty)
, there exist constants
K_{\mathrm{G},b;T},K_{\mathrm{G},\sigma;T}\in[1,\infty)
and increasing families
\{D_{\mathrm{G},b;T}(R)\}_{R\in[1,\infty)}
and
\{D_{\mathrm{G},\sigma;T}(R)\}_{R\in[1,\infty)}
of open and convex subsets of
D
with
\bigcup_{R\in[1,\infty)}D_{\mathrm{G},b;T}(R)=D
and
\bigcup_{R\in[1,\infty)}D_{\mathrm{G},\sigma;T}(R)=D
such that

|\varphi(t,x)|\leq K_{\mathrm{G},\varphi;T}R

for any
(t,x)\in\Lambda\big[\mathrm{supp}\,\mu_{0},D_{\mathrm{G},\varphi;T}(R)\big]
with
t\leq T
,
R\in[1,\infty)
and
\varphi\in\{b,\sigma\}
.

(C)

(The local Hölder continuity condition.)
For any
T\in(0,\infty)
, there exist constants
K_{\mathrm{C},b;T},K_{\mathrm{C},\sigma;T}\in(0,\infty)
,
\alpha_{b}\in(0,1]
,
\alpha_{\sigma}\in(\frac{1}{2},1]
, and increasing families
\{D_{\mathrm{C},b;T}(R)\}_{R\in[1,\infty)}
and
\{D_{\mathrm{C},\sigma;T}(R)\}_{R\in[1,\infty)}
of open and convex subsets of
D
with
\bigcup_{R\in[1,\infty)}D_{\mathrm{C},b;T}(R)=D
and
\bigcup_{R\in[1,\infty)}D_{\mathrm{C},\sigma;T}(R)=D
such that

|\varphi(t,x)-\varphi(t,y)|\leq K_{\mathrm{C},\varphi;T}R\|x_{t}-y_{t}\|^{\alpha_{\varphi}}_{\infty}

(3.1)

and

|\varphi(t,x)-\varphi(s,x)|\leq K_{\mathrm{C},\varphi;T}R\Big\{\varpi(t,x;t-s)+(t-s)^{1/2}\Big\}^{\alpha_{\varphi}}

(3.2)

for any
(t,x),(t,y)\in\Lambda\big[\mathrm{supp}\,\mu_{0},D_{\mathrm{C},\varphi;T}(R)\big]
with
t\leq T
,
s\in[0,t]
,
R\in[1,\infty)
and
\varphi\in\{b,\sigma\}
.

(E)

(The local ellipticity condition.)
For any
T\in(0,\infty)
, there exists a constant
K_{\mathrm{E};T}\in[1,\infty)
and an increasing family
\{D_{\mathrm{E};T}(R)\}_{R\in[1,\infty)}
of open and convex subsets of
D
with
\bigcup_{R\in[1,\infty)}D_{\mathrm{E};T}(R)=D
such that

\langle\sigma(t,x)\sigma(t,x)^{\top}\xi,\xi\rangle\geq\frac{|\xi|^{2}}{K_{\mathrm{E};T}^{2}R^{2}}

for any
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
(t,x)\in\Lambda\big[\mathrm{supp}\,\mu_{0},D_{\mathrm{E};T}(R)\big]
with
t\leq T
and
R\in[1,\infty)
.

Under
Assumption

1
, for each
T\in(0,\infty)
and
\vec{R}=(R_{\mathrm{G},b},R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{C},b},R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{E}})\in[1,\infty)^{5}
, denote

D_{T}(\vec{R}):=D_{\mathrm{G},b;T}(R_{\mathrm{G},b})\cap D_{\mathrm{G},\sigma;T}(R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma})\cap D_{\mathrm{C},b;T}(R_{\mathrm{C},b})\cap D_{\mathrm{C},\sigma;T}(R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma})\cap D_{\mathrm{E};T}(R_{\mathrm{E}}).

Now we are ready to state our main result. The proof is postponed to
Section

5
.

Theorem 3.1
.

Fix data
(D,\mu_{0},b,\sigma)
satisfying
Assumption

1
. Suppose that we are given a weak solution
(X,W,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
of the SFDE (
1.1
) associated with
(D,\mu_{0},b,\sigma)
. For each
T\in(0,\infty)
and
\pi\in\Pi_{T}
, let
X^{\pi}
be the Euler–Maruyama scheme defined on
(\Omega^{\pi},\mathcal{F}^{\pi},\mathbb{P}^{\pi})
and given by (
2.8
) with initial distribution
\mu_{0}
. Then, the following hold:

(i)

For any
T\in(0,\infty)
,
\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}]\to X_{T}
weakly on
\mathcal{C}_{T}
as
|\pi|\downarrow 0
along
\pi\in\Pi_{T}
. In particular, uniqueness in law holds for the SFDE (
1.1
) associated with
(D,\mu_{0},b,\sigma)
.

(ii)

For any
T\in(0,\infty)
,
\pi\in\Pi_{T}
,
\Delta\in(0,1]
and
\vec{R}=(R_{\mathrm{G},b},R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{C},b},R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{E}})\in[1,\infty)^{5}
, it holds that

\begin{split}&d_{\mathrm{LP}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T}),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}])\big)\\
&\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{t\in[0,T]}\mathrm{dist}\!\left(X(t),\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus D_{T}(\vec{R})\right)\leq\Delta\right)\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt+2^{12}n\left(T+\frac{1}{T}\right)K_{\mathrm{E};T}R_{\mathrm{E}}\max\left\{\mathcal{E},K_{\mathrm{C},b;T}R_{\mathrm{C},b}\mathcal{E}^{\alpha_{b}},\frac{\left(K_{\mathrm{C},\sigma;T}R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}\mathcal{E}^{\alpha_{\sigma}}\right)^{2}}{\Delta}\log\frac{1}{\Delta}\right\},\end{split}

(3.3)

where
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}(|\pi|,\Delta,R_{\mathrm{G},b},R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma})\in(0,\infty)
is defined by

\mathcal{E}:=\max\left\{\Delta,K_{\mathrm{G},b;T}R_{\mathrm{G},b}|\pi|,K_{\mathrm{G},\sigma;T}R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}\sqrt{|\pi|\log\frac{T}{|\pi|}}\right\}.

(3.4)

(iii)

Let
T\in(0,\infty)
be fixed. Assume that there exist
\widehat{C}\in(0,\infty)
and
\vec{\beta}=(\beta_{0},\beta_{\mathrm{G},b},\beta_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},\beta_{\mathrm{C},b},\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},\beta_{\mathrm{E}})\in(0,\infty)^{6}
such that

\begin{split}\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{t\in[0,T]}\mathrm{dist}\!\left(X(t),\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus D_{T}(\vec{R})\right)\leq\Delta\right)&\leq\widehat{C}\max\left\{\Delta^{\beta_{0}},R_{\mathrm{G},b}^{-\beta_{\mathrm{G},b}},R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}^{-\beta_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}},R_{\mathrm{C},b}^{-\beta_{\mathrm{C},b}},R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}^{-\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}},R_{\mathrm{E}}^{-\beta_{\mathrm{E}}}\right\}\end{split}

(3.5)

for any
\Delta\in(0,1]
and
\vec{R}=(R_{\mathrm{G},b},R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{C},b},R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{E}})\in[1,\infty)^{5}
. Define

\begin{split}\gamma_{*}&:=\min\left\{\frac{\alpha_{b}}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+\beta_{\mathrm{C},b}^{-1}+\beta_{\mathrm{G},b}^{-1}\alpha_{b}},\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{\alpha_{b}}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+\beta_{\mathrm{C},b}^{-1}+\beta_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}^{-1}\alpha_{b}},\right.\\
&\hskip 42.67912pt\left.\frac{2\alpha_{\sigma}}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+2\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}^{-1}+2\beta_{\mathrm{G},b}^{-1}\alpha_{\sigma}+\beta_{*}^{-1}},\frac{\alpha_{\sigma}}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+2\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}^{-1}+2\beta_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}^{-1}\alpha_{\sigma}+\beta_{*}^{-1}}\right\},\end{split}

(3.6)

where

\beta_{*}:=\min\left\{\beta_{0},\frac{\alpha_{b}}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+\beta_{\mathrm{C},b}^{-1}},\frac{2\alpha_{\sigma}-1}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+2\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}^{-1}}\right\}.

(3.7)

Then, for any
\gamma\in(0,\gamma_{*})
, there exists a constant
\mathcal{K}_{T,\gamma}\in[1,\infty)
, which depends only on
T
,
K_{\mathrm{G},b;T}
,
K_{\mathrm{G},\sigma;T}
,
K_{\mathrm{C},b;T}
,
K_{\mathrm{C},\sigma;T}
,
K_{\mathrm{E};T}
,
\alpha_{b}
,
\alpha_{\sigma}
and
\gamma_{*}-\gamma
, such that

d_{\mathrm{LP}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T}),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}])\big)\leq\big(\widehat{C}+n\mathcal{K}_{T,\gamma}\big)|\pi|^{\gamma}

for any
\pi\in\Pi_{T}
.

Remark 3.2
.

The numbers
\beta_{*}=\beta_{*}(\alpha_{b},\alpha_{\sigma},\vec{\beta})
and
\gamma_{*}=\gamma_{*}(\alpha_{b},\alpha_{\sigma},\vec{\beta})
defined in (
3.7
) and (
3.6
), respectively, are positive and non-decreasing with respect to each variable of
\vec{\beta}=(\beta_{0},\beta_{\mathrm{G},b},\beta_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},\beta_{\mathrm{C},b},\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},\beta_{\mathrm{E}})\in(0,\infty)^{6}
. Moreover,

\sup_{\vec{\beta}\in(0,\infty)^{6}}\beta_{*}(\alpha_{b},\alpha_{\sigma},\vec{\beta})=\alpha_{b}\wedge\left(2\alpha_{\sigma}-1\right)\ \ \text{and}\ \ \sup_{\vec{\beta}\in(0,\infty)^{6}}\gamma_{*}(\alpha_{b},\alpha_{\sigma},\vec{\beta})=\frac{\alpha_{b}}{2}\wedge\left(\alpha_{\sigma}-\frac{1}{2}\right).

As an immediate consequence of the above result, we obtain the next corollary for the SFDE (
1.1
) defined on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
or
(0,\infty)^{n}
having “polynomial type” growth conditions. Here, in order to state the results in a unified manner, we introduce a progressively measurable map
\Psi:\Lambda[D]\to[0,\infty)
, which is defined depending on whether
D=\mathbb{R}^{n}
or
D=(0,\infty)^{n}
:

\Psi(t,x):=\begin{cases}\|x_{t}\|_{\infty}\ \ &\text{if $D=\mathbb{R}^{n}$},\\
\|x_{t}\|_{\infty}+\|x^{-1}_{t}\|_{\infty}\ \ &\text{if $D=(0,\infty)^{n}$},\end{cases}

for
(t,x)\in\Lambda[D]
, where
\xi^{-1}:=(\xi_{1}^{-1},\dots,\xi_{n}^{-1})^{\top}
for each
\xi=(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n})^{\top}\in(0,\infty)^{n}
, and
x_{t}^{-1}:=x(t\wedge\cdot)^{-1}
for each
(t,x)\in\Lambda[(0,\infty)^{n}]
.

Corollary 3.3
.

Consider data
(D,\mu_{0},b,\sigma)
with
D=\mathbb{R}^{n}
or
D=(0,\infty)^{n}
. Assume that there exist constants
\check{C}\in[1,\infty)
,
p\in[0,\infty)
,
\alpha_{b}\in(0,1]
and
\alpha_{\sigma}\in(\frac{1}{2},1]
such that the following hold:

(G’)

(The polynomial growth condition.)

|\varphi(t,x)|\leq\check{C}\big(1+\Psi(t,x)^{p}\big)

for any
(t,x)\in\Lambda[\mathrm{supp}\,\mu_{0},D]
and
\varphi\in\{b,\sigma\}
.

(C’)

(The local Hölder continuity condition with polynomial coefficients.)

|\varphi(t,x)-\varphi(t,y)|\leq\check{C}\big(1+\Psi(t,x)^{p}+\Psi(t,y)^{p}\big)\|x_{t}-y_{t}\|^{\alpha_{\varphi}}_{\infty}

and

|\varphi(t,x)-\varphi(s,x)|\leq\check{C}\big(1+\Psi(t,x)^{p}\big)\Big\{\varpi(t,x;t-s)+(t-s)^{1/2}\Big\}^{\alpha_{\varphi}}

for any
(t,x),(t,y)\in\Lambda[\mathrm{supp}\,\mu_{0},D]
,
s\in[0,t]
and
\varphi\in\{b,\sigma\}
.

(E’)

(The local ellipticity condition with polynomial coefficients.)

\langle\sigma(t,x)\sigma(t,x)^{\top}\xi,\xi\rangle\geq\frac{|\xi|^{2}}{\check{C}^{2}\big(1+\Psi(t,x)^{p}\big)^{2}}

for any
(t,x)\in\Lambda[\mathrm{supp}\,\mu_{0},D]
and
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Then, the data
(D,\mu_{0},b,\sigma)
satisfy
Assumption

1
. Suppose that we are given a weak solution
(X,W,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
to the SFDE (
1.1
) associated with
(D,\mu_{0},b,\sigma)
. Let
T\in(0,\infty)
be fixed. For each
\pi\in\Pi_{T}
, let
X^{\pi}
be the Euler–Maruyama scheme defined on
(\Omega^{\pi},\mathcal{F}^{\pi},\mathbb{P}^{\pi})
and given by (
2.8
) with initial distribution
\mu_{0}
.

(i)

Assume that

\mathbb{E}\big[\Psi(T,X)^{q}\big]<\infty\ \ \text{for any $q\in[1,\infty)$}.

(3.8)

Then, for any
\gamma\in(0,\frac{\alpha_{b}}{2}\wedge(\alpha_{\sigma}-\frac{1}{2}))
, there exists a constant
C_{\gamma}\in[1,\infty)
such that

d_{\mathrm{LP}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T}),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}])\big)\leq C_{\gamma}|\pi|^{\gamma}

for any
\pi\in\Pi_{T}
.

(ii)

Suppose that
D=\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
p=0
, that is, the progressively measurable maps
b:\Lambda[\mathrm{supp}\,\mu_{0},\mathbb{R}^{n}]\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\sigma:\Lambda[\mathrm{supp}\,\mu_{0},\mathbb{R}^{n}]\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
are bounded, globally Hölder continuous (with exponent
\alpha_{b}\in(0,1]
and
\alpha_{\sigma}\in(\frac{1}{2},1]
, respectively) and uniformly elliptic.

(ii-A)

If
\alpha_{b}<2\alpha_{\sigma}-1
or the diffusion coefficient
\sigma
is constant, then there exists a constant
C\in[1,\infty)
such that

d_{\mathrm{LP}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T}),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}])\big)\leq C|\pi|^{\alpha_{b}/2}\left(1+\log\frac{T}{|\pi|}\right)^{\alpha_{b}/2}

(3.9)

for any
\pi\in\Pi_{T}
.

(ii-B)

If
\alpha_{b}\geq 2\alpha_{\sigma}-1
and the diffusion coefficient
\sigma
is not constant, then there exists a constant
C\in[1,\infty)
such that

d_{\mathrm{LP}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T}),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}])\big)\leq C|\pi|^{\alpha_{\sigma}-1/2}\left(1+\log\frac{T}{|\pi|}\right)^{\alpha_{\sigma}+1/2}

(3.10)

for any
\pi\in\Pi_{T}
.

Proof.

Proof of (i).
We prove assertion (i) only for the case
D=(0,\infty)^{n}
. The case of
D=\mathbb{R}^{n}
can be proved by a similar (and simpler) manner. To show assertion (i) for
D=(0,\infty)^{n}
, without loss of generality, we may assume that
p>0
. By the assumptions in this corollary, we see that
Assumption

1
holds with Hölder exponents
\alpha_{b}
and
\alpha_{\sigma}
, the sets

\displaystyle D_{\mathrm{G},b;T}(R)=D_{\mathrm{G},\sigma;T}(R)=D_{\mathrm{C},b;T}(R)=D_{\mathrm{C},\sigma;T}(R)=D_{\mathrm{E};T}(R)=\left(R^{-1/p},2R^{1/p}\right)^{n},\ R\in[1,\infty),

and the constants

K_{\mathrm{G},b;T}=K_{\mathrm{G},\sigma;T}=K_{\mathrm{C},b;T}=K_{\mathrm{C},\sigma;T}=K_{\mathrm{E};T}=\check{C}\big(1+2\cdot 3^{p}n^{p/2}\big),

for each
T\in(0,\infty)
. Suppose that we are given a weak solution
(X,W,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
to the SFDE (
1.1
) associated with
(D,\mu_{0},b,\sigma)
with
D=(0,\infty)^{n}
. Let
T\in(0,\infty)
be fixed. We apply assertion (iii) in
Theorem

3.1
to the current setting. To do so, we check the condition (
3.5
). Observe that, for any
\Delta\in(0,1]
and
\vec{R}=(R_{\mathrm{G},b},R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{C},b},R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{E}})\in[1,\infty)^{5}
, using the notation
R_{\min}=\min\{R_{\mathrm{G},b},R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{C},b},R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{E}}\}
and noting that
\Delta\leq 1\leq R_{\min}^{1/p}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{t\in[0,T]}\mathrm{dist}\!\left(X(t),\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus D_{T}(\vec{R})\right)\leq\Delta\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(X(t)\notin\left(R_{\min}^{-1/p}+\Delta,2R_{\min}^{1/p}-\Delta\right)^{n}\ \ \text{for some $t\in[0,T]$}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\|X_{T}\|_{\infty}\geq 2R_{\min}^{1/p}-\Delta\right)+\mathbb{P}\left(\|X^{-1}_{T}\|_{\infty}\geq\left(R_{\min}^{-1/p}+\Delta\right)^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\|X_{T}\|_{\infty}\geq R_{\min}^{1/p}\right)+\mathbb{P}\left(\|X^{-1}_{T}\|_{\infty}\geq\left(R_{\min}^{-1/p}+\Delta\right)^{-1}\right).

By Markov’s inequality and the assumption (
3.8
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{t\in[0,T]}\mathrm{dist}\!\left(X(t),\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus D_{T}(\vec{R})\right)\leq\Delta\right)

\displaystyle\leq R_{\min}^{-q/p}\mathbb{E}\big[\|X_{T}\|_{\infty}^{q}\big]+\left(R_{\min}^{-1/p}+\Delta\right)^{q}\mathbb{E}\big[\|X^{-1}_{T}\|_{\infty}^{q}\big]

\displaystyle\leq C_{q}\max\left\{\Delta^{q},R_{\mathrm{G},b}^{-q/p},R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}^{-q/p},R_{\mathrm{C},b}^{-q/p},R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}^{-q/p},R_{\mathrm{E}}^{-q/p}\right\}

for any
q\in[1,\infty)
, where
C_{q}\in(0,\infty)
is a constant which does not depend on
\Delta
or
\vec{R}
. The above estimate shows that (
3.5
) holds for any
\vec{\beta}=(\beta_{0},\beta_{\mathrm{G},b},\beta_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},\beta_{\mathrm{C},b},\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},\beta_{\mathrm{E}})\in(0,\infty)^{6}
. Therefore, noting
Remark

3.2
, by
Theorem

3.1
(iii), we see that assertion (i) of
Corollary

3.3
holds.

Proof of (ii).
Let
D=\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
p=0
. We apply assertion (ii) of
Theorem

3.1
to this setting. Notice that
Assumption

1
holds with exponents
\alpha_{b}
and
\alpha_{\sigma}
, the sets

D_{\mathrm{G},b;T}(R)=D_{\mathrm{G},\sigma;T}(R)=D_{\mathrm{C},b;T}(R)=D_{\mathrm{C},\sigma;T}(R)=D_{\mathrm{E};T}(R)=\mathbb{R}^{n},\ \ R\in[1,\infty),

and the constants

K_{\mathrm{G},b;T}=K_{\mathrm{G},\sigma;T}=K_{\mathrm{C},b;T}=K_{\mathrm{C},\sigma;T}=K_{\mathrm{E};T}=3\check{C},

for each
T\in(0,\infty)
. Moreover, if the diffusion coefficient
\sigma
is constant, then we can take the constant
K_{\mathrm{C},\sigma;T}
as an arbitrarily small positive number. Let
T\in(0,\infty)
and
\pi\in\Pi_{T}
be fixed. We apply the estimate (
3.3
) with

\Delta=\sqrt{\frac{|\pi|}{T}\left(1+\log\frac{T}{|\pi|}\right)}\ \ \text{and}\ \ R_{\mathrm{G},b}=R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}=R_{\mathrm{C},b}=R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}=R_{\mathrm{E}}=1.

Observe that
D_{T}(\vec{R})=\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and hence the probability appearing in the right-hand side of (
3.3
) is zero. Furthermore,

\mathcal{E}\leq C_{T}|\pi|^{1/2}\left(1+\log\frac{T}{|\pi|}\right)^{1/2},\ \ \log\frac{1}{\Delta}\leq 1+\log\frac{T}{|\pi|},

and

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{LP}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T}),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}])\big)

\displaystyle\leq nC_{T}\max\left\{|\pi|^{\alpha_{b}/2}\left(1+\log\frac{T}{|\pi|}\right)^{\alpha_{b}/2},K_{\mathrm{C},\sigma;T}^{2}|\pi|^{\alpha_{\sigma}-1/2}\left(1+\log\frac{T}{|\pi|}\right)^{\alpha_{\sigma}+1/2}\right\},

where
C_{T}\in[1,\infty)
is a constant which depends only on
\check{C}
and
T
and varies from line to line. As we mentioned above,
K_{\mathrm{C},\sigma;T}
can be taken as an arbitrary positive number if
\sigma
is constant, and
K_{\mathrm{C},\sigma;T}=3\check{C}
otherwise. In the former case, taking the limit
K_{\mathrm{C},\sigma;T}\downarrow 0
in the above estimate, we get (
3.9
). In the latter case, noting that
1+\log x\leq\frac{1}{\nu}x^{\nu}
for any
x\in[1,\infty)
and
\nu\in(0,1]
, we get (
3.9
) if
\alpha_{b}<2\alpha_{\sigma}-1
and (
3.10
) if
\alpha_{b}\geq 2\alpha_{\sigma}-1
. This completes the proof.
∎

4
Remarks on the main result and special classes of SFDEs

Let us make some remarks on the main results.

4.1
On the generality of the setting

Theorem

3.1
shows uniqueness in law and functional type weak approximation results for SFDEs in a highly general setting, providing a quantitative weak error estimate in terms of the Lévy–Prokhorov metric. Our results allow the coefficients to be path-dependent. Interestingly, thanks to the path-dependence, we can apply our results not only to “purely non-Markovian” systems such as SDEs with delay but also to some non-standard (Markovian) SDEs such as reflected SDEs and stochastic oscillator models after appropriate transformations. We discuss these special classes of SFDEs in
Section

4.4
in detail. Furthermore, it is worth mentioning that
Assumption

1
does not impose either boundedness (or linear growth), global Lipschitz (or global Hölder) continuity or uniform ellipticity condition on the coefficients, which are often assumed in the existing works on weak approximation of SDEs and SFDEs. Instead,
Assumption

1
incorporates the “localities” of the boundedness, continuity and ellipticity conditions which are characterized by the families of subsets
D_{\mathrm{G},b;T}(R)
,
D_{\mathrm{G},\sigma;T}(R)
,
D_{\mathrm{C},b;T}(R)
,
D_{\mathrm{C},\sigma;T}(R)
and
D_{\mathrm{E};T}(R)
of the domain
D
. Thanks to these kinds of localities (and the path-dependences), we can apply our results to stochastic systems arising in a wide range of scientific fields such as physics, chemistry, economics, mathematical finance and so on, which are beyond the framework of existing works on Euler–Maruyama approximations; see
Section

6
where we study ten concrete examples arising in science and provide a precise weak error estimate for each model.

To the best of our knowledge, even the uniqueness in law result itself shown in
Theorem

3.1
(i) is new in this generality. A comparable result can be found in the paper
[
48
]
by Kulik and Scheutzow, where they show (besides other important results on ergodicity) that weak existence and uniqueness in law hold for SFDEs defined on
D=\mathbb{R}^{n}
under the assumptions that the drift coefficient
b
satisfies the one-sided (finite-range)
\alpha_{b}
-Hölder (
\alpha_{b}\in(0,1]
) continuity condition and the one-sided linear-growth condition and that the diffusion coefficient
\sigma
satisfies the (finite-range)
\alpha_{\sigma}
-Hölder (
\alpha_{\sigma}\in(\frac{1}{2},1]
) continuity condition, the linear growth condition and the uniform ellipticity condition. Compared with the result in
[
48
]
, our result on uniqueness in law for SFDEs includes the case where the domain
D
is a proper subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and allows for locally bounded, locally Hölder continuous and locally uniformly elliptic coefficients. Also, this paper is the first to provide functional type weak approximations for SFDEs in this generality.

4.2
On the statements of
Theorem

3.1
and
Corollary

3.3

Notice that the weak error estimate in
Theorem

3.1
(ii) has flexibility for the parameters
\Delta\in(0,1]
and
\vec{R}=(R_{\mathrm{G},b},R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{C},b},R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{E}})\in[1,\infty)^{5}
which are regarded as benchmark parameters and related to the locality of the assumption discussed above. The right-hand side of (
3.3
) consists of the following two error terms:

\mathrm{Error}_{1}=\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{t\in[0,T]}\mathrm{dist}\!\left(X(t),\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus D_{T}(\vec{R})\right)\leq\Delta\right)

and

\mathrm{Error}_{2}=(\mathrm{constant})\times K_{\mathrm{E};T}R_{\mathrm{E}}\max\left\{\mathcal{E},K_{\mathrm{C},b;T}R_{\mathrm{C},b}\mathcal{E}^{\alpha_{b}},\frac{\left(K_{\mathrm{C},\sigma;T}R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}\mathcal{E}^{\alpha_{\sigma}}\right)^{2}}{\Delta}\log\frac{1}{\Delta}\right\}

with
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}(|\pi|,\Delta,R_{\mathrm{G},b},R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma})
defined by (
3.4
). The first term
\mathrm{Error}_{1}
is the probability term, which depends on the (unique) law of the given weak solution but does not depend on the partition
\pi
. This term can be seen as a probability of a “rare event” where the given weak solution exits a large sub-domain of
D
until the time
T
. The second term
\mathrm{Error}_{2}
is the discretization error term, which depends on the partition
\pi
but does not depend on the law of the weak solution. The two terms
\mathrm{Error}_{1}
and
\mathrm{Error}_{2}
are related to each other via the free parameters
\Delta
and
\vec{R}
. It can be easily shown (see
Section

5.4
) that, on the one hand, the probability term (
\mathrm{Error}_{1}
) tends to zero as
\Delta\downarrow 0
and
R_{\mathrm{G},b},R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{C},b},R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{E}}\uparrow\infty
. On the other hand, thanks to the assumption
\alpha_{\sigma}>\frac{1}{2}
, the discretization error term (
\mathrm{Error}_{2}
) tends to zero as
|\pi|\downarrow 0
and
\Delta\downarrow 0
(in this order) for each fixed
\vec{R}
, but diverges as
R_{\mathrm{G},b},R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{C},b},R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{E}}\uparrow\infty
for each fixed
\pi
and
\Delta
. Therefore, in order to get a convergence speed (with respect to
|\pi|
) of
\lim_{|\pi|\downarrow 0}d_{\mathrm{LP}}(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T}),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}]))=0
as fast as possible, it is important to choose appropriate free parameters
\Delta
and
\vec{R}
according to
|\pi|
. This is possible if we a priori know the convergence rate of the probability of the rare event, that is the first term
\mathrm{Error}_{1}
, with respect to
\Delta
and
\vec{R}
. Indeed, assertion (iii) of
Theorem

3.1
, which follows from assertion (ii), provides a convergence rate (in the form of the power of
|\pi|
) assuming the a priori estimate (
3.5
) for the probability of the rare event. This result shows that, in order to get a more precise weak convergence rate for each model, it suffices to investigate the a priori estimate of the form (
3.5
) and determine the parameters
\vec{\beta}=(\beta_{0},\beta_{\mathrm{G},b},\beta_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},\beta_{\mathrm{C},b},\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},\beta_{\mathrm{E}})\in(0,\infty)^{6}
. After that, computing the numbers
\beta_{*}=\beta_{*}(\alpha_{b},\alpha_{\sigma},\vec{\beta})
and
\gamma_{*}=\gamma_{*}(\alpha_{b},\alpha_{\sigma},\vec{\beta})
by the formulas (
3.7
) and (
3.6
), we will obtain the rate of (almost)
\gamma_{*}
for the convergence order. We again stress that the probability of the rare event (the term
\mathrm{Error}_{1}
) depends only on the (unique) law of the given weak solution and hence determined by the structure of the system. Actually, as we demonstrate in
Section

6
, this term can be easily estimated in many examples.

Corollary

3.3
(i) immediately follows from
Theorem

3.1
(iii) and provides us with a useful sufficient condition (
3.8
) to obtain the rate of (almost)
\frac{\alpha_{b}}{2}\wedge(\alpha_{\sigma}-\frac{1}{2})
for the convergence order for SFDEs defined on
D=\mathbb{R}^{n}
or
D=(0,\infty)^{n}
under the “polynomial type” conditions (G’), (C’) and (E’). One of the simplest sufficient conditions ensuring the moment condition (
3.8
) is the one-sided linear growth condition for the coefficients; see
Appendix

B
. As we mentioned in
Remark

3.2
, the number
\frac{\alpha_{b}}{2}\wedge(\alpha_{\sigma}-\frac{1}{2})
is the supremum of
\gamma_{*}
over all
\vec{\beta}
.

Furthermore, under the assumptions that
D=\mathbb{R}^{n}
and that the coefficients are bounded, globally Hölder continuous and uniformly elliptic,
Corollary

3.3
(ii) shows a more accurate convergence order than the one obtained in
Corollary

3.3
(i), incorporating the logarithmic terms. In particular, in the case of
Corollary

3.3
(ii-A) with
\alpha_{b}=1
and the diffusion coefficient
\sigma
being constant, we reach the order
|\pi|^{1/2}(1+\log\frac{T}{|\pi|})^{1/2}
, which turns out to be sharp as we discuss below.

4.3
On the sharpness of the results

The significance of our result lies not only in the generality of the assumption as discussed above but also in the sharpness of the weak error estimate. More precisely, our results are sharp in both (I) topological and (II) quantitative senses, that is:

(I)

The weak convergence results and the estimates in terms of the Lévy–Prokhorov metric in
Theorem

3.1
and
Corollary

3.3
(i) cannot be improved to the topology of the convergence in terms of the Wasserstein metric in general.

(II)

The weak convergence order with respect to the Lévy–Prokhorov metric obtained in
Corollary

3.3
(ii-A) is optimal for the case where
\alpha_{b}=1
and the diffusion coefficient
\sigma
is constant.

In order to explain statement (I) (the sharpness in the topological sense), let us first recall the definition of the (
L^{1}
-)Wasserstein metric
\mathcal{W}_{1}
associated with the base space
(\mathcal{C}_{T},\|\cdot\|_{\infty})
. It is a metric on the space

\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{C}_{T}):=\left\{\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{C}_{T})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\int_{\mathcal{C}_{T}}\|x\|_{\infty}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}x)<\infty\right\}

defined by

\mathcal{W}_{1}(\mu,\nu):=\inf_{\mathfrak{m}\in\mathscr{C}(\mu,\nu)}\int_{\mathcal{C}_{T}\times\mathcal{C}_{T}}\|x-y\|_{\infty}\,\mathfrak{m}(\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y),\ \ \mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{C}_{T}).

The Kantorovich–Rubinstein theorem (cf.
[
25
, Theorem 11.8.2]
) states that
\mathcal{W}_{1}
coincides with the metric
d_{\Xi}
introduced in
Section

2.2
associated with the class
\Xi=\mathrm{Lip}
of Lipschitz continuous test functions, that is,

\mathcal{W}_{1}(\mu,\nu)=d_{\mathrm{Lip}}(\mu,\nu):=\sup_{f\in\mathrm{Lip}}\left|\int_{\mathcal{C}_{T}}f(x)\,\mu(\mathrm{d}x)-\int_{\mathcal{C}_{T}}f(x)\,\nu(\mathrm{d}x)\right|\ \ \text{for any $\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{C}_{T})$},

where

\mathrm{Lip}:=\left\{f:\mathcal{C}_{T}\to\mathbb{R}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y\in\mathcal{C}_{T}\\
x\neq y\end{subarray}}\frac{|f(x)-f(y)|}{\|x-y\|_{\infty}}\leq 1\right\}.

It is known that, for
\mu,\mu_{m}\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{C}_{T})
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
\lim_{m\to\infty}\mathcal{W}_{1}(\mu,\mu_{m})=0
if and only if
(\mu_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
weakly converges to
\mu
and is uniformly integrable (cf.
[
16
, Theorem 5.5]
). The latter is also equivalent to

\lim_{m\to\infty}d_{\mathrm{LP}}(\mu,\mu_{m})=0\ \ \text{and}\ \ \lim_{r\to\infty}\sup_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{\mathcal{C}_{T}}\|x\|_{\infty}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{\{\|x\|_{\infty}>r\}}\,\mu_{m}(\mathrm{d}x)=0.

It is shown in
[
38
,
40
]
that, for any non-degenerate, one-dimensional and Markovian SDEs defined on
D=\mathbb{R}
, if either the drift coefficient
b
or the diffusion coefficient
\sigma
is super-linearly growing, then any moments of the corresponding (standard) Euler–Maruyama scheme with respect to the uniform partition
\pi_{T,m}=(\frac{k}{m}T)^{m}_{k=0}\in\Pi_{T}
(
m\in\mathbb{N}
) must diverge as
m\to\infty
in finite time, that is,

\lim_{m\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi_{T,m}}}\big[|X^{\pi_{T,m}}(T)|^{p}\big]=\infty\ \ \text{for any $p\in(0,\infty)$ and $T\in(0,\infty)$}.

(4.1)

In particular, for such a Markovian SDE, (if the weak solution
(X,W,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
satisfies
\mathbb{E}[\|X\|_{\infty}]<\infty
) we have

\lim_{m\to\infty}\mathcal{W}_{1}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T}),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi_{T,m}}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi_{T,m}}[X^{\pi_{T,m}}])\big)=\infty\ \ \text{for any $T\in(0,\infty)$}.

(4.2)

Our results on weak convergence and error estimates in terms of the Lévy–Prokhorov metric are valid even for (multi-dimensional and path-dependent) SFDEs having super-linearly growing coefficients
b
and
\sigma
, but cannot be improved to the Wasserstein metric in general in the above sense. The following provides a concrete example showing the sharpness in the topological sense.

Example 4.1
.

Consider the following one-dimensional Markovian SDE:

\mathrm{d}X(t)=\big\{X(t)-X(t)^{3}\big\}\,\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}W(t),\ \ t\in[0,\infty),\ \ X(0)=\xi\in\mathbb{R}.

This SDE can be seen as SFDE (
1.1
) with data
(\mathbb{R},\delta_{\xi},x(t)-x(t)^{3},1)
. Clearly, the conditions (G’), (C’) (with
\alpha_{b}=\alpha_{\sigma}=1
) and (E’) in
Corollary

3.3
hold. Furthermore, by
Lemma

B.1
(i), we see that
\mathbb{E}[\|X_{T}\|_{\infty}^{p}]<\infty
for any
p\in[2,\infty)
and
T\in(0,\infty)
, and hence the moment condition (
3.8
) holds as well. Hence, by
Corollary

3.3
(i), for any
T\in(0,\infty)
and
\gamma\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
, there exists a constant
C_{\gamma}\in(0,\infty)
such that

d_{\mathrm{LP}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T}),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}])\big)\leq C_{\gamma}|\pi|^{\gamma}

for any
\pi\in\Pi_{T}
, where
X^{\pi}
is the corresponding (standard) Euler–Maruyama scheme with initial condition
X^{\pi}(t_{0})=\xi
. However, by
[
40
, Theorem 2.1]
, we have the divergences (
4.1
) and (
4.2
) of moments of the Euler–Maruyama scheme and Wasserstein error in finite time.

Concerning statement (II) (the sharpness in the quantitative sense), notice that
Corollary

3.3
(ii-A) in particular implies that, in the case of
D=\mathbb{R}^{n}
, bounded Lipschitz (but still path-dependent) drift coefficient
b
and constant diffusion coefficient
\sigma
, the Lévy–Prokhorov metric
d_{\mathrm{LP}}(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T}),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi_{T,m}}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi_{T,m}}[X^{\pi_{T,m}}])\big)
converges to zero as
m\to\infty
for any
T\in(0,\infty)
along the uniform partition
\pi_{T,m}=(\frac{k}{m}T)^{m}_{k=0}\in\Pi_{T}
(
m\in\mathbb{N}
) with order of at least
\sqrt{\frac{1}{m}\log m}
. This result then implies the same convergence order with respect to the metric
d_{\mathrm{BL}}
induced by the class
\mathrm{BL}
of bounded and Lipschitz continuous (with respect to
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
) functions from
\mathcal{C}_{T}
to
\mathbb{R}
; see
Section

2.2
above. The following proposition shows that these convergence orders with respect to
d_{\mathrm{LP}}
and
d_{\mathrm{BL}}
are optimal in the case of the standard Brownian motion
X=W
(which corresponds to SFDE (
1.1
) with data
(D,\mu_{0},b,\sigma)=(\mathbb{R}^{n},\delta_{0},0,I_{n\times n})
). In the following proposition, we denote by
\mathbb{W}_{T}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{C}_{T})
the Wiener measure on
\mathcal{C}_{T}
and by
\pi_{T,m}:=(\frac{k}{m}T)^{m}_{k=0}\in\Pi_{T}
the uniform partition of
[0,T]
.

Proposition 4.2
.

For any
T\in(0,\infty)
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
with
m\geq 3\vee(T\log m)
, it holds that
1
1

1

The numbers
\pi
appearing in the right-hand sides of (
4.3
) and (
4.4
) represent the circle ratio, not the partition of
[0,T]
. Be careful not to confuse them. These are clear from the context, and the circle ratio does not appear explicitly anywhere else in this article.

\inf_{\mu\in\mathcal{P}((\mathbb{R}^{n})^{m+1})}d_{\mathrm{BL}}\big(\mathbb{W}_{T},\mu\circ(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi_{T,m}})^{-1}\big)\geq\frac{1}{5}\left(1-\exp\left(-\frac{n}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\right)\right)\sqrt{\frac{T}{m}\log m},

(4.3)

and in particular,

\inf_{\mu\in\mathcal{P}((\mathbb{R}^{n})^{m+1})}d_{\mathrm{LP}}\big(\mathbb{W}_{T},\mu\circ(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi_{T,m}})^{-1}\big)\geq\frac{1}{10}\left(1-\exp\left(-\frac{n}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\right)\right)\sqrt{\frac{T}{m}\log m}.

(4.4)

Proof.

Estimate (
4.4
) follows from (
4.3
) and the second inequality in (
2.5
). We show estimate (
4.3
). Fix
T\in(0,\infty)
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
with
m\geq 3\vee(T\log m)
. Define
f_{T,m}:\mathcal{C}_{T}\to\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle f_{T,m}(x)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{5}\min\left\{\sqrt{\frac{T}{m}\log m},\ \max_{k\in\{0,\dots,m-1\}}\left|x\left(\frac{k}{m}T\right)+x\left(\frac{k+1}{m}T\right)-2x\left(\frac{k+\frac{1}{2}}{m}T\right)\right|\right\},\ x\in\mathcal{C}_{T}.

Observe that, for any
x,y\in\mathcal{C}_{T}
,

|f_{T,m}(x)-f_{T,m}(y)|\leq\frac{4}{5}\|x-y\|_{\infty}\ \ \text{and}\ \ 0\leq f_{T,m}(x)\leq\frac{1}{5}\sqrt{\frac{T}{m}\log m}\leq\frac{1}{5}.

In particular, the function
f_{T,m}
is in the class
\mathrm{BL}
defined by (
2.4
) with
(S,d_{S})=(\mathcal{C}_{T},\|\cdot-\cdot\|_{\infty})
. Moreover, notice that

f_{T,m}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi_{T,m}}[z])=0\ \ \text{for any $z=(z(t_{k}))^{m}_{k=0}\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{m+1}$},

and

f_{T,m}(x)\geq\frac{1}{5}\sqrt{\frac{T}{m}\log m}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{A_{T,m}}(x)\ \ \text{for any $x\in\mathcal{C}_{T}$},

where
A_{T,m}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{C}_{T})
is defined by

\displaystyle A_{T,m}

\displaystyle:=\left\{x\in\mathcal{C}_{T}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\max_{k\in\{0,\dots,m-1\}}\left|x\left(\frac{k}{m}T\right)+x\left(\frac{k+1}{m}T\right)-2x\left(\frac{k+\frac{1}{2}}{m}T\right)\right|>\sqrt{\frac{T}{m}\log m}\right\}.

Therefore, for any
\mu\in\mathcal{P}((\mathbb{R}^{n})^{m+1})
, we have

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{BL}}\big(\mathbb{W}_{T},\mu\circ(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi_{T,m}})^{-1}\big)

\displaystyle\geq\left|\int_{\mathcal{C}_{T}}f_{T,m}(x)\,\mathbb{W}_{T}(\mathrm{d}x)-\int_{\mathcal{C}_{T}}f_{T,m}(x)\,\big(\mu\circ(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi_{T,m}})^{-1}\big)(\mathrm{d}x)\right|=\int_{\mathcal{C}_{T}}f_{T,m}(x)\,\mathbb{W}_{T}(\mathrm{d}x)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{5}\sqrt{\frac{T}{m}\log m}\mathbb{W}_{T}(A_{T,m})=\frac{1}{5}\sqrt{\frac{T}{m}\log m}\left(1-\mathbb{W}_{T}\left(A_{T,m}^{\complement}\right)\right).

Hence, in order to prove (
4.3
), it suffices to show that

\mathbb{W}_{T}\left(A_{T,m}^{\complement}\right)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{n}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\right).

(4.5)

Observe that, under the Wiener measure
\mathbb{W}_{T}
, the random variables

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{T}\ni x\mapsto

\displaystyle\,x\left(\frac{k}{m}T\right)+x\left(\frac{k+1}{m}T\right)-2x\left(\frac{k+\frac{1}{2}}{m}T\right)

\displaystyle=x\left(\frac{k+1}{m}T\right)-x\left(\frac{k+\frac{1}{2}}{m}T\right)-\left(x\left(\frac{k+\frac{1}{2}}{m}T\right)-x\left(\frac{k}{m}T\right)\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\ k\in\{0,\dots,m-1\},

are independent and identically distributed according to the
n
-dimensional normal distribution
N(0,\frac{T}{m}I_{n\times n})
with mean zero and covariance matrix
\frac{T}{m}I_{n\times n}
. Hence,

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}_{T}\left(A_{T,m}^{\complement}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{W}_{T}\left(\bigcap^{m-1}_{k=0}\left\{x\in\mathcal{C}_{T}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\left|x\left(\frac{k}{m}T\right)+x\left(\frac{k+1}{m}T\right)-2x\left(\frac{k+\frac{1}{2}}{m}T\right)\right|\leq\sqrt{\frac{T}{m}\log m}\right\}\right)

\displaystyle=N\left(0,\frac{T}{m}I_{n\times n}\right)\left(\left\{z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}|z|\leq\sqrt{\frac{T}{m}\log m}\right\}\right)^{m}

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-\delta_{m}\right)^{nm},

where

\delta_{m}:=\frac{2}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int^{\infty}_{\sqrt{\log m}}\exp\left(-\frac{\theta^{2}}{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\theta.

Noting that
1-\delta\leq\exp(-\delta)
for any
\delta\in[0,1]
and that

\int^{\infty}_{\xi}\exp\left(-\frac{\theta^{2}}{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\theta\geq\left(\xi+\frac{1}{\xi}\right)^{-1}\exp\left(-\frac{\xi^{2}}{2}\right)\geq\frac{1}{2}\exp\left(-\xi^{2}\right)

for any
\xi\geq 1
, together with
\sqrt{\log m}\geq\sqrt{\log 3}\geq 1
, we see that

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}_{T}\left(A_{T,m}^{\complement}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-nm\delta_{m}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-nm\cdot\frac{2}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\cdot\frac{1}{2}\exp\left(-\big(\sqrt{\log m}\big)^{2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-\frac{n}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\right).

Hence, we obtain the desired estimate (
4.5
). This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 4.3
.

•

Proposition

4.2
provides a lower bound for the error between the law of the
n
-dimensional standard Brownian motion
W
on
[0,T]
(that is, the Wiener measure
\mathbb{W}_{T}
) and the law of the linear interpolation of a random vector in
(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{m+1}
with respect to the partition
\pi_{T,m}=(\frac{k}{m}T)^{m}_{k=0}
of
[0,T]
with the
m
-equal length
|\pi_{T,m}|=\frac{T}{m}
, and the estimate holds uniformly in the laws of the underlying random vectors. In other words, this provides a lower bound, which does not depend on the choice of the underlying random vectors, for the weak convergence order in the Donsker-type functional central limit theorem; the weak convergence order as
m\to\infty
cannot be faster than
\sqrt{\frac{1}{m}\log m}
in terms of either the metric
d_{\mathrm{LP}}
or
d_{\mathrm{BL}}
.

•

A similar lower bound as in
Proposition

4.2
can be found in
[
21
, Theorem 1.16]
, which states that, for “any” sequence
(Y_{k})^{\infty}_{k=1}
of independent and identically distributed random variables satisfying
\mathbb{E}[Y_{1}]=0
,
\mathbb{E}[Y_{1}^{2}]=1
, and
\mathbb{E}[\exp(a|Y_{1}|)]<\infty
for some positive constant
a
, the sharp order of the weak convergence of the laws of the processes
S^{m}(t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\sum_{k=1}^{\lfloor mt\rfloor}Y_{k}
,
t\in[0,1]
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
, to the Wiener measure with respect to the Lévy–Prokhorov metric on the space of
\mathbb{R}
-valued right-continuous functions on
[0,1]
is
\sqrt{\frac{1}{m}}\log m
, which is slightly slower than
\sqrt{\frac{1}{m}\log m}
. We would like to stress that our results do not contradict to (a more precise statement of)
[
21
, Theorem 1.16]
by the following two reasons. First, the frameworks in the present paper and in
[
21
, Theorem 1.16]
are different; the present paper considers the quantitative weak approximation by the polylinear extension of the discrete time random vectors in the space of continuous functions equipped with the supremum norm
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
, while
[
21
, Theorem 1.16]
considers quantitative weak approximation by the discontinuous extension of the random walk in the space of right continuous functions equipped with the Skorokhod metric. Second, more importantly, the proof of
[
21
, Theorem 1.16]
(or more precisely
[
21
, Equation (1.1.37)]
) does not provide a meaningful lower bound (in the sense that the constant
C_{15}
in
[
21
, Equation (1.1.32)]
should be zero) when the underlying random variables
Y_{k}
are distributed according to the standard normal distribution, which is nothing but the case corresponding to our framework; recall that each random variable
Z^{\pi}_{k}
,
k\in\{0,\dots,m-1\}
, appearing in the definition (
2.8
) of the Euler–Maruyama scheme is assumed to be distributed according to
N(0,(t_{k+1}-t_{k})I_{d\times d})
.

Let us make a remark that statement (I) (the sharpness in the topological sense) above does not mean that the Wasserstein metric always diverges under our general assumption. Also, statement (II) (the sharpness in the quantitative sense) above does not mean that the polynomial convergence rate in
Theorem

3.1
(iii) or the convergence order in
Corollary

3.3
(ii-B) is sharp. From the observations in this section, the following questions naturally arise:

•

Is it possible to weaken the assumptions in
[
38
,
40
]
showing the divergence of the Wasserstein metric?

•

Are the convergence orders shown in
Theorem

3.1
and
Corollary

3.3
optimal in the general setting (including the Hölder coefficients case)?

•

Is it possible to show similar results as in
Theorem

3.1
and
Corollary

3.3
for the case of Euler–Maruyama scheme (
2.8
) with
Z^{\pi}=(Z^{\pi}_{k})^{m-1}_{k=0}
replaced by more general (not necessarily Gaussian) random vectors?

We do not have positive answers to these questions at this moment, and we leave them to the future research.

4.4
Special classes of SFDEs

As we mentioned above, the path-dependency of the coefficients makes it possible to apply our results to many kinds of Markovian and non-Markovian systems. Here, we demonstrate that our framework can be applied to the following four classes of stochastic models (after appropriate transformations for the last two):

•

Section

4.4.1
:
Markovian stochastic differential equations

•

Section

4.4.2
:
Stochastic delay differential equations

•

Section

4.4.3
:
Reflected stochastic differential equations

•

Section

4.4.4
:
Stochastic oscillator models / Stochastic integro-differential equations

4.4.1
Markovian stochastic differential equations

We begin with the following Markovian SDE defined on a non-empty, open and convex subset
D
of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
:

\mathrm{d}X(t)=\bar{b}(X(t))\,\mathrm{d}t+\bar{\sigma}(X(t))\,\mathrm{d}W(t),\ \ t\in[0,\infty),

(4.6)

where
\bar{b}:D\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\bar{\sigma}:D\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
are Borel measurable maps. Let an initial distribution
\mu_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with
