\mu_{0}(D)=1
be given. The above SDE can be seen as SFDE (
1.1
) with data
(D,\mu_{0},b,\sigma)
, where
b:\Lambda[\mathrm{supp}\,\mu_{0},D]\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\sigma:\Lambda[\mathrm{supp}\,\mu_{0},D]\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
are given by

\varphi(t,x):=\bar{\varphi}(x(t)),\ \ (t,x)\in\Lambda[\mathrm{supp}\,\mu_{0},D],\ \ \varphi\in\{b,\sigma\}.

(4.7)

Since the map
\Lambda[\mathrm{supp}\,\mu_{0},D]\ni(t,x)\mapsto x(t)\in D
is progressively measurable and
\bar{b}:D\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\bar{\sigma}:D\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
are Borel measurable, the coefficients
b
and
\sigma
defined by (
4.7
) are progressively measurable.

For each
T\in(0,\infty)
and
\pi\in\Pi_{T}
, the corresponding Euler–Maruyama scheme (
2.8
) with
b
and
\sigma
defined by (
4.7
) and extended to be
0
outside
\Lambda[\mathrm{supp}\,\mu_{0},D]
can be written as

\begin{cases}X^{\pi}(t_{0})=\xi^{\pi},\\
X^{\pi}(t_{k+1})=X^{\pi}(t_{k})+\bar{b}\big(X^{\pi}(t_{k})\big)(t_{k+1}-t_{k})+\bar{\sigma}\big(X^{\pi}(t_{k})\big)Z^{\pi}_{k},\ \ k\in\{0,\dots,m-1\},\end{cases}

(4.8)

where we set
\bar{b}(\bar{x})=0
and
\bar{\sigma}(\bar{x})=0
for each
\bar{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus D
; see
Remark

2.5
. The above is the standard Euler–Maruyama scheme for the Markovian SDE (
4.6
).

Now let us check condition (C) in
Assumption

1
for the map
b
defined by (
4.7
) in view of the drift coefficient
\bar{b}
of the original Markovian SDE (
4.6
); the case of
\varphi=\sigma
is similar, and the correspondences for conditions (G) and (E) are straightforward. Suppose that there exist constants
L\in(0,\infty)
and
\alpha\in(0,1]
and an increasing family
\{U(R)\}_{R\in[1,\infty)}
of open and convex subsets of
D
with
\bigcup_{R\in[1,\infty)}U(R)=D
such that

|\bar{b}(\bar{x})-\bar{b}(\bar{y})|\leq LR|\bar{x}-\bar{y}|^{\alpha}

for any
\bar{x},\bar{y}\in U(R)
and
R\in[1,\infty)
. Then, the corresponding map
b
defined by (
4.7
) satisfies condition (C) in
Assumption

1
with

\alpha_{b}=\alpha,\ \ K_{\mathrm{C},b;T}=L,\ \ D_{\mathrm{C},b;T}(R)=U(R),\ \ R\in[1,\infty),\ \ T\in(0,\infty).

Indeed, (
3.1
) is clear, and (
3.2
) follows from the following calculation: for any
(t,x)\in\Lambda[\mathrm{supp}\,\mu_{0},U(R)]
with
R\in[1,\infty)
,

\displaystyle|b(t,x)-b(s,x)|

\displaystyle=|\bar{b}(x(t))-\bar{b}(x(s))|\leq LR|x(t)-x(s)|^{\alpha}\leq LR\varpi(t,x;t-s)^{\alpha}.

Notice that the term
\varpi(t,x;t-s)
naturally arises in (
3.2
) even in the (time-homogeneous) Markovian setting. The case of Markovian SDE (
4.6
) with time-dependent coefficients can be treated similarly.

4.4.2
Stochastic delay differential equations

Next, consider the following stochastic delay differential equation (SDDE) defined on a non-empty, open and convex subset
D
of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
:

\begin{cases}\mathrm{d}X(t)=\bar{b}\big(X(t),X(t-\tau)\big)\,\mathrm{d}t+\bar{\sigma}\big(X(t),X(t-\tau)\big)\,\mathrm{d}W(t),\ \ t\in[0,\infty),\\
X(t)=\xi(t),\ \ t\in[-\tau,0],\end{cases}

(4.9)

where
\tau\in(0,\infty)
is a fixed constant,
\xi:[-\tau,0]\to D
is a given continuous function, and
\bar{b}:D\times D\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\bar{\sigma}:D\times D\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
are Borel measurable maps.
The above SDDE can be seen as SFDE (
1.1
) with data
(D,\delta_{\xi(0)},b,\sigma)
, where
b:\Lambda[\{\xi(0)\},D]\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\sigma:\Lambda[\{\xi(0)\},D]\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
are given by

\begin{split}&\varphi(t,x):=\bar{\varphi}(x(t),\xi(t-\tau))\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[0,\tau)}(t)+\bar{\varphi}(x(t),x(t-\tau))\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\tau,\infty)}(t),\\
&\hskip 142.26378pt(t,x)\in\Lambda[\{\xi(0)\},D],\ \ \varphi\in\{b,\sigma\}.\end{split}

(4.10)

Since the map
\Lambda[\{\xi(0)\},D]\ni(t,x)\mapsto(x(t),\xi(t-\tau)\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[0,\tau)}(t)+x(t-\tau)\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\tau,\infty)}(t))\in D\times D
is progressively measurable and
\bar{b}:D\times D\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\bar{\sigma}:D\times D\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
are Borel measurable, the coefficients
b
and
\sigma
defined by (
4.10
) are progressively measurable.

Consider the corresponding Euler–Maruyama scheme (
2.8
) with
b
and
\sigma
defined by (
4.10
) and extended to be
0
outside
\Lambda[\{\xi(0)\},D]
. For simplicity, we consider the special partition
\pi=(t_{k})^{m}_{k=0}\in\Pi_{T}
of
[0,T]
with
\tau<T<m\tau
given by
t_{k}=\frac{k\tau}{N}
,
k\in\{0,\ldots,m-1\}
and
t_{m}=T
, where
N=N_{m}\in\{1,\dots,m-1\}
is such that
(m-1)\tau/T<N\leq m\tau/T
. Also, we set
t_{k}:=\frac{k\tau}{N}
for
k\in\{-N,\dots,-1\}
. Noting that
t_{-N}=-\tau
,
t_{0}=0
and
t_{N}=\tau
, the corresponding Euler–Maruyama scheme
X^{\pi}=(X^{\pi}(t_{k}))^{m}_{k=0}
with initial distribution
\mu_{0}=\delta_{\xi(0)}
is defined inductively as follows:

\begin{cases}X^{\pi}(t_{0})=\xi(0),\\
X^{\pi}(t_{k+1})=X^{\pi}(t_{k})+\bar{b}\big(X^{\pi}(t_{k}),\xi(t_{k-N})\big)\,(t_{k+1}-t_{k})+\bar{\sigma}\big(X^{\pi}(t_{k}),\xi(t_{k-N})\big)Z^{\pi}_{k}\ \text{for $k\in\{0,\dots,N-1\}$},\\
X^{\pi}(t_{k+1})=X^{\pi}(t_{k})+\bar{b}\big(X^{\pi}(t_{k}),X^{\pi}(t_{k-N})\big)\,(t_{k+1}-t_{k})+\bar{\sigma}\big(X^{\pi}(t_{k}),X^{\pi}(t_{k-N})\big)Z^{\pi}_{k}\ \text{for $k\in\{N,\ldots,m-1\}$}.\end{cases}

Here, as before, we set
\bar{b}(\bar{x}_{1},\bar{x}_{2})=0
and
\bar{\sigma}(\bar{x}_{1},\bar{x}_{2})=0
for each
(\bar{x}_{1},\bar{x}_{2})\in(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n})\setminus(D\times D)
.

Let us check condition (C) in Assumption
1
for the map
b
defined by (
4.10
) in view of the drift coefficient
\bar{b}
of the original SDDE (
4.9
); the case of
\varphi=\sigma
is similar, and the correspondences for conditions (G) and (E) can be discussed similarly as well, and hence we omit them. Assume that the initial function
\xi:[-\tau,0]\to D
is
\frac{1}{2}
-Hölder continuous, that is,

[\xi]_{1/2}:=\sup_{-\tau\leq s<t\leq 0}\frac{|\xi(t)-\xi(s)|}{(t-s)^{1/2}}<\infty.

Furthermore, assume that there exist constants
L\in(0,\infty)
and
\alpha\in(0,1]
and an increasing family
\{U(R)\}_{R\in[1,\infty)}
of open and convex subsets of
D
with
\bigcup_{R\in[1,\infty)}U(R)=D
such that

|\bar{b}(\bar{x}_{1},\bar{x}_{2})-\bar{b}(\bar{y}_{1},\bar{y}_{2})|\leq LR\big\{|\bar{x}_{1}-\bar{y}_{1}|^{\alpha}+|\bar{x}_{2}-\bar{y}_{2}|^{\alpha}\big\}

for any
\bar{x}_{1},\bar{x}_{2},\bar{y}_{1},\bar{y}_{2}\in U(R)
and
R\in[1,\infty)
. Since
\xi:[-\tau,0]\to D
is continuous and
\{U(R)\}_{R\in[1,\infty)}
is an increasing open covering of
D
, there exists
R_{0}\in[1,\infty)
such that
\xi(t)\in U(R)
for any
t\in[-\tau,0]
and
R\in[R_{0},\infty)
. Also, recall that the initial distribution of the corresponding SFDE is
\mu_{0}=\delta_{\xi(0)}
. In this setting, the corresponding map
b
defined by (
4.10
) satisfies condition (C) in
Assumption

1
with

\alpha_{b}=\alpha,\ \ K_{\mathrm{C},b;T}=(2+[\xi]_{1/2}^{\alpha})L,\ \ D_{\mathrm{C},b;T}(R)=\begin{cases}\emptyset\ \ &\text{if $R\in[1,R_{0})$},\\
U(R)\ \ &\text{if $R\in[R_{0},\infty)$},\end{cases}\ \ \text{for $T\in(0,\infty)$}.

(4.11)

Indeed, for any
(t,x),(t,y)\in\Lambda[\{\xi(0)\},U(R)]
with
R\in[R_{0},\infty)
,

\displaystyle|b(t,x)-b(t,y)|

\displaystyle=|\bar{b}(x(t),\xi(t-\tau))-\bar{b}(y(t),\xi(t-\tau))|\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[0,\tau)}(t)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+|\bar{b}(x(t),x(t-\tau))-\bar{b}(y(t),y(t-\tau))|\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\tau,\infty)}(t)

\displaystyle\leq LR|x(t)-y(t)|^{\alpha}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[0,\tau)}(t)+LR\big\{|x(t)-y(t)|^{\alpha}+|x(t-\tau)-y(t-\tau)|^{\alpha}\big\}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\tau,\infty)}(t)

\displaystyle\leq 2LR\|x_{t}-y_{t}\|_{\infty}^{\alpha},

and hence the first condition (
3.1
) holds. Moreover, the second condition (
3.2
) can be checked by the following observations: Let
(t,x)\in\Lambda[\{\xi(0)\},U(R)]
with
R\in[R_{0},\infty)
and
s\in[0,t]
. Notice that
x(0)=\xi(0)
by the definition of the set
\Lambda[\{\xi(0)\},U(R)]
. Also, recall that
\xi:[-\tau,0]\to D
is
\frac{1}{2}
-Hölder continuous. We consider the three cases of
0\leq s\leq t\leq\tau
,
0\leq s\leq\tau<t
and
\tau<s\leq t
separately. First, if
0\leq s\leq t\leq\tau
, we have

\displaystyle|b(t,x)-b(s,x)|

\displaystyle=\Big|\bar{b}(x(t),\xi(t-\tau))-\bar{b}(x(s),\xi(s-\tau))\Big|

\displaystyle\leq LR\Big\{\big|x(t)-x(s)\big|^{\alpha}+\big|\xi(t-\tau)-\xi(s-\tau)\big|^{\alpha}\Big\}

\displaystyle\leq LR\Big\{\varpi(t,x;t-s)^{\alpha}+[\xi]_{1/2}^{\alpha}(t-s)^{\alpha/2}\Big\}

\displaystyle\leq(1+[\xi]_{1/2}^{\alpha})LR\Big\{\varpi(t,x;t-s)+(t-s)^{1/2}\Big\}^{\alpha}.

Second, if
0\leq s\leq\tau<t
, thanks to the fact that
x(0)=\xi(0)
, we have

\displaystyle|b(t,x)-b(s,x)|

\displaystyle=\Big|\bar{b}(x(t),x(t-\tau))-\bar{b}(x(t),x(0))+\bar{b}(x(t),\xi(0))-\bar{b}(x(s),\xi(s-\tau))\Big|

\displaystyle\leq LR\Big\{\big|x(t-\tau)-x(0)\big|^{\alpha}+\big|x(t)-x(s)\big|^{\alpha}+\big|\xi(0)-\xi(s-\tau)\big|^{\alpha}\Big\}

\displaystyle\leq LR\Big\{2\varpi(t,x;t-s)^{\alpha}+[\xi]_{1/2}^{\alpha}(t-s)^{\alpha/2}\Big\}

\displaystyle\leq(2+[\xi]_{1/2}^{\alpha})LR\Big\{\varpi(t,x;t-s)+(t-s)^{1/2}\Big\}^{\alpha}.

Third, if
\tau<s\leq t
, we have

\displaystyle|b(t,x)-b(s,x)|

\displaystyle=\Big|\bar{b}(x(t),x(t-\tau))-\bar{b}(x(s),x(s-\tau))\Big|

\displaystyle\leq LR\Big\{\big|x(t)-x(s)\big|^{\alpha}+\big|x(t-\tau)-x(s-\tau)\big|^{\alpha}\Big\}

\displaystyle\leq 2LR\varpi(t,x;t-s)^{\alpha}.

The above observations show that the map
b
satisfies (
3.2
), and hence condition (C) in
Assumption

1
holds with the setting of (
4.11
).

4.4.3
Reflected stochastic differential equations

Next, we investigate reflected SDEs and show that our framework is applicable to this non-standard (Markovian) SDE after an appropriate transformation using the so-called Skorokhod map.
Here, for the sake of simplicity of presentation, we focus only on one-dimensional reflected SDEs defined on the half real line
[0,\infty)
having reflection at
\{0\}
. Specifically, we consider the following reflected SDE:

\mathrm{d}\check{X}(t)=\bar{b}(\check{X}(t))\,\mathrm{d}t+\bar{\sigma}(\check{X}(t))\,\mathrm{d}W(t)+\mathrm{d}\Phi_{\check{X}}(t),\ \ t\in[0,\infty),

(4.12)

where
\bar{b}:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
and
\bar{\sigma}:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
are Borel measurable maps. Fix
\mu_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R})
with
\mathrm{supp}\,\mu_{0}\subset[0,\infty)
. We call a tuple
(\check{X},\Phi_{\check{X}},W,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
a weak solution of the reflected SDE (
4.12
) with initial distribution
\mu_{0}
if
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
is a complete probability space,
\mathbb{F}
is a filtration satisfying the usual conditions,
W
is a one-dimensional Brownian motion relative to
\mathbb{F}
,
\check{X}
is a one-dimensional continuous
\mathbb{F}
-adapted process with
\mathbb{P}\circ\check{X}(0)^{-1}=\mu_{0}
such that

\mathbb{P}\left(\text{$\check{X}(t)\geq 0$ for any $t\in[0,\infty)$}\right)=1

and

\mathbb{P}\left(\text{$\int^{T}_{0}\big\{|\bar{b}(\check{X}(s))|+|\bar{\sigma}(\check{X}(s))|^{2}\big\}\,\mathrm{d}s<\infty$ for any $T\in(0,\infty)$}\right)=1,

\Phi_{\check{X}}
is a one-dimensional, non-decreasing and
\mathbb{F}
-adapted process with
\Phi_{\check{X}}(0)=0
, and
\mathbb{P}
-a.s., they satisfy

\check{X}(t)=\check{X}(0)+\int^{t}_{0}\bar{b}(\check{X}(s))\,\mathrm{d}s+\int^{t}_{0}\bar{\sigma}(\check{X}(s))\,\mathrm{d}W(s)+\Phi_{\check{X}}(t)

and

\Phi_{\check{X}}(t)=\int^{t}_{0}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{\{0\}}(\check{X}(s))\,\mathrm{d}\Phi_{\check{X}}(s)

for all
t\in[0,\infty)
. The non-decreasing process
\Phi_{\check{X}}
plays the role of the reflection of
\check{X}
at the boundary
\{0\}
of
[0,\infty)
.

The reflected SDE (
4.12
) can be understood as a solution of the Skorokhod equation on
[0,\infty)
.
Here, a pair
(\psi,\phi)
is called a solution of the Skorokhod equation on
[0,\infty)
associated with a given input function
x\in\mathcal{C}_{+}:=\{x\in C([0,\infty);\mathbb{R})\mid x(0)\geq 0\}
if
(\psi,\phi)\in\mathcal{C}_{+}\times\mathcal{C}_{+}
,
\psi(t)\geq 0
for all
t\in[0,\infty)
,
\phi
is non-decreasing with
\phi(0)=0
, and the following hold:

\displaystyle\psi(t)=x(t)+\phi(t)\ \ \text{and}\ \ \phi(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{\{0\}}(\psi(s))\,\mathrm{d}\phi(s),\ \ t\in[0,\infty).

It is known that, for any
x\in\mathcal{C}_{+}
, there exists a unique solution
(\psi,\phi)
of the Skorokhod equation on
[0,\infty)
associated with
x
(see for example
[
41
, Chapter III, Lemma 4.2]
and also
[
66
]
for more general domain).
The map
\Gamma:x\mapsto\psi
is called the Skorokhod map, which can be explicitly written by

(\Gamma x)(t)=x(t)-\min_{s\in[0,t]}(x(s)\wedge 0),\ \ t\in[0,\infty),\ x\in\mathcal{C}_{+},

(4.13)

see
[
41
, Chapter III, Lemma 4.2]
for more details. Observe that

\Gamma 0=0,\ \ |(\Gamma x)(t)-(\Gamma y)(t)|\leq 2\|x_{t}-y_{t}\|_{\infty}\ \ \text{and}\ \ |(\Gamma x)(t)-(\Gamma x)(s)|\leq 2\varpi(t,x;t-s)

(4.14)

for any
x,y\in\mathcal{C}_{+}
and
0\leq s\leq t<\infty
. Note also that the map
\Lambda[[0,\infty),\mathbb{R}]\ni(t,x)\mapsto(\Gamma x)(t)\in[0,\infty)
is progressively measurable.

Using the Skorokhod map
\Gamma
, we can convert the reflected SDE (
4.12
) to the following path-dependent SDE:

\mathrm{d}X(t)=\bar{b}\big((\Gamma X)(t)\big)\,\mathrm{d}t+\bar{\sigma}\big((\Gamma X)(t)\big)\,\mathrm{d}W(t),\ \ t\in[0,\infty),

(4.15)

which can be seen as SFDE (
1.1
) with data
(\mathbb{R},\mu_{0},b,\sigma)
, where the progressively measurable coefficients
b,\sigma:\Lambda[\mathrm{supp}\,\mu_{0},\mathbb{R}]\to\mathbb{R}
are given by

\varphi(t,x):=\bar{\varphi}\big((\Gamma x)(t)\big),\ \ (t,x)\in\Lambda[\mathrm{supp}\,\mu_{0},\mathbb{R}],\ \ \varphi\in\{b,\sigma\}.

(4.16)

The solutions of (
4.12
) and (
4.15
) are related to each other via the following one-to-one correspondence:

(\check{X},\Phi_{\check{X}})=(\Gamma X,\Gamma X-X)

(4.17)

and

X=\check{X}(0)+\int^{\cdot}_{0}\bar{b}(\check{X}(s))\,\mathrm{d}s+\int^{\cdot}_{0}\bar{\sigma}(\check{X}(s))\,\mathrm{d}W(s).

(4.18)

Indeed, if
(X,W,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
is a weak solution of the SFDE (
4.15
) with initial distribution
\mu_{0}
, then the pair
(\check{X},\Phi_{\check{X}})
defined by (
4.17
) is nothing but the unique solution (for each
\omega\in\Omega
) of the Skorokhod equation on
[0,\infty)
associated with the input

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle=X(0)+\int^{\cdot}_{0}\bar{b}\big((\Gamma X)(s)\big)\,\mathrm{d}s+\int^{\cdot}_{0}\bar{\sigma}\big((\Gamma X)(s)\big)\,\mathrm{d}W(s)

\displaystyle=\check{X}(0)+\int^{\cdot}_{0}\bar{b}(\check{X}(s))\,\mathrm{d}s+\int^{\cdot}_{0}\bar{\sigma}(\check{X}(s))\,\mathrm{d}W(s),

which in turn implies that the tuple
(\check{X},\Phi_{\check{X}},W,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
is a weak solution of the reflected SDE (
4.12
) with initial distribution
\mu_{0}
and that the relation (
4.18
) holds. Conversely, if
(\check{X},\Phi_{\check{X}},W,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
is a weak solution of the reflected SDE (
4.12
) with initial distribution
\mu_{0}
, then the pair
(\check{X},\Phi_{\check{X}})
is the unique solution (for each
\omega\in\Omega
) of the Skorokhod equation on
[0,\infty)
associated with the input
X
defined by (
4.18
), and hence the relation (
4.17
) holds. Inserting the relation (
4.17
) to (
4.18
), we see that
(X,W,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
is a weak solution of the SFDE (
4.15
) with initial distribution
\mu_{0}
.

Although the reflected SDE (
4.12
) itself is beyond the framework of the present paper due to the additional reflection term
\Phi_{\check{X}}
, the SFDE (
4.15
) fits into our framework. Then, we focus on the latter and consider its weak approximation by means of the Euler–Maruyama scheme (
2.8
). Noting the explicit expression (
4.13
) of the Skorokhod map
\Gamma
, for each
T\in(0,\infty)
and
\pi=(t_{k})_{k=0}^{m}\in\Pi_{T}
, the Euler–Maruyama scheme (
2.8
) corresponding to the SFDE (
4.15
) is written by

\begin{cases}X^{\pi}(t_{0})=\xi^{\pi},\\
X^{\pi}(t_{k+1})=X^{\pi}(t_{k})+\bar{b}\big(X^{\pi}(t_{k})-\min\{X^{\pi}(t_{0}),\ldots,X^{\pi}(t_{k}),0\}\big)\,(t_{k+1}-t_{k})\\
\hskip 56.9055pt+\bar{\sigma}\big(X^{\pi}(t_{k})-\min\{X^{\pi}(t_{0}),\ldots,X^{\pi}(t_{k}),0\}\big)Z_{k}^{\pi},\ \ k\in\{0,\dots,m-1\}.\end{cases}

(4.19)

Thanks to the Lipschitz continuity of the mapping (
4.17
) from
X
to
(\check{X},\Phi_{\check{X}})
,
Lemma

A.1
in
Appendix

A
ensures that a quantitative estimate for the weak convergence
\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}]\to X_{T}
on
\mathcal{C}_{T}
is carried over to that of
(\Gamma\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}],\Gamma\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}]-\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}])\to(\check{X}_{T},(\Phi_{\check{X}})_{T})
on
\mathcal{C}_{T}\times\mathcal{C}_{T}
with the same rate of convergence with respect to the Lévy–Prokhorov metric. This leads to a quantitative weak approximation for the original reflected SDE (
4.12
). The idea to convert the reflected SDE (
4.12
) to the SFDE (
4.15
) originates from
[
7
]
and is used in
[
1
]
for the purpose of Euler–Maruyama approximation under the Lipschitz continuity condition for the coefficients.

Let us check condition (C) in Assumption
1
for the map
b
defined by (
4.16
) in view of the coefficient
\bar{b}
of the original reflected SDE (
4.12
); the case of
\sigma
is similar, and the correspondences for conditions (G) and (E) can be discussed similarly as well, and hence we omit them.
Suppose that there exist a non-decreasing function
V:[0,\infty)\to[1,\infty)
and a constant
\alpha\in(0,1]
such that

|\bar{b}(\bar{x})-\bar{b}(\bar{y})|\leq V(\bar{x}+\bar{y})|\bar{x}-\bar{y}|^{\alpha}\ \ \text{for any $\bar{x},\bar{y}\in[0,\infty)$.}

Thanks to (
4.14
), we have

\displaystyle|b(t,x)-b(t,y)|

\displaystyle=\Big|\bar{b}\big((\Gamma x)(t)\big)-\bar{b}\big((\Gamma y)(t)\big)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq V((\Gamma x)(t)+(\Gamma y)(t))\Big|(\Gamma x)(t)-(\Gamma y)(t)\Big|^{\alpha}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{\alpha}V(2\|x_{t}\|_{\infty}+2\|y_{t}\|_{\infty})\|x_{t}-y_{t}\|_{\infty}^{\alpha}

for any
(t,x),(t,y)\in\Lambda[\mathrm{supp}\,\mu_{0},\mathbb{R}]
. Similarly,

\displaystyle|b(t,x)-b(s,x)|

\displaystyle=\Big|\bar{b}\big((\Gamma x)(t)\big)-\bar{b}\big((\Gamma x)(s)\big)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq V((\Gamma x)(t)+(\Gamma x)(s))\Big|(\Gamma x)(t)-(\Gamma x)(s)\Big|^{\alpha}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{\alpha}V(4\|x_{t}\|_{\infty})\varpi(t,x;t-s)^{\alpha}

for any
(t,x)\in\Lambda[\mathrm{supp}\,\mu_{0},\mathbb{R}]
and
s\in[0,t]
. Hence, the map
b
satisfies condition (C) in Assumption
1
with

\alpha_{b}=\alpha,\ \ K_{\mathrm{C},b;T}=2^{\alpha},\ \ D_{\mathrm{C},b;T}=\begin{cases}\left\{\bar{x}\in\mathbb{R}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}|\bar{x}|<V^{-1}(R)/4\right\}\ &\text{for $R\in[1,V(\infty))$,}\\
\mathbb{R}\ &\text{for $R\in[V(\infty),\infty)$,}\end{cases}

for
T\in(0,\infty)
, where
V(\infty):=\lim_{\theta\to\infty}V(\theta)
, and
V^{-1}(R):=\inf\{\theta\geq 0\,|\,V(\theta)>R\}
for
R\in[1,V(\infty))
.

4.4.4
Stochastic oscillator models / Stochastic integro-differential equations

We consider the stochastic oscillator model formally described by

\displaystyle\ddot{x}(t)+f(x(t),\dot{x}(t))=g(x(t),\dot{x}(t))\dot{W}(t),\ \ (x(0),\dot{x}(0))=(\xi,\dot{\xi})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n},

where
\dot{W}
is a
d
-dimensional white noise and
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
g:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
are Borel measurable maps. The above oscillator model is rewritten as the following
2n
-dimensional Markovian SDE for the pair
(x,\dot{x})
:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\mathrm{d}x(t)=\dot{x}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t,\\
\mathrm{d}\dot{x}(t)=-f(x(t),\dot{x}(t))\,\mathrm{d}t+g(x(t),\dot{x}(t))\,\mathrm{d}W(t),\\
(x(0),\dot{x}(0))=(\xi,\dot{\xi}).\end{cases}\ \ t\in[0,\infty),

(4.20)

One of the main difficulties of the system (
4.20
) lies in the degeneracy of the noise. Due to this difficulty, the above Markovian SDE does not satisfy the condition (E) in
Assumption

1
even if the coefficient
g
is uniformly elliptic. However, we can still apply our main results to this setting after transforming the degenerate Markovian SDE (
4.20
) to a “non-degenerate” and non-Markovian SFDE. Namely, noting that the first component
x
of the solution of (
4.20
) is explicitly written by
x(t)=\xi+\int^{t}_{0}\dot{x}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s
, we can rewrite the system (
4.20
) as a sole equation for the second component
X=\dot{x}
, which is of form of the following stochastic integro-differential equation:

\begin{cases}\mathrm{d}X(t)=-f\left(\xi+\int_{0}^{t}X(s)\,\mathrm{d}s,X(t)\right)\,\mathrm{d}t+g\left(\xi+\int_{0}^{t}X(s)\,\mathrm{d}s,X(t)\right)\,\mathrm{d}W(t),\\
\hskip 227.62204ptt\in[0,\infty),\\
X(0)=\dot{\xi}.\end{cases}

(4.21)

Clearly, if
(x,\dot{x})
solves the SDE (
4.20
), then
X:=\dot{x}
solves the stochastic integro-differential equation (
4.21
). Conversely, if
X
solves the stochastic integro-differential equation (
4.21
), then the pair
(x,\dot{x}):=(\xi+\int^{\cdot}_{0}X(s)\,\mathrm{d}s,X)
solves the SDE (
4.20
).

The stochastic integro-differential equation (
4.21
) can be seen as SFDE (
1.1
) with data
(D,\mu_{0},b,\sigma)=(\mathbb{R}^{n},\delta_{\dot{\xi}},b,\sigma)
, where the coefficients
b:\Lambda[\{\dot{\xi}\},\mathbb{R}^{n}]\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\sigma:\Lambda[\{\dot{\xi}\},\mathbb{R}^{n}]\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
are given by

\begin{split}&b(t,\dot{x}):=-f\left(\xi+\int^{t}_{0}\dot{x}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s,\dot{x}(t)\right),\ \ \sigma(t,\dot{x}):=g\left(\xi+\int^{t}_{0}\dot{x}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s,\dot{x}(t)\right),\\
&\hskip 142.26378pt(t,\dot{x})\in\Lambda[\{\dot{\xi}\},\mathbb{R}^{n}].\end{split}

(4.22)

Since the map
\Lambda[\{\dot{\xi}\},\mathbb{R}^{n}]\ni(t,\dot{x})\mapsto(\xi+\int^{t}_{0}\dot{x}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s,\dot{x}(t))\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
is progressively measurable and
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
g:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
are Borel measurable, the coefficients
b
and
\sigma
defined by (
4.22
) are progressively measurable.

The Euler–Maruyama scheme (
2.8
) corresponding to the stochastic integro-differential equation (
4.21
) is of the following form:

\begin{split}X^{\pi}(t_{k+1})&=X^{\pi}(t_{k})-f\left(\xi+\sum^{k-1}_{j=0}\frac{t_{j+1}-t_{j}}{2}\left(X^{\pi}(t_{j})+X^{\pi}(t_{j+1})\right),X^{\pi}(t_{k})\right)(t_{k+1}-t_{k})\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt+g\left(\xi+\sum^{k-1}_{j=0}\frac{t_{j+1}-t_{j}}{2}\left(X^{\pi}(t_{j})+X^{\pi}(t_{j+1})\right),X^{\pi}(t_{k})\right)Z^{\pi}_{k},\ k\in\{0,\dots,m-1\},\end{split}

(4.23)

with initial condition
X^{\pi}(t_{0})=\dot{\xi}
. By virtue of the Lipschitz continuity of the mapping
\dot{x}\mapsto(\xi+\int^{\cdot\wedge T}_{0}\dot{x}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s,\dot{x})
from
\mathcal{C}_{T}
to
\mathcal{C}_{T}\times\mathcal{C}_{T}
, a quantitative estimate for the weak convergence
\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}]\to X_{T}
on
\mathcal{C}_{T}
is translated into that of
(\xi+\int^{\cdot\wedge T}_{0}\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}](s)\,\mathrm{d}s,\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}])\to(\xi+\int^{\cdot\wedge T}_{0}X(s)\,\mathrm{d}s,X_{T})
on
\mathcal{C}_{T}\times\mathcal{C}_{T}
via
Lemma

A.1
in
Appendix

A
. This yields a quantitative weak approximation with the same convergence rate for the original stochastic oscillator model (
4.20
).

The important thing is that, although the equation (
4.21
) is a path-dependent (and hence non-Markovian) SFDE, it is non-degenerate in the sense of condition (E) in
Assumption

1
under an appropriate ellipticity condition for
g
. Indeed, assuming that

\big\langle g(x_{1},x_{2})g(x_{1},x_{2})^{\top}\eta,\eta\big\rangle\geq\frac{|\eta|^{2}}{V(|x_{1}|+|x_{2}|)^{2}}\ \ \text{for any $(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}$ and $\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{n}$}

for some non-decreasing function
V:[0,\infty)\to[1,\infty)
, the map
\sigma
defined by (
4.22
) satisfies condition (E) in
Assumption

1
with

K_{\mathrm{E};T}=1,\ \ D_{\mathrm{E};T}(R)=\begin{cases}\left\{\bar{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}|\xi|+(T+1)|\bar{x}|<V^{-1}(R)\right\}\ \ &\text{for $R\in[1,V(\infty))$},\\
\mathbb{R}^{n}\ \ &\text{for $R\in[V(\infty),\infty)$},\end{cases}

for
T\in(0,\infty)
, where
V(\infty):=\lim_{\theta\to\infty}V(\theta)
, and
V^{-1}(R):=\inf\{\theta\geq 0\,|\,V(\theta)>R\}
for
R\in[1,V(\infty))
. The correspondence for condition (G) in
Assumption

1
is similar. Now let us check condition (C) in
Assumption

1
for the map
b
defined by (
4.22
) in view of the coefficient
f
of the original stochastic oscillator model (
4.20
). Suppose that

|f(x_{1},x_{2})-f(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime})|\leq V\big(|x_{1}|+|x_{2}|+|x_{1}^{\prime}|+|x_{2}^{\prime}|\big)\big\{|x_{1}-x_{1}^{\prime}|+|x_{2}-x_{2}^{\prime}|\big\}^{\alpha}

for any
x_{1},x_{2},x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
for some constant
\alpha\in(0,1]
and non-decreasing function
V:[0,\infty)\to[1,\infty)
. Observe that, for any
(t,\dot{x}),(t,\dot{y})\in\Lambda[\{\dot{\xi}\},\mathbb{R}^{n}]
,

\displaystyle|b(t,\dot{x})-b(t,\dot{y})|

\displaystyle\leq V\left(2|\xi|+\int^{t}_{0}|\dot{x}(s)|\,\mathrm{d}s+|\dot{x}(t)|+\int^{t}_{0}|\dot{y}(s)|\,\mathrm{d}s+|\dot{y}(t)|\right)\left\{\int^{t}_{0}|\dot{x}(s)-\dot{y}(s)|\,\mathrm{d}s+|\dot{x}(t)-\dot{y}(t)|\right\}^{\alpha}

\displaystyle\leq V\Big(2|\xi|+(t+1)(\|\dot{x}_{t}\|_{\infty}+\|\dot{y}_{t}\|_{\infty})\Big)(t+1)\|\dot{x}_{t}-\dot{y}_{t}\|_{\infty}^{\alpha}.

Also, for any
(t,\dot{x})\in\Lambda[\{\dot{\xi}\},\mathbb{R}^{n}]
and
s\in[0,t]
, we have

\displaystyle|b(t,\dot{x})-b(s,\dot{x})|

\displaystyle\leq V\big(2|\xi|+2(t+1)\|\dot{x}_{t}\|_{\infty}\big)\left\{\int^{t}_{s}|\dot{x}(r)|\,\mathrm{d}r+|\dot{x}(t)-\dot{x}(s)|\right\}^{\alpha}

\displaystyle\leq V\Big(2|\xi|+2(t+1)\|\dot{x}_{t}\|_{\infty}\Big)(\sqrt{t}\|\dot{x}_{t}\|_{\infty}+1)^{\alpha}\big\{(t-s)^{1/2}+\varpi(t,\dot{x};t-s)\big\}^{\alpha}.

Hence, the map
b
satisfies condition (C) in
Assumption

1
with

\alpha_{b}=\alpha,\ \ K_{\mathrm{C},b;T}=T+1,\ \ D_{\mathrm{C},b;T}(R)=\left\{\bar{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}|\bar{x}|<\widetilde{V}_{T,|\xi|}^{-1}(R)\right\}\ \ \text{for $R\in[1,\infty)$},

for
T\in(0,\infty)
, where
\widetilde{V}_{T,|\xi|}(\theta):=V(2|\xi|+2(T+1)\theta)(\sqrt{T}\theta+1)^{\alpha}
for
\theta\in[0,\infty)
.

5
Proof of the main result: A generalized coupling approach

This section is devoted to the proof of
Theorem

3.1
. First, let us sketch the idea of the proof.

Our goal is to estimate, for each
T\in(0,\infty)
and
\pi=(t_{k})^{m}_{k=0}\in\Pi_{T}
, the weak approximation error
d_{\mathrm{LP}}(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T}),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}]))
between a (given) weak solution
(X,W,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
of the original SFDE (
1.1
) and (the linear interpolation of) the Euler–Maruyama scheme
X^{\pi}=(X^{\pi}(t_{k}))^{m}_{k=0}
with the same initial distribution as
X
. To do so, by the duality formula (
2.2
) for the Lévy–Prokhorov metric, it is important to construct a suitable coupling between
\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T})\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{C}_{T})
and
\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}])\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{C}_{T})
, that is, a pair of
\mathcal{C}_{T}\times\mathcal{C}_{T}
-valued random element
(\widetilde{X}_{T},\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widetilde{X}^{\pi}])
defined on a probability space
(\widetilde{\Omega},\widetilde{\mathcal{F}},\widetilde{\mathbb{P}})
with
\mathrm{Law}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}}(\widetilde{X}_{T})=\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T})
and
\mathrm{Law}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widetilde{X}^{\pi}])=\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}])
such that the pathwise error
\|\widetilde{X}_{T}-\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widetilde{X}^{\pi}]\|_{\infty}
becomes as small as possible with respect to the topology of the convergence in
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}
-probability. When the coefficients
b
and
\sigma
satisfy good regularity conditions such as the global Lipschitz continuity condition, considering the synchronous coupling of
\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T})
and
\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}])
, that is, the pair of the (strong) solution
\widetilde{X}_{T}=X_{T}
of (
1.1
) and the Euler–Maruyama scheme
\widetilde{X}^{\pi}
defined on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
and given by (
2.8
) with
(\xi^{\pi},Z^{\pi}_{0},\dots,Z^{\pi}_{m-1})
replaced by
(X(0),W(t_{1})-W(t_{0}),\dots,W(t_{m})-W(t_{m-1}))
, we can estimate the error
\|\widetilde{X}_{T}-\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widetilde{X}^{\pi}]\|_{\infty}
(in the
L^{p}(\mathbb{P})
-sense) by a standard argument based on the stability estimate. However, under our general setting of
Assumption

1
, such a standard argument does not work well, and a direct construction of the (true) coupling
(\widetilde{X}_{T},\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widetilde{X}^{\pi}])
and the estimate of
\|\widetilde{X}_{T}-\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widetilde{X}^{\pi}]\|_{\infty}
are quite difficult tasks.

The main idea for the proof of our main result (
Theorem

3.1
) is to divide the weak approximation error
d_{\mathrm{LP}}(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T}),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}]))
into the following two terms:

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{LP}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T}),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}])\big)

\displaystyle\leq d_{\mathrm{LP}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T}),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}])\big)+d_{\mathrm{LP}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}])\big).

Here, the auxiliary term
\widehat{X}^{\pi}
, which will be introduced in
Section

5.1
below, is a “controlled version” of the (true) Euler–Maruyama scheme
X^{\pi}
. In this paper, we call
\widehat{X}^{\pi}
a
controlled Euler–Maruyama scheme
. We will construct
\widehat{X}^{\pi}
on the same probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
as the (given) weak solution
(X,W,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
of the original SFDE (
1.1
) to meet the following requirements:

(i)

The pathwise error
\|X_{T}-\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]\|_{\infty}
becomes as small as possible with respect to the topology of the convergence in
\mathbb{P}
-probability, which provides us an estimate of
d_{\mathrm{LP}}(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T}),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]))
.

(ii)

The error between
\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}])
and
\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}])
becomes as small as possible in the sense of the total variation, which provides us an estimate of
d_{\mathrm{LP}}(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}]))
.

The pair
(X_{T},\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}]^{\pi})
of random elements on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
can be seen as a
generalized coupling
between the prescribed probability measures
\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T})
and
\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}])
on
\mathcal{C}_{T}
in the sense that, although it is not a true coupling since the second marginal
\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}])
is not equal to
\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}])
, they are close in some sense. Using a stochastic control technique, we construct the auxiliary term
\widehat{X}^{\pi}
which “controls” the corrective error by means of (i) and (ii) above. This kind of argument is called a
generalized coupling approach
(also known as the Control-and-Reimburse strategy) and has been applied to the study of ergodicity of infinite-dimensional Markov models in
[
15
,
48
]
among others. To the best of our knowledge, the present paper is the first to apply the idea of generalized couplings to the theory of Euler–Maruyama approximations.

5.1
Controlled Euler–Maruyama scheme

Now we introduce the controlled Euler–Maruyama scheme. In the rest of this section, we use the following additional notations: for each
T\in(0,\infty)
and
\pi=(t_{k})^{m}_{k=0}\in\Pi_{T}
, define

\pi(t):=\sum^{m-1}_{k=0}t_{k}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[t_{k},t_{k+1})}(t)+t_{m}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[t_{m},\infty)}(t),\ \ t\in[0,\infty).

Also, with slight abuse of notation, we define the polygonal function
\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[x]\in\mathcal{C}_{T}
for each
x\in\mathcal{C}
by the same way as in (
2.6
), which represents the linear interpolation of the points
(x(t_{k}))^{m}_{k=0}
.

Let data
(D,\mu_{0},b,\sigma)
satisfying
Assumption

1
be given. As before, we extend the domain
\Lambda[\mathrm{supp}\,\mu_{0},D]
of the coefficients
b
and
\sigma
to the whole space
\Lambda
by setting
b(t,x)=0
and
\sigma(t,x)=0
for each
(t,x)\in\Lambda\setminus\Lambda[\mathrm{supp}\,\mu_{0},D]
. Suppose that we are given a weak solution
(X,W,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
of SFDE (
1.1
) associated with
(D,\mu_{0},b,\sigma)
. For each
T\in(0,\infty)
,
\pi\in\Pi_{T}
,
\lambda\in(0,\infty)
,
\Delta\in(0,1]
and
\vec{R}=(R_{\mathrm{G},b},R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{C},b},R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{E}})\in[1,\infty)^{5}
, consider the following SFDE:

\begin{cases}\mathrm{d}\widehat{X}^{\pi}(t)=b\big(\pi(t),\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]\big)\,\mathrm{d}t+\sigma\big(\pi(t),\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]\big)\,\mathrm{d}W(t)\\
\hskip 113.81102pt+\frac{\lambda}{\Delta}\big(X(t)-\widehat{X}^{\pi}(t)\big)\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[0,T\wedge\tau^{\pi}\wedge\zeta)}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t,\ \ t\in[0,\infty),\\
\widehat{X}^{\pi}(0)=X(0),\ \ \tau^{\pi}=\inf\left\{t\geq 0\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\big|X(t)-\widehat{X}^{\pi}(t)\big|\geq\Delta\right\},\end{cases}

(5.1)

where

\zeta:=\inf\left\{t\geq 0\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\mathrm{dist}\!\left(X(t),\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus D_{T}(\vec{R})\right)\leq\Delta\right\}.

Notice that, for each
t\in[t_{k},t_{k+1})
with
k\in\{0,\dots,m-1\}
, we have
b(\pi(t),\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}])=b(t_{k},\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]_{t_{k}})
,
\sigma(\pi(t),\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}])=\sigma(t_{k},\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]_{t_{k}})
, and

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]_{t_{k}}(\cdot)

\displaystyle=\sum^{k-1}_{j=0}\left\{\frac{t_{j+1}-\cdot}{t_{j+1}-t_{j}}\widehat{X}^{\pi}(t_{j})+\frac{\cdot-t_{j}}{t_{j+1}-t_{j}}\widehat{X}^{\pi}(t_{j+1})\right\}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[t_{j},t_{j+1})}(\cdot)+\widehat{X}^{\pi}(t_{k})\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[t_{k},\infty)}(\cdot),

which depends only on
(\widehat{X}^{\pi}(t_{j}))^{k}_{j=0}
. Also, noting that
t_{m}=T
, for each
t\in[T,\infty)
, we have
b(\pi(t),\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}])=b(T,\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}])
,
\sigma(\pi(t),\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}])=\sigma(T,\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}])
, and

\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}](\cdot)=\sum^{m-1}_{j=0}\left\{\frac{t_{j+1}-\cdot}{t_{j+1}-t_{j}}\widehat{X}^{\pi}(t_{j})+\frac{\cdot-t_{j}}{t_{j+1}-t_{j}}\widehat{X}^{\pi}(t_{j+1})\right\}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[t_{j},t_{j+1})}(\cdot)+\widehat{X}^{\pi}(T)\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[T,\infty)}(\cdot),

which depends only on
(\widehat{X}^{\pi}(t_{j}))^{m}_{j=0}
. By
Lemma

C.2
in
Appendix

C
(see also
Remark

C.3
), there exists a unique (up to
\mathbb{P}
-indistinguishability)
\mathbb{R}^{n}
-valued continuous and
\mathbb{F}
-adapted process
\widehat{X}^{\pi}=(\widehat{X}^{\pi}(t))_{t\in[0,\infty)}
on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
satisfying (
5.1
). We call
\widehat{X}^{\pi}
the
controlled Euler–Maruyama scheme
associated with
(X,W,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
and
(T,\pi,\lambda,\Delta,\vec{R})
.

Remark 5.1
.

•

Notice that (
5.1
) is an SFDE defined on the filtered probability space and
d
-dimensional Brownian motion appearing in a given weak solution
(X,W,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
of the original SFDE, and it involves the stopping time
\tau^{\pi}
which depends on the solution
\widehat{X}^{\pi}
itself. Furthermore, we do not assume that the coefficient
b
or
\sigma
is globally Lipschitz continuous. Hence, the existence and uniqueness of the solution
\widehat{X}^{\pi}
is a non-trivial issue. However, thanks to the appearance of the time-discretization in
b(\pi(t),\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}])
and
\sigma(\pi(t),\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}])
, we can construct the solution
\widehat{X}^{\pi}
by the step-by-step argument. For more details, see
Appendix

C
.

•

The controlled Euler–Maruyama scheme
\widehat{X}^{\pi}
itself cannot be used for the approximation purpose since it involves the true solution
X
. We will use it just as an auxiliary process.

•

The additional drift term
\frac{\lambda}{\Delta}(X(t)-\widehat{X}^{\pi}(t))\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[0,T\wedge\tau^{\pi}\wedge\zeta)}(t)
in (
5.1
) is regarded as a control process, which plays a role of “dissipation” of the system and is valid until the stopping time
T\wedge\tau^{\pi}\wedge\zeta
. Namely, if
\widehat{X}^{\pi}(t)=X(t)+\kappa v
for some
\kappa\in(0,\Delta)
and
v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
|v|=1
at time
t\in[0,T\wedge\tau^{\pi}\wedge\zeta)
, then the control process attempts to move
\widehat{X}^{\pi}
to the direction
-v
with size
\lambda\times\frac{\kappa}{\Delta}
in the infinitesimal time duration
[t,t+\mathrm{d}t]
. The control parameters
\lambda
,
\Delta
and
\vec{R}=(R_{\mathrm{G},b},R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{C},b},R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{E}})
have the following interpretations:

–

The parameter
\lambda
can be seen as an intensity of the dissipation;

–

The parameter
\Delta
can be seen as a benchmark of the (pathwise) error between
X
and
\widehat{X}^{\pi}
;

–

The vector-parameter
\vec{R}
can be seen as a benchmark of the “locality” of the growth, continuity and ellipticity conditions specified in
Assumption

1
.

Intuitively speaking, the larger the intensity parameter
\lambda
is, the closer the controlled Euler–Maruyama scheme
\widehat{X}^{\pi}
is to the true (weak) solution
X
in the pathwise sense. On the other hand, the smaller the intensity parameter
\lambda
is, the closer
\widehat{X}^{\pi}
is to the original Euler–Maruyama scheme
X^{\pi}
in the sense of probability laws. In order to understand the latter idea, consider the case of
\lambda=0
(at least formally). In this case, the system (
5.1
) is nothing but a continuous time analogue of the Euler–Maruyama scheme (
2.8
) defined on the probability basis
(W,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
.

The important thing is to seek for “optimal control parameters”
\lambda
,
\Delta
and
\vec{R}
which minimize the corrective error term

d_{\mathrm{LP}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T}),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}])\big)+d_{\mathrm{LP}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}])\big).

To do so, the first task is to formulate an appropriate “control problem” by estimating the above two terms separately for each fixed control parameters. An estimate of the first term
d_{\mathrm{LP}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T}),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}])\big)
is given in
Section

5.2
below, where the growth condition (G) and the local Hölder continuity condition (C) in
Assumption

1
are crucial. The second term
d_{\mathrm{LP}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}])\big)
is estimated in
Section

5.3
below, where we utilize the local ellipticity condition (E) in
Assumption

1
to construct a new probability measure
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{\pi}\sim\mathbb{P}
on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F})
under which the controlled Euler–Maruyama scheme
\widehat{X}^{\pi}
is distributed according to the law of the true Euler–Maruyama scheme
X^{\pi}
. Based on these two estimates, in
Section

5.4
below, we tackle the corresponding “control problem” and complete the proof of
Theorem

3.1
.

Notice that, by the definitions of the stopping times
\tau^{\pi}
and
\zeta
,

B_{X(t)}(\Delta)\subset D_{T}(\vec{R})\ \ \text{for any $t\in[0,\zeta)$, and}\ \ \widehat{X}^{\pi}(t)\in B_{X(t)}(\Delta)\ \ \text{for any $t\in[0,\tau^{\pi})$},

where
B_{X(t)}(\Delta)
denotes the open ball in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
with center
X(t)
and radius
\Delta
. From the above observation, together with the facts that
\widehat{X}^{\pi}(0)=X(0)\in\mathrm{supp}\,\mu_{0}

\mathbb{P}
-a.s. and that
D_{T}(\vec{R})
is convex, we see that the following hold
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.:

\begin{split}&(t,X),(\pi(t),\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X])\in\Lambda\big[\mathrm{supp}\,\mu_{0},D_{T}(\vec{R})\big]\ \ \text{for any $t\in[0,T\wedge\zeta)$, and}\\
&\big(t,\widehat{X}^{\pi}\big),\big(\pi(t),\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]\big)\in\Lambda\big[\mathrm{supp}\,\mu_{0},D_{T}(\vec{R})\big]\ \ \text{for any $t\in[0,T\wedge\tau^{\pi}\wedge\zeta)$.}\end{split}

(5.2)

5.2
Error estimate between the weak solution and controlled Euler–Maruyama scheme

First, we show a probabilistic estimate for the pathwise error
\|X_{T}-\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]\|_{\infty}
in terms of the control parameters. To do so, we need the following standard lemma:

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
T\in(0,\infty)
and
\pi=(t_{k})^{m}_{k=0}\in\Pi_{T}
be fixed. Then, for any
x,y\in\mathcal{C}
and
k\in\{0,\dots,m\}
,

\|x_{t_{k}}-\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[x]_{t_{k}}\|_{\infty}\leq\varpi(t_{k},x;|\pi|)

(5.3)

and

\|\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[x]_{t_{k}}-\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[y]_{t_{k}}\|_{\infty}\leq\|x_{t_{k}}-y_{t_{k}}\|_{\infty}.

(5.4)

Proof.

Notice that, for any
z\in\mathcal{C}
,

\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[z]_{t_{k}}(\cdot)=\sum^{k-1}_{j=0}\left\{\frac{t_{j+1}-\cdot}{t_{j+1}-t_{j}}z(t_{j})+\frac{\cdot-t_{j}}{t_{j+1}-t_{j}}z(t_{j+1})\right\}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[t_{j},t_{j+1})}(\cdot)+z(t_{k})\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[t_{k},\infty)}(\cdot).

On the one hand,

\displaystyle\|x_{t_{k}}-\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[x]_{t_{k}}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\max_{j\in\{0,\dots,k-1\}}\sup_{s\in[t_{j},t_{j+1})}\left|\frac{t_{j+1}-s}{t_{j+1}-t_{j}}\big(x(s)-x(t_{j})\big)+\frac{s-t_{j}}{t_{j+1}-t_{j}}\big(x(s)-x(t_{j+1})\big)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\max_{j\in\{0,\dots,k-1\}}\sup_{s\in[t_{j},t_{j+1})}\left\{\frac{t_{j+1}-s}{t_{j+1}-t_{j}}\big|x(s)-x(t_{j})\big|+\frac{s-t_{j}}{t_{j+1}-t_{j}}\big|x(s)-x(t_{j+1})\big|\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\max_{j\in\{0,\dots,k-1\}}\varpi(t_{j+1},x;t_{j+1}-t_{j})

\displaystyle\leq\varpi(t_{k},x;|\pi|).

Hence, (
5.3
) holds. On the other hand,

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[x]_{t_{k}}-\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[y]_{t_{k}}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\max_{j\in\{0,\dots,k-1\}}\sup_{s\in[t_{j},t_{j+1})}\left|\frac{t_{j+1}-s}{t_{j+1}-t_{j}}\big(x(t_{j})-y(t_{j})\big)+\frac{s-t_{j}}{t_{j+1}-t_{j}}\big(x(t_{j+1})-y(t_{j+1})\big)\right|

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\vee|x(t_{k})-y(t_{k})|

\displaystyle\leq\max_{j\in\{0,\dots,k-1\}}\sup_{s\in[t_{j},t_{j+1})}\left\{\frac{t_{j+1}-s}{t_{j+1}-t_{j}}\big|x(t_{j})-y(t_{j})\big|+\frac{s-t_{j}}{t_{j+1}-t_{j}}\big|x(t_{j+1})-y(t_{j+1})\big|\right\}

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\vee|x(t_{k})-y(t_{k})|

\displaystyle\leq\max_{j\in\{0,\dots,k\}}|x(t_{j})-y(t_{j})|

\displaystyle\leq\|x_{t_{k}}-y_{t_{k}}\|_{\infty}.

Hence, (
5.4
) holds.
∎

Using the above standard lemma and fundamental results in stochastic calculus shown in
Appendix

D
, we provide the following estimate between the weak solution and controlled Euler–Maruyama scheme. Here, the growth condition (G) and the local Hölder continuity condition (C) in
Assumption

1
are essential.

Proposition 5.3
.

Fix data
(D,\mu_{0},b,\sigma)
satisfying
Assumption

1
. Suppose that we are given a weak solution
(X,W,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
of the SFDE (
1.1
) associated with
(D,\mu_{0},b,\sigma)
. Fix
T\in(0,\infty)
,
\pi\in\Pi_{T}
,
\lambda\in(0,\infty)
,
\Delta\in(0,1]
and
\vec{R}=(R_{\mathrm{G},b},R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{C},b},R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{E}})\in[1,\infty)^{5}
, and assume that the parameters satisfy the constraint

\lambda\geq 2^{10}n\max\left\{K_{\mathrm{C},b;T}R_{\mathrm{C},b}\mathcal{E}^{\alpha_{b}},\frac{\left(K_{\mathrm{C},\sigma;T}R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}\mathcal{E}^{\alpha_{\sigma}}\right)^{2}}{\Delta}\left(\log\frac{1}{\Delta}+\log\left(1\vee\frac{2T\lambda}{\Delta}\right)\right)\right\},

(5.5)

where
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}(|\pi|,\Delta,R_{\mathrm{G},b},R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma})\in(0,\infty)
is defined by (
3.4
). Let
\widehat{X}^{\pi}
be the controlled Euler–Maruyama scheme, given by (
5.1
), associated with
(X,W,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
and
(T,\pi,\lambda,\Delta,\vec{R})
. Then, it holds that

\begin{split}\mathbb{P}\Big(\|X_{T}-\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]\|_{\infty}\geq 6\sqrt{n}\mathcal{E}\Big)&\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{t\in[0,T]}\mathrm{dist}\!\left(X(t),\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus D_{T}(\vec{R})\right)\leq\Delta\right)+4n\frac{|\pi|}{T}+219\Delta.\end{split}

(5.6)

In particular, it holds that

\begin{split}d_{\mathrm{LP}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T}),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}])\big)&\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{t\in[0,T]}\mathrm{dist}\!\left(X(t),\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus D_{T}(\vec{R})\right)\leq\Delta\right)+\left(\frac{4n}{T}+219+6\sqrt{n}\right)\mathcal{E}.\end{split}

(5.7)

Proof.

The estimate (
5.7
) follows from (
5.6
). Indeed, by the fundamental inequality (
2.3
) for the Lévy–Prokhorov metric, we have

d_{\mathrm{LP}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T}),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}])\big)\leq\inf\left\{\varepsilon>0\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\mathbb{P}\big(\|X_{T}-\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]\|_{\infty}>\varepsilon\big)<\varepsilon\right\}.

Then, the estimate (
5.6
) and the definition (
3.4
) of
\mathcal{E}
imply that the number

\varepsilon=\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{t\in[0,T]}\mathrm{dist}\!\left(X(t),\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus D_{T}(\vec{R})\right)\leq\Delta\right)+\left(\frac{4n}{T}+219+6\sqrt{n}\right)\mathcal{E}

belongs to the set over which the infimum is taken, proving (
5.7
). In the following, we show that the estimate (
5.6
) holds under the constraint (
5.5
) on the parameters. Without loss of generality, we may assume that
\log\frac{T}{|\pi|}\geq 1
and
\log\frac{1}{\Delta}\geq 1
; otherwise
4n\frac{|\pi|}{T}+219\Delta>1
, and hence the estimate (
5.6
) becomes trivial.

Define a stopping time
\eta^{\pi}
by

\eta^{\pi}:=\inf\left\{t\geq 0\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\varpi\big(t,X;|\pi|\big)\geq K_{\mathrm{G},b;T}R_{\mathrm{G},b}|\pi|+4K_{\mathrm{G},\sigma;T}R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}\sqrt{n|\pi|\log\frac{T}{|\pi|}}\right\}.

Then, by the definitions of the stopping times
\tau^{\pi}
and
\eta^{\pi}
,

\displaystyle\Big\{T<\eta^{\pi}\wedge\tau^{\pi}\Big\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\varpi\big(T,X;|\pi|\big)<K_{\mathrm{G},b;T}R_{\mathrm{G},b}|\pi|+4K_{\mathrm{G},\sigma;T}R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}\sqrt{n|\pi|\log\frac{T}{|\pi|}}\right\}\cap\Big\{\|X_{T}-\widehat{X}^{\pi}_{T}\|_{\infty}<\Delta\Big\}.

Furthermore, by
Lemma

5.2
, we have

\displaystyle\|X_{T}-\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\|X_{T}-\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X]\|_{\infty}+\|\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X]-\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\varpi\big(T,X;|\pi|\big)+\|X_{T}-\widehat{X}^{\pi}_{T}\|_{\infty}.

Therefore, noting the definition (
3.4
) of
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}(|\pi|,\Delta,R_{\mathrm{G},b},R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma})\in(0,\infty)
, we obtain

\displaystyle\Big\{T<\eta^{\pi}\wedge\tau^{\pi}\Big\}

\displaystyle\subset\left\{\|X_{T}-\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]\|_{\infty}<K_{\mathrm{G},b;T}R_{\mathrm{G},b}|\pi|+4K_{\mathrm{G},\sigma;T}R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}\sqrt{n|\pi|\log\frac{T}{|\pi|}}+\Delta\right\}

\displaystyle\subset\Big\{\|X_{T}-\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]\|_{\infty}<6\sqrt{n}\mathcal{E}\Big\}.

Hence,

\displaystyle\Big\{\|X_{T}-\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]\|_{\infty}\geq 6\sqrt{n}\mathcal{E}\Big\}

\displaystyle\subset\Big\{\eta^{\pi}\wedge\tau^{\pi}\leq T\Big\}\subset\Big\{\zeta\leq T\Big\}\cup\Big\{\eta^{\pi}\leq T\wedge\zeta\Big\}\cup\Big\{\tau^{\pi}\leq T\wedge\zeta\wedge\eta^{\pi}\Big\},

which implies that

\mathbb{P}\Big(\|X_{T}-\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]\|_{\infty}\geq 6\sqrt{n}\mathcal{E}\Big)\leq P_{1}+P_{2}+P_{3},

(5.8)

where

P_{1}:=\mathbb{P}\Big(\zeta\leq T\Big),\ \ P_{2}:=\mathbb{P}\Big(\eta^{\pi}\leq T\wedge\zeta\Big),\ \ \text{and}\ \ P_{3}:=\mathbb{P}\Big(\tau^{\pi}\leq T\wedge\zeta\wedge\eta^{\pi}\Big).

We estimate the three terms in the right-hand side of (
5.8
).

Step 1. Estimate of
P_{1}
.
By the definition of the stopping time
\zeta
, we have

P_{1}=\mathbb{P}\Big(\zeta\leq T\Big)=\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{t\in[0,T]}\mathrm{dist}\!\left(X(t),\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus D_{T}(\vec{R})\right)\leq\Delta\right).

(5.9)

Step 2. Estimate of
P_{2}
.
By the definition of the stopping time
\eta^{\pi}
, we have

\displaystyle P_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\Big(\eta^{\pi}\leq T\wedge\zeta\Big)=\mathbb{P}\left(\varpi\big(T\wedge\zeta,X;|\pi|\big)\geq K_{\mathrm{G},b;T}R_{\mathrm{G},b}|\pi|+4K_{\mathrm{G},\sigma;T}R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}\sqrt{n|\pi|\log\frac{T}{|\pi|}}\right).

(5.10)

Observe that

\varpi\big(T\wedge\zeta,X;|\pi|\big)\leq\varpi(T,V;|\pi|)+\varpi(T,M;|\pi|),

(5.11)

where

V(t):=\int^{t}_{0}b(s,X)\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[0,T\wedge\zeta)}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s,\ \ M(t):=\int^{t}_{0}\sigma(s,X)\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[0,T\wedge\zeta)}(s)\,\mathrm{d}W(s),\ \ t\in[0,\infty).

By (
5.2
), we see that
(s,X)\in\Lambda[\mathrm{supp}\,\mu_{0},D_{\mathrm{G},b;T}(R_{\mathrm{G},b})]\cap\Lambda[\mathrm{supp}\,\mu_{0},D_{\mathrm{G},\sigma;T}(R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma})]
for any
s\in[0,T\wedge\zeta)

\mathbb{P}
-a.s. Thus, by condition (G) in
Assumption

1
, we have

|b(s,X)|\leq K_{\mathrm{G},b;T}R_{\mathrm{G},b}\ \ \text{and}\ \ |\sigma(s,X)|\leq K_{\mathrm{G},\sigma;T}R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}\ \ \text{for any $s\in[0,T\wedge\zeta)$ $\mathbb{P}$-a.s.},

which implies that

\varpi(T,V;|\pi|)\leq K_{\mathrm{G},b;T}R_{\mathrm{G},b}|\pi|\ \ \text{and}\ \ \max_{i\in\{1,\dots,n\}}\varpi(T,\langle M^{i}\rangle;|\pi|)\leq(K_{\mathrm{G},\sigma;T}R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma})^{2}|\pi|\ \ \text{$\mathbb{P}$-a.s.}

(5.12)

Hence, by (
5.10
), (
5.11
) and the first estimate in (
5.12
), we have

P_{2}\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\varpi(T,M;|\pi|)\geq 4K_{\mathrm{G},\sigma;T}R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}\sqrt{n|\pi|\log\frac{T}{|\pi|}}\right).

Then, noting the second estimate in (
5.12
), applying
Lemma

D.1
to the
n
-dimensional continuous local
\mathbb{P}
-martingale
M
and constants
\delta=|\pi|
,
\kappa=(K_{\mathrm{G},\sigma;T}R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma})^{2}|\pi|
and
\theta=\sqrt{\log\frac{T}{|\pi|}}\geq 1
, we obtain

P_{2}\leq 4n\frac{|\pi|}{T}.

(5.13)

Step 3. Estimate of
P_{3}
.
By the definition of the stopping time
\tau^{\pi}
, we have

P_{3}=\mathbb{P}\Big(\tau^{\pi}\leq T\wedge\zeta\wedge\eta^{\pi}\Big)=\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{t\in[0,T\wedge\tau^{\pi}\wedge\zeta\wedge\eta^{\pi}]}\big|X(t)-\widehat{X}^{\pi}(t)\big|\geq\Delta\right).

(5.14)

We will apply
Lemma

D.2
to the nonnegative Itô process
Z(t)=|X(t)-\widehat{X}^{\pi}(t)|^{2}
and nonnegative random variable
\varsigma=T\wedge\tau^{\pi}\wedge\zeta\wedge\eta^{\pi}
, together with constants
\kappa
,
A
,
B
and
\theta
to be determined in the following observation. By using Itô’s formula, we have

\big|X(t)-\widehat{X}^{\pi}(t)\big|^{2}=\int^{t}_{0}v^{\pi}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s+N^{\pi}(t),\ \ t\in[0,\infty),

(5.15)

where, for each
t\in[0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle v^{\pi}(t)

\displaystyle:=-\frac{2\lambda}{\Delta}\big|X(t)-\widehat{X}^{\pi}(t)\big|^{2}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[0,T\wedge\tau^{\pi}\wedge\zeta)}(t)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+2\big\langle X(t)-\widehat{X}^{\pi}(t),b(t,X)-b\big(\pi(t),\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]\big)\big\rangle+\big|\sigma(t,X)-\sigma\big(\pi(t),\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]\big)\big|^{2}

and

N^{\pi}(t):=2\int^{t}_{0}\Big\langle X(s)-\widehat{X}^{\pi}(s),\Big(\sigma(s,X)-\sigma\big(\pi(s),\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]\big)\Big)\,\mathrm{d}W(s)\Big\rangle.

For each
\varphi\in\{b,\sigma\}
and
t\in[0,\infty)
, define

v^{\pi}_{\varphi}(t):=\big|\varphi(t,X)-\varphi\big(\pi(t),\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]\big)\big|.

By the definition of the stopping time
\tau^{\pi}
, we have

v^{\pi}(t)\leq-\frac{2\lambda}{\Delta}\big|X(t)-\widehat{X}^{\pi}(t)\big|^{2}+2\Delta v^{\pi}_{b}(t)+v^{\pi}_{\sigma}(t)^{2},\ \ t\in[0,T\wedge\tau^{\pi}\wedge\zeta).

(5.16)

Also, notice that
N^{\pi}
is a one-dimensional continuous local martingale on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
with the quadratic variation satisfying

\frac{\mathrm{d}\langle N^{\pi}\rangle(t)}{\mathrm{d}t}\leq 4\big|X(t)-\widehat{X}^{\pi}(t)\big|^{2}\,\big|\sigma(t,X)-\sigma\big(\pi(t),\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]\big)\big|^{2}\leq 4\Delta^{2}v^{\pi}_{\sigma}(t)^{2},\ \ t\in[0,T\wedge\tau^{\pi}).

(5.17)

Now we estimate the terms
v^{\pi}_{b}(t)
and
v^{\pi}_{\sigma}(t)
for
t\in[0,T\wedge\tau^{\pi}\wedge\zeta\wedge\eta^{\pi})
. Notice that, by (
5.2
),

\displaystyle(t,X),\,\big(\pi(t),\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X]\big),\,\big(\pi(t),\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]\big)\in\Lambda\big[\mathrm{supp}\,\mu_{0},D_{\mathrm{C},\varphi;T}(R_{\mathrm{C},\varphi})\big]\ \text{for any $t\in[0,T\wedge\tau^{\pi}\wedge\zeta)$ $\mathbb{P}$-a.s.},\ \ \varphi\in\{b,\sigma\}.

Hence, thanks to condition (C) in
Assumption

1
, we have

\begin{split}v^{\pi}_{\varphi}(t)&\leq\big|\varphi(t,X)-\varphi\big(\pi(t),X\big)\big|+\big|\varphi\big(\pi(t),X\big)-\varphi\big(\pi(t),\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X]\big)\big|+\big|\varphi\big(\pi(t),\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X]\big)-\varphi\big(\pi(t),\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]\big)\big|\\
&\leq K_{\mathrm{C},\varphi;T}R_{\mathrm{C},\varphi}\big\{V^{\pi}_{1}(t)^{\alpha_{\varphi}}+V^{\pi}_{2}(t)^{\alpha_{\varphi}}+V^{\pi}_{3}(t)^{\alpha_{\varphi}}\big\}\ \text{for any $t\in[0,T\wedge\tau^{\pi}\wedge\zeta\wedge\eta^{\pi})$ $\mathbb{P}$-a.s.},\ \ \varphi\in\{b,\sigma\},\end{split}

(5.18)

where

\displaystyle V^{\pi}_{1}(t)

\displaystyle:=\Big\{\varpi\big(t,X;t-\pi(t)\big)+\big(t-\pi(t)\big)^{1/2}\Big\}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[0,T\wedge\eta^{\pi})}(t),

\displaystyle V^{\pi}_{2}(t)

\displaystyle:=\big\|X_{\pi(t)}-\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X]_{\pi(t)}\big\|_{\infty}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[0,\eta^{\pi})}(t),

\displaystyle V^{\pi}_{3}(t)

\displaystyle:=\big\|\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X]_{\pi(t)}-\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]_{\pi(t)}\big\|_{\infty}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[0,\tau^{\pi})}(t).

As for
V^{\pi}_{1}
, we have

\displaystyle V^{\pi}_{1}(t)

\displaystyle\leq\Big\{\varpi\big(t,X;|\pi|\big)+\sqrt{|\pi|}\Big\}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[0,\eta^{\pi})}(t)

\displaystyle\leq K_{\mathrm{G},b;T}R_{\mathrm{G},b}|\pi|+4K_{\mathrm{G},\sigma;T}R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}\sqrt{n|\pi|\log\frac{T}{|\pi|}}+\sqrt{|\pi|}

\displaystyle\leq 6\sqrt{n}\mathcal{E},

(5.19)

where we used the fact that
0\leq t-\pi(t)\leq|\pi|
for any
t\in[0,T]
in the first inequality, the definition of
\eta^{\pi}
in the second inequality, and the definition (
3.4
) of
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}(|\pi|,\Delta,R_{\mathrm{G},b},R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma})
together with the assumptions that
\log\frac{T}{|\pi|}\geq 1
(imposed in this proof) and
K_{\mathrm{G},\sigma;T}R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}\geq 1
in the third inequality. Similarly, as for
V^{\pi}_{2}
, by using (
5.3
) in
Lemma

5.2
and noting the definition of the stopping time
\eta^{\pi}
, we have

\displaystyle V^{\pi}_{2}(t)

\displaystyle\leq\varpi(\pi(t),X;|\pi|)\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[0,\eta^{\pi})}(t)

\displaystyle\leq K_{\mathrm{G},b;T}R_{\mathrm{G},b}|\pi|+4K_{\mathrm{G},\sigma;T}R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}\sqrt{n|\pi|\log\frac{T}{|\pi|}}

\displaystyle\leq 5\sqrt{n}\mathcal{E}.

(5.20)

As for
V^{\pi}_{3}
, by using (
5.4
) in
Lemma

5.2
and noting the definition of the stopping time
\tau^{\pi}
, we have

V^{\pi}_{3}(t)\leq\|X_{\pi(t)}-\widehat{X}^{\pi}_{\pi(t)}\|_{\infty}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[0,\tau^{\pi})}(t)\leq\Delta\leq\mathcal{E}.

(5.21)

By (
5.18
), (
5.19
), (
5.20
) and (
5.21
), we get

v^{\pi}_{\varphi}(t)\leq 12\sqrt{n}K_{\mathrm{C},\varphi;T}R_{\mathrm{C},\varphi}\mathcal{E}^{\alpha_{\varphi}}\ \ \text{for any $t\in[0,T\wedge\tau^{\pi}\wedge\zeta\wedge\eta^{\pi})$ $\mathbb{P}$-a.s.},\ \ \varphi\in\{b,\sigma\}.

(5.22)

By (
5.16
) and (
5.22
), we obtain

\begin{split}&v^{\pi}(t)\leq-\frac{2\lambda}{\Delta}\big|X(t)-\widehat{X}^{\pi}(t)\big|^{2}+24\sqrt{n}K_{\mathrm{C},b;T}R_{\mathrm{C},b}\Delta\mathcal{E}^{\alpha_{b}}+144n\big(K_{\mathrm{C},\sigma;T}R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}\mathcal{E}^{\alpha_{\sigma}}\big)^{2}\\
&\hskip 85.35826pt\text{for any $t\in[0,T\wedge\tau^{\pi}\wedge\zeta\wedge\eta^{\pi})$ $\mathbb{P}$-a.s.}\end{split}

(5.23)

Also, by (
5.17
) and (
5.22
) with
\varphi=\sigma
, we obtain

\frac{\mathrm{d}\langle N^{\pi}\rangle(t)}{\mathrm{d}t}\leq 576n\left(K_{\mathrm{C},\sigma;T}R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}\Delta\mathcal{E}^{\alpha_{\sigma}}\right)^{2}\ \ \text{for any $t\in[0,T\wedge\tau^{\pi}\wedge\zeta\wedge\eta^{\pi})$ $\mathbb{P}$-a.s.}

(5.24)

Now we apply
Lemma

D.2
to the nonnegative Itô process

Z(t)=|X(t)-\widehat{X}^{\pi}(t)|^{2},

(5.25)

nonnegative random variable

\varsigma=T\wedge\tau^{\pi}\wedge\zeta\wedge\eta^{\pi},

(5.26)

and constants

\begin{split}&\kappa=\frac{2\lambda}{\Delta},\ \ A=24\sqrt{n}K_{\mathrm{C},b;T}R_{\mathrm{C},b}\Delta\mathcal{E}^{\alpha_{b}}+144n\big(K_{\mathrm{C},\sigma;T}R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}\mathcal{E}^{\alpha_{\sigma}}\big)^{2},\\
&B=576n\left(K_{\mathrm{C},\sigma;T}R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}\Delta\mathcal{E}^{\alpha_{\sigma}}\right)^{2},\end{split}

(5.27)

and

\theta=\sqrt{\log\frac{1}{\Delta}}.

(5.28)

By (
5.15
), (
5.23
) and (
5.24
), we see that
Z
,
\varsigma
,
\kappa
,
A
and
B
specified in (
5.25
), (
5.26
) and (
5.27
) satisfy

\mathrm{d}Z(t)=v^{\pi}(t)\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}N^{\pi}(t),\ \ t\in[0,\infty),\ \ Z(0)=0,

and

v^{\pi}(t)\leq-\kappa Z(t)+A,\ \ \frac{\mathrm{d}\langle N^{\pi}\rangle(t)}{\mathrm{d}t}\leq B,\ \ \text{for any $t\in[0,\varsigma)$ $\mathbb{P}$-a.s.}

Furthermore, under the constraint (
5.5
) on the parameters and the assumption that
\log\frac{1}{\Delta}\geq 1
imposed in this proof, we see that the constants
\kappa,A,B
and
\theta
specified in (
5.27
) and (
5.28
) satisfy

\frac{A}{\kappa}+\sqrt{\frac{2B\big(\theta^{2}+\log(1\vee(T\kappa))\big)}{\kappa}}\leq\Delta^{2}.

Hence, by (
5.14
) and
Lemma

D.2
, we have

\displaystyle P_{3}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{t\in[0,\varsigma]}Z(t)\geq\Delta^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{t\in[0,\varsigma]}Z(t)\geq\frac{A}{\kappa}+\sqrt{\frac{2B\big(\theta^{2}+\log(1\vee(T\kappa))\big)}{\kappa}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 219\exp\left(-\theta^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=219\Delta.

(5.29)

By (
5.8
), (
5.9
), (
5.13
) and (
5.29
), we get the estimate (
5.6
). This completes the proof.
∎

5.3
Error estimate between the controlled and true Euler–Maruyama schemes

Next, we provide an estimate for the error-in-law between the controlled Euler–Maruyama scheme
\widehat{X}^{\pi}
and the true Euler–Maruyama scheme
X^{\pi}
. Before that, we recall some standard facts on several metrics on the space of probability measures. The total variation distance
d_{\mathrm{TV}}(\mu,\nu)
between two probability measures
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}(S)
on a measurable space
(S,\mathcal{S})
is defined by

d_{\mathrm{TV}}(\mu,\nu):=\sup_{A\in\mathcal{S}}|\mu(A)-\nu(A)|.

Also, for any
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}(S)
such that
\mu\ll\nu
, the so-called Kullback–Leibler divergence of
\mu
from
\nu
is defined by

D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mu\|\nu):=\int_{S}\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu}{\mathrm{d}\nu}\,\mathrm{d}\mu.

The following are fundamental facts:

•

Let
(S,d_{S})
be a complete separable metric space and
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{B}(S)
be the corresponding Borel
\sigma
-algebra. Observe that the number
\varepsilon=\sup_{A\in\mathcal{S}}|\mu(A)-\nu(A)|
belongs to the set over which the infimum in the definition (
2.1
) of the Lévy–Prokhorov metric is taken, and hence we have
d_{\mathrm{LP}}(\mu,\nu)\leq d_{\mathrm{TV}}(\mu,\nu)
for any
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}(S)
.

•

It is clear from the definition that

d_{\mathrm{TV},T}(\mu\circ f^{-1},\nu\circ f^{-1})\leq d_{\mathrm{TV},S}(\mu,\nu)

for any
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}(S)
and any measurable map
f
from a measurable space
(S,\mathcal{S})
to another measurable space
(T,\mathcal{T})
, where
d_{\mathrm{TV},S}
and
d_{\mathrm{TV},T}
denote the total variation distances on
(S,\mathcal{S})
and
(T,\mathcal{T})
, respectively.

•

The well-known Pinsker’s inequality (cf.
[
73
, Lemma 2.5 (i)]
) shows that

d_{\mathrm{TV}}(\mu,\nu)\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mu\|\nu)}

for any
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}(S)
such that
\mu\ll\nu
.

Now we provide an estimate for the error between the controlled and true Euler–Maruyama schemes. Here, the local ellipticity condition (E) in
Assumption

1
is crucial.

Proposition 5.4
.

Fix data
(D,\mu_{0},b,\sigma)
satisfying
Assumption

1
. Suppose that we are given a weak solution
(X,W,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
of the SFDE (
1.1
) associated with
(D,\mu_{0},b,\sigma)
. Fix
T\in(0,\infty)
,
\pi\in\Pi_{T}
,
\lambda\in(0,\infty)
,
\Delta\in(0,1]
and
\vec{R}=(R_{\mathrm{G},b},R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{C},b},R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{E}})\in[1,\infty)^{5}
. Let
X^{\pi}
be the Euler–Maruyama scheme defined on
(\Omega^{\pi},\mathcal{F}^{\pi},\mathbb{P}^{\pi})
and given by (
2.8
) with initial distribution
\mu_{0}
. Let
\widehat{X}^{\pi}
be the controlled Euler–Maruyama scheme, given by (
5.1
), associated with
(X,W,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
and
(T,\pi,\lambda,\Delta,\vec{R})
. Then, there exists a probability measure
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{\pi}\sim\mathbb{P}
on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_{T})
such that

\mathrm{Law}_{\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}])=\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}])

(5.30)

and

D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{\pi}\|\mathbb{P})\leq\frac{T}{2}\big(K_{\mathrm{E};T}R_{\mathrm{E}}\lambda\big)^{2}.

(5.31)

In particular, it holds that

d_{\mathrm{LP}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}])\big)\leq\frac{\sqrt{T}}{2}K_{\mathrm{E};T}R_{\mathrm{E}}\lambda.

(5.32)

Proof.

The inequality (
5.32
) follows from (
5.30
) and (
5.31
). Indeed,

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{LP}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}])\big)

\displaystyle=d_{\mathrm{LP}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]),\mathrm{Law}_{\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}])\big)

\displaystyle\leq d_{\mathrm{TV}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]),\mathrm{Law}_{\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}])\big)

\displaystyle\leq d_{\mathrm{TV}}\big(\mathbb{P},\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{\pi}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}D_{\mathrm{KL}}\big(\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{\pi}\|\mathbb{P}\big)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sqrt{T}}{2}K_{\mathrm{E};T}R_{\mathrm{E}}\lambda,

where the first equality follows from (
5.30
), and the last inequality follows from (
5.31
); the second, third and fourth inequalities follow from the fundamental properties of metrics on the space of probability measures summarized above. In the following, we construct a probability measure
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{\pi}\sim\mathbb{P}
on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_{T})
such that (
5.30
) and (
5.31
) hold.

By (
5.2
), we see that
(\pi(t),\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}])\in\Lambda[\mathrm{supp}\,\mu_{0},D_{\mathrm{E};T}(R_{\mathrm{E}})]
for any
t\in[0,T\wedge\tau^{\pi}\wedge\zeta)
outside a
\mathbb{P}
-null set
N\in\mathcal{F}
. Thus, by condition (E) in
Assumption

1
, we have

\big\langle\sigma\big(\pi(t),\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]\big)\sigma\big(\pi(t),\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]\big)^{\top}\xi,\xi\big\rangle\geq\frac{|\xi|^{2}}{K_{\mathrm{E};T}^{2}R_{\mathrm{E}}^{2}}

for any
t\in[0,T\wedge\tau^{\pi}\wedge\zeta)
and
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
on
\Omega\setminus N
. In particular, on
\Omega\setminus N
, for any
t\in[0,T\wedge\tau^{\pi}\wedge\zeta)
, we can define the pseudo-inverse
\sigma\big(\pi(t),\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]\big)^{\dagger}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times n}
of the matrix
\sigma\big(\pi(t),\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]\big)\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, that is,

\sigma\big(\pi(t),\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]\big)^{\dagger}:=\sigma\big(\pi(t),\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]\big)^{\top}\Big(\sigma\big(\pi(t),\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]\big)\sigma\big(\pi(t),\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]\big)^{\top}\Big)^{-1}.

Notice that

\sigma\big(\pi(t),\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]\big)\sigma\big(\pi(t),\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]\big)^{\dagger}=I_{n\times n},\ \ \text{for $t\in[0,T\wedge\tau^{\pi}\wedge\zeta)$ on $\Omega\setminus N$},

(5.33)

and

\|\sigma\big(\pi(t),\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]\big)^{\dagger}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq K_{\mathrm{E};T}R_{\mathrm{E}}\ \ \text{for $t\in[0,T\wedge\tau^{\pi}\wedge\zeta)$ on $\Omega\setminus N$},

(5.34)

where
\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{op}}
denotes the operator norm. Define an
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued
\mathbb{F}
-progressively measurable process
u^{\pi}=(u^{\pi}(t))_{t\in[0,T]}
on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_{T})
by

u^{\pi}(t):=\frac{\lambda}{\Delta}\sigma\big(\pi(t),\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]\big)^{\dagger}\big(X(t)-\widehat{X}^{\pi}(t)\big)\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[0,T\wedge\tau^{\pi}\wedge\zeta)}(t)\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{\Omega\setminus N},\ \ t\in[0,T].

Then, by (
5.34
) and the definition of the stopping time
\tau^{\pi}
, we have

\displaystyle\int^{T}_{0}\big|u^{\pi}(t)\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\lambda^{2}}{\Delta^{2}}\int^{T\wedge\tau^{\pi}\wedge\zeta}_{0}\big\|\sigma\big(\pi(t),\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]\big)^{\dagger}\big\|^{2}_{\mathrm{op}}\,\big|X(t)-\widehat{X}^{\pi}(t)\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\big(K_{\mathrm{E};T}R_{\mathrm{E}}\lambda\big)^{2}}{\Delta^{2}}\int^{T\wedge\tau^{\pi}}_{0}\big|X(t)-\widehat{X}^{\pi}(t)\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq T\big(K_{\mathrm{E};T}R_{\mathrm{E}}\lambda\big)^{2}\ \ \text{$\mathbb{P}$-a.s.}

(5.35)

Now we define a new measure
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{\pi}\sim\mathbb{P}
on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_{T})
by

\frac{\mathrm{d}\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{\pi}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}:=\exp\Big(-\int^{T}_{0}\big\langle u^{\pi}(t),\mathrm{d}W(t)\big\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\int^{T}_{0}\big|u^{\pi}(t)\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\Big).

By (
5.35
), Novikov’s condition is satisfied, and hence
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{\pi}
is a probability measure on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_{T})
. We show that
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{\pi}
satisfies (
5.30
) and (
5.31
).

By Girsanov’s theorem, the process

\widehat{W}^{\pi}(t):=W(t)+\int^{t}_{0}u^{\pi}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s,\ \ t\in[0,T],

is a
d
-dimensional Brownian motion on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_{T},\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{\pi})
relative to
\mathbb{F}
. Furthermore, by (
5.33
) and the definition of
u^{\pi}
, we see that
\widehat{X}^{\pi}
solves the following SFDE
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{\pi}
-a.s.:

\begin{cases}\mathrm{d}\widehat{X}^{\pi}(t)=b\big(\pi(t),\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]\big)\,\mathrm{d}t+\sigma\big(\pi(t),\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]\big)\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{W}^{\pi}(t),\ \ t\in[0,T],\\
\widehat{X}^{\pi}(0)=X(0).\end{cases}

In particular, denoting
\pi=(t_{k})^{m}_{k=0}
,

\begin{cases}\widehat{X}^{\pi}(t_{k+1})=\widehat{X}^{\pi}(t_{k})+b\big(t_{k},\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]\big)(t_{k+1}-t_{k})+\sigma\big(t_{k},\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]\big)\widehat{Z}^{\pi}_{k},\\
\hskip 199.16928ptk\in\{0,\dots,m-1\},\\
\widehat{X}^{\pi}(t_{0})=X(0),\end{cases}

where
\widehat{Z}^{\pi}_{k}:=\widehat{W}^{\pi}(t_{k+1})-\widehat{W}^{\pi}(t_{k})
for each
k\in\{0,\dots,m-1\}
.
Therefore,
(\widehat{X}^{\pi}(t_{k}))^{m}_{k=0}
satisfies (
2.8
) on the probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_{T},\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{\pi})
with
(\xi^{\pi},Z^{\pi}_{0},\dots,Z^{\pi}_{m-1})
replaced by
(X(0),\widehat{Z}^{\pi}_{0},\dots,\widehat{Z}^{\pi}_{m-1})
. Thus, we have
(\widehat{X}^{\pi}(t_{k}))^{m}_{k=0}=\Psi^{\pi}(X(0),\widehat{Z}^{\pi}_{0},\dots,\widehat{Z}^{\pi}_{m-1})
, where
\Psi^{\pi}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{m}\to(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{m+1}
is the measurable map appearing in the argument just after (
2.8
). Notice that
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{\pi}\circ X(0)^{-1}=\mathbb{P}\circ X(0)^{-1}=\mu_{0}
,
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{\pi}\circ(\widehat{Z}^{\pi}_{k})^{-1}=N(0,(t_{k+1}-t_{k})I_{d\times d})
for each
k\in\{0,\dots,m-1\}
, and the random variables
X(0),\widehat{Z}^{\pi}_{0},\dots,\widehat{Z}^{\pi}_{m-1}
are independent under
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{\pi}
. On the other hand, recall that
X^{\pi}=(X^{\pi}(t_{k}))^{m}_{k=0}=\Psi^{\pi}(\xi^{\pi},Z^{\pi}_{0},\dots,Z^{\pi}_{m-1})
,
\mathbb{P}^{\pi}\circ(\xi^{\pi})^{-1}=\mu_{0}
by the assumption,
\mathbb{P}^{\pi}\circ(Z^{\pi}_{k})^{-1}=N(0,(t_{k+1}-t_{k})I_{d\times d})
for each
k\in\{0,\dots,m-1\}
, and the random variables
\xi^{\pi},Z^{\pi}_{0},\dots,Z^{\pi}_{m-1}
are independent under
\mathbb{P}^{\pi}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Law}_{\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}])

\displaystyle=\left(\mu_{0}\otimes\bigotimes^{m-1}_{k=0}N\left(0,(t_{k+1}-t_{k})I_{d\times d}\right)\right)\circ(\Psi^{\pi})^{-1}\circ(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi})^{-1}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}]),

and hence the equality (
5.30
) holds. Furthermore, denoting by
\mathbb{E}_{\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{\pi}}[\cdot]
the expectation operator on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_{T},\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{\pi})
, the estimate (
5.35
) and the fact that
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{\pi}\sim\mathbb{P}
yield

\displaystyle D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{\pi}\|\mathbb{P})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{\pi}}\Big[-\int^{T}_{0}\big\langle u^{\pi}(t),\mathrm{d}W(t)\big\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\int^{T}_{0}\big|u^{\pi}(t)\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{\pi}}\Big[-\int^{T}_{0}\big\langle u^{\pi}(t),\mathrm{d}\widehat{W}^{\pi}(t)\big\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\int^{T}_{0}\big|u^{\pi}(t)\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\Big]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{\pi}}\Big[\int^{T}_{0}\big|u^{\pi}(t)\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{T}{2}\big(K_{\mathrm{E};T}R_{\mathrm{E}}\lambda\big)^{2}.

Thus, we obtain the desired estimate (
5.31
). This completes the proof.
∎

5.4
Optimal control and completion of the proof of the main result

Fix data
(D,\mu_{0},b,\sigma)
satisfying
Assumption

1
. Suppose that we are given a weak solution
(X,W,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
of the SFDE (
1.1
) associated with
(D,\mu_{0},b,\sigma)
. For each
T\in(0,\infty)
and
\pi\in\Pi_{T}
, let
X^{\pi}
be the Euler–Maruyama scheme defined on
(\Omega^{\pi},\mathcal{F}^{\pi},\mathbb{P}^{\pi})
and given by (
2.8
) with initial distribution
\mu_{0}
. For each
T\in(0,\infty)
,
\pi\in\Pi_{T}
,
\lambda\in(0,\infty)
,
\Delta\in(0,1]
and
\vec{R}=(R_{\mathrm{G},b},R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{C},b},R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{E}})\in[1,\infty)^{5}
, let
\widehat{X}^{\pi}
be the controlled Euler–Maruyama scheme, given by (
5.1
), associated with
(X,W,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
and
(T,\pi,\lambda,\Delta,\vec{R})
. By the triangle inequality, we have

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{LP}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T}),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}])\big)

\displaystyle\leq d_{\mathrm{LP}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T}),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}])\big)+d_{\mathrm{LP}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[\widehat{X}^{\pi}]),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}])\big).

Thus,
Proposition

5.3
and
Proposition

5.4
yield

\begin{split}&d_{\mathrm{LP}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T}),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}])\big)\\
&\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{t\in[0,T]}\mathrm{dist}\!\left(X(t),\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus D_{T}(\vec{R})\right)\leq\Delta\right)+\left(\frac{4n}{T}+219+6\sqrt{n}\right)\mathcal{E}+\frac{\sqrt{T}}{2}K_{\mathrm{E};T}R_{\mathrm{E}}\lambda,\end{split}

(5.36)

whenever the parameters satisfy the constraint (
5.5
). Notice that the left-hand side of (
5.36
) does not depend on the choice of the control parameter
\lambda
,
\Delta
, or
\vec{R}=(R_{\mathrm{G},b},R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{C},b},R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{E}})\in[1,\infty)^{5}
. In order to show the convergence of
d_{\mathrm{LP}}(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T}),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}]))
as
|\pi|\downarrow 0
with convergence speed as fast as possible, we focus on the minimization problem for the right-hand side of (
5.36
) in terms of the control parameters satisfying the constraint (
5.5
). This is the main idea of the proof of
Theorem

3.1
. In the following proof, we first show assertion (ii) by choosing suitable intensity parameter
\lambda
which makes the right-hand side of (
5.36
) as small as possible (in view of the convergence order) for fixed
T
,
\pi
,
\Delta
and
\vec{R}
. Then, assertion (i) follows from (ii) by taking the limits
|\pi|\downarrow 0
,
\Delta\downarrow 0
and
R_{\mathrm{G},b},R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{C},b},R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{E}}\uparrow\infty
in this order. Lastly, based on the estimate derived in assertion (ii), together with the additional assumption (
3.5
) on the decay rate of the probability of the “rare event”, we solve an optimal control problem with respect to
\Delta
and
\vec{R}
and obtain the (nearly) optimal convergence rate in terms of
|\pi|
, showing assertion (iii).

Remark 5.5
.

Under the constraint (
5.5
), the last term in the right-hand side of (
5.36
) is greater than

2^{9}n\sqrt{T}K_{\mathrm{E};T}R_{\mathrm{E}}\max\left\{K_{\mathrm{C},b;T}R_{\mathrm{C},b}\mathcal{E}^{\alpha_{b}},\frac{\left(K_{\mathrm{C},\sigma;T}R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}\mathcal{E}^{\alpha_{\sigma}}\right)^{2}}{\Delta}\log\frac{1}{\Delta}\right\},

which is a benchmark for the error estimate of
d_{\mathrm{LP}}(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T}),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}]))
; notice that, by the definition (
3.4
) of
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}(|\pi|,\Delta,R_{\mathrm{G},b},R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma})\in(0,\infty)
, the benchmark-term above converges to zero as
|\pi|\downarrow 0
and
\Delta\downarrow 0
if and only if
\alpha_{\sigma}>\frac{1}{2}
, which is the standing assumption imposed in
Assumption

1
.

With the above observations in mind, we provide a proof of our main result.

Proof of
Theorem

3.1
.

We first show assertion (ii). Assertions (i) and (iii) follow from (ii).

Proof of (ii).
Fix
T\in(0,\infty)
,
\pi\in\Pi_{T}
,
\Delta\in(0,1]
and
\vec{R}=(R_{\mathrm{G},b},R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{C},b},R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{E}})\in[1,\infty)^{5}
. We prove the estimate (
3.3
). Without loss of generality, we may assume that each term in the right-hand side of (
3.3
) is less than
1
; otherwise the estimate becomes trivial. In particular, noting that
K_{\mathrm{E};T}R_{\mathrm{E}}\geq 1
, we may assume that

2^{12}nT\max\left\{K_{\mathrm{C},b;T}R_{\mathrm{C},b}\mathcal{E}^{\alpha_{b}},\frac{\left(K_{\mathrm{C},\sigma;T}R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}\mathcal{E}^{\alpha_{\sigma}}\right)^{2}}{\Delta}\log\frac{1}{\Delta}\right\}\leq 1.

(5.37)

As discussed above, the estimate (
5.36
) holds whenever the parameters satisfy the constraint (
5.5
). With the observations in
Remark

5.5
in mind, we take

\lambda=2^{11}n\max\left\{K_{\mathrm{C},b;T}R_{\mathrm{C},b}\mathcal{E}^{\alpha_{b}},\frac{\left(K_{\mathrm{C},\sigma;T}R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}\mathcal{E}^{\alpha_{\sigma}}\right)^{2}}{\Delta}\log\frac{1}{\Delta}\right\}.

Notice that the assumption (
5.37
) implies that
2T\lambda\leq 1
, and hence
\log(1\vee\frac{2T\lambda}{\Delta})\leq\log\frac{1}{\Delta}
. Therefore, the above
\lambda
satisfies the constraint (
5.5
), and the estimate (
5.36
) yields

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{LP}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T}),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}])\big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{t\in[0,T]}\mathrm{dist}\!\left(X(t),\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus D_{T}(\vec{R})\right)\leq\Delta\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\left(\frac{4n}{T}+219+6\sqrt{n}+2^{10}n\sqrt{T}\right)K_{\mathrm{E};T}R_{\mathrm{E}}\max\left\{\mathcal{E},K_{\mathrm{C},b;T}R_{\mathrm{C},b}\mathcal{E}^{\alpha_{b}},\frac{\left(K_{\mathrm{C},\sigma;T}R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}\mathcal{E}^{\alpha_{\sigma}}\right)^{2}}{\Delta}\log\frac{1}{\Delta}\right\}.

Since
\frac{4n}{T}+219+6\sqrt{n}+2^{10}n\sqrt{T}\leq 2^{12}n(T+\frac{1}{T})
, we get the desired estimate (
3.3
).

Proof of (i).
Let
T\in(0,\infty)
be given. We take the limits

|\pi|\downarrow 0,\ \Delta\downarrow 0\ \text{and}\ R_{\mathrm{G},b},R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{C},b},R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{E}}\uparrow\infty

in (
3.3
) in this order. First, observe that the number
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}(|\pi|,\Delta,R_{\mathrm{G},b},R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma})\in(0,\infty)
defined by (
3.4
) tends to
\Delta
as
|\pi|\downarrow 0
, and hence the estimate (
3.3
) yields

\displaystyle\limsup_{|\pi|\downarrow 0}d_{\mathrm{LP}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T}),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}])\big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{t\in[0,T]}\mathrm{dist}\!\left(X(t),\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus D_{T}(\vec{R})\right)\leq\Delta\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+2^{12}n\left(T+\frac{1}{T}\right)K_{\mathrm{E};T}R_{\mathrm{E}}\max\left\{\Delta,K_{\mathrm{C},b;T}R_{\mathrm{C},b}\Delta^{\alpha_{b}},K_{\mathrm{C},\sigma;T}^{2}R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}^{2}\Delta^{2\alpha_{\sigma}-1}\log\frac{1}{\Delta}\right\}.

Then, noting that
2\alpha_{\sigma}-1>0
, by taking the limit
\Delta\downarrow 0
, we have

\limsup_{|\pi|\downarrow 0}d_{\mathrm{LP}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T}),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}])\big)\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{t\in[0,T]}\mathrm{dist}\!\left(X(t),\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus D_{T}(\vec{R})\right)=0\right).

Lastly, we take the limits
R_{\mathrm{G},b},R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{C},b},R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{E}}\to\infty
in the right-hand side of the above inequality. Notice that the set

D_{T}(\vec{R})=D_{\mathrm{G},b;T}(R_{\mathrm{G},b})\cap D_{\mathrm{G},\sigma;T}(R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma})\cap D_{\mathrm{C},b;T}(R_{\mathrm{C},b})\cap D_{\mathrm{C},\sigma;T}(R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma})\cap D_{\mathrm{E};T}(R_{\mathrm{E}})

is open in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
for each
\vec{R}=(R_{\mathrm{G},b},R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{C},b},R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{E}})\in[1,\infty)^{5}
, increasing in each of the variables, and
\bigcup_{\vec{R}\in[1,\infty)^{5}}D_{T}(\vec{R})=D
. Notice also that, for
\mathbb{P}
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
, the path
t\mapsto X(\omega,t)
is continuous and hence the set
\{X(\omega,t)\,|\,t\in[0,T]\}
is compact in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. From these observations, we see that

\displaystyle\lim_{R_{\mathrm{G},b},R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{C},b},R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{E}}\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{t\in[0,T]}\mathrm{dist}\!\left(X(t),\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus D_{T}(\vec{R})\right)=0\right)
