\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{t\in[0,T]}\mathrm{dist}\!\left(X(t),\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus D_{T}(\vec{R})\right)=0\ \text{for any $\vec{R}\in[1,\infty)^{5}$}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(\left\{\omega\in\Omega\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\big\{X(\omega,t)\,\big|\,t\in[0,T]\big\}\subset D_{T}(\vec{R})\ \text{for some $\vec{R}\in[1,\infty)^{5}$}\right\}^{\complement}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(\left\{\omega\in\Omega\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\big\{X(\omega,t)\,\big|\,t\in[0,T]\big\}\subset D\right\}^{\complement}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\Big(X(t)\notin D\ \text{for some $t\in[0,T]$}\Big),

where it does not matter in which order the limits are taken in the first line. By the definition of the weak solution of the SFDE (
1.1
) associated with
(D,\mu_{0},b,\sigma)
, we have
\mathbb{P}(\text{$X(t)\in D$ for any $t\in[0,\infty)$})=1
, and hence the last term above is zero. Consequently, we get

\lim_{|\pi|\downarrow 0}d_{\mathrm{LP}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T}),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}])\big)=0,

and hence
\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}]\to X_{T}
weakly on
\mathcal{C}_{T}
as
|\pi|\downarrow 0
. This result indicates that, for any weak solution
(X,W,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
of the SFDE (
1.1
) associated with the data
(D,\mu_{0},b,\sigma)
, the law of
X_{T}
on
\mathcal{C}_{T}
is characterized as the weak limit of the law of
\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}]
on
\mathcal{C}_{T}
. Since
T\in(0,\infty)
is arbitrary, we see that uniqueness in law holds for the SFDE (
1.1
) associated with
(D,\mu_{0},b,\sigma)
.

Proof of (iii).
Since
\alpha_{\sigma}>\frac{1}{2}
, we can take a number
\varepsilon\in(0,2\alpha_{\sigma}-1)
. Then, noting that
\log x\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon}x^{\varepsilon}
for any
x\in[1,\infty)
, we obtain the following simpler (but slightly weaker) version of assertion (ii): For any
T\in(0,\infty)
,
\pi\in\Pi_{T}
,
\Delta\in(0,1]
and
\vec{R}=(R_{\mathrm{G},b},R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{C},b},R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{E}})\in[1,\infty)^{5}
, it holds that

\begin{split}&d_{\mathrm{LP}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T}),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}])\big)\\
&\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{t\in[0,T]}\mathrm{dist}\!\left(X(t),\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus D_{T}(\vec{R})\right)\leq\Delta\right)+n\widehat{\mathcal{K}}_{T,\varepsilon}R_{\mathrm{E}}\max\left\{R_{\mathrm{C},b}\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}^{\alpha_{b}},\frac{R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}^{2}\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}^{2\alpha_{\sigma}}}{\Delta^{1+\varepsilon}}\right\},\end{split}

(5.38)

where the constant
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(|\pi|,\Delta,R_{\mathrm{G},b},R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma})\in(0,\infty)
is defined by

\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}:=\max\left\{\Delta,R_{\mathrm{G},b}|\pi|,R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}|\pi|^{(1-\varepsilon)/2}\right\},

and the constant
\widehat{\mathcal{K}}_{T,\varepsilon}\in[1,\infty)
, which depends only on
T
,
K_{\mathrm{G},b;T}
,
K_{\mathrm{G},\sigma;T}
,
K_{\mathrm{C},b;T}
,
K_{\mathrm{C},\sigma;T}
,
K_{\mathrm{E};T}
,
\alpha_{b}
,
\alpha_{\sigma}
and
\varepsilon
, is defined by

\widehat{\mathcal{K}}_{T,\varepsilon}:=2^{12}\left(T+\frac{1}{T}\right)K_{\mathrm{E};T}\max\left\{\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{T,\varepsilon},K_{\mathrm{C},b;T}\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{T,\varepsilon}^{\alpha_{b}},\frac{1}{\varepsilon}K_{\mathrm{C},\sigma;T}^{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{T,\varepsilon}^{2\alpha_{\sigma}}\right\}

with

\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{T,\varepsilon}:=\max\left\{K_{\mathrm{G},b;T},\sqrt{\frac{T^{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon}}K_{\mathrm{G},\sigma;T}\right\}.

Here, we used the facts that the left-hand side of (
5.38
) is less than or equal to
1
and that
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}\wedge 1\leq R_{\mathrm{C},b}\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}^{\alpha_{b}}
; the latter is due to
\alpha_{b}\leq 1
and
R_{\mathrm{C},b}\geq 1
. Combining (
5.38
) and the assumption (
3.5
) on the decay rate of the probability of the “rare event”, we will show that, for each
\varepsilon\in(0,2\alpha_{\sigma}-1)
,
T\in(0,\infty)
and
\pi\in\Pi_{T}
,

d_{\mathrm{LP}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T}),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}])\big)\leq\big(\widehat{C}+n\widehat{\mathcal{K}}_{T,\varepsilon}\big)|\pi|^{\gamma_{\varepsilon}},

(5.39)

where the exponent
\gamma_{\varepsilon}=\gamma_{\varepsilon}(\alpha_{b},\alpha_{\sigma},\vec{\beta})\in(0,\infty)
is determined in (
5.40
) below. After that, we will take the limit
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
and obtain the statement of (iii).

Let
\varepsilon\in(0,2\alpha_{\sigma}-1)
,
T\in(0,\infty)
and
\pi\in\Pi_{T}
be fixed. In order to show (
5.39
), without loss of generality, we may assume that
|\pi|\leq 1
; otherwise the estimate becomes trivial. We apply the estimate (
5.38
) and the assumption (
3.5
) to the parameters
\Delta\in(0,1]
and
\vec{R}\in[1,\infty)^{5}
of the following forms:

\displaystyle\Delta=|\pi|^{\delta},

\displaystyle R_{\mathrm{G},b}=|\pi|^{-r_{\mathrm{G},b}},\ R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}=|\pi|^{-r_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}},\ R_{\mathrm{C},b}=|\pi|^{-r_{\mathrm{C},b}},\ R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}=|\pi|^{-r_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}},\ R_{\mathrm{E}}=|\pi|^{-r_{\mathrm{E}}},

for some constants
\delta,r_{\mathrm{G},b},r_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},r_{\mathrm{C},b},r_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},r_{\mathrm{E}}\in[0,\infty)
. Then, we obtain

d_{\mathrm{LP}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T}),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}])\big)\leq\big(\widehat{C}+n\widehat{\mathcal{K}}_{T,\varepsilon}\big)|\pi|^{\Gamma_{\varepsilon}(\delta,r_{\mathrm{G},b},r_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},r_{\mathrm{C},b},r_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},r_{\mathrm{E}})},

where

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\varepsilon}(\delta,r_{\mathrm{G},b},r_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},r_{\mathrm{C},b},r_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},r_{\mathrm{E}})

\displaystyle:=\min\Big\{\beta_{0}\delta,\beta_{\mathrm{G},b}r_{\mathrm{G},b},\beta_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}r_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},\beta_{\mathrm{C},b}r_{\mathrm{C},b},\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}r_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},\beta_{\mathrm{E}}r_{\mathrm{E}},

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt-r_{\mathrm{E}}-r_{\mathrm{C},b}+\alpha_{b}\min\Big\{\delta,1-r_{\mathrm{G},b},\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}-r_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}\Big\},

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt-r_{\mathrm{E}}-2r_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}+2\alpha_{\sigma}\min\Big\{\delta,1-r_{\mathrm{G},b},\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}-r_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}\Big\}-(1+\varepsilon)\delta\Big\}.

Since the parameters
\delta,r_{\mathrm{G},b},r_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},r_{\mathrm{C},b},r_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},r_{\mathrm{E}}\in[0,\infty)
are arbitrary, we see that (
5.39
) holds with exponent
\gamma_{\varepsilon}=\gamma_{\varepsilon}(\alpha_{b},\alpha_{\sigma},\vec{\beta})
given by

\gamma_{\varepsilon}:=\sup_{\delta,r_{\mathrm{G},b},r_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},r_{\mathrm{C},b},r_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},r_{\mathrm{E}}\in[0,\infty)}\Gamma_{\varepsilon}(\delta,r_{\mathrm{G},b},r_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},r_{\mathrm{C},b},r_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},r_{\mathrm{E}}).

(5.40)

Notice that, extending the domain of the function
\Gamma_{\varepsilon}
to
\mathbb{R}^{6}
, we see that
\Gamma_{\varepsilon}:\mathbb{R}^{6}\to\mathbb{R}
is continuous and
\Gamma_{\varepsilon}\leq 0
on
\mathbb{R}^{6}\setminus(0,1)^{6}
. Furthermore, thanks to
2\alpha_{\sigma}-1-\varepsilon>0
, we can easily show that
\Gamma_{\varepsilon}
takes positive values on a compact subset of
(0,1)^{6}
. Therefore, the supremum in (
5.40
) over
[0,\infty)^{6}
is a maximum over
\mathbb{R}^{6}
, and there exists an optimizer in
(0,1)^{6}
which attains the maximal value
\gamma_{\varepsilon}>0
.

In the following, we solve the optimization problem (
5.40
) and provide a more precise expression of the maximal value
\gamma_{\varepsilon}
. Observe that

\gamma_{\varepsilon}=\max_{\delta,r_{\mathrm{G},b},r_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}\in\mathbb{R}}\min\Big\{\beta_{0}\delta,\beta_{\mathrm{G},b}r_{\mathrm{G},b},\beta_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}r_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},\gamma^{(1)}_{\varepsilon}(\delta,r_{\mathrm{G},b},r_{\mathrm{G},\sigma})\Big\},

(5.41)

where

\displaystyle\gamma^{(1)}_{\varepsilon}(\delta,r_{\mathrm{G},b},r_{\mathrm{G},\sigma})

\displaystyle:=\max_{r_{\mathrm{E}}\in\mathbb{R}}\min\left\{\beta_{\mathrm{E}}r_{\mathrm{E}},\gamma^{(2)}_{\varepsilon}(\delta,r_{\mathrm{G},b},r_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},r_{\mathrm{E}}),\gamma^{(3)}_{\varepsilon}(\delta,r_{\mathrm{G},b},r_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},r_{\mathrm{E}})\right\},

\displaystyle\gamma^{(2)}_{\varepsilon}(\delta,r_{\mathrm{G},b},r_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},r_{\mathrm{E}})

\displaystyle:=\max_{r_{\mathrm{C},b}\in\mathbb{R}}\min\left\{\beta_{\mathrm{C},b}r_{\mathrm{C},b},-r_{\mathrm{E}}-r_{\mathrm{C},b}+\alpha_{b}\min\Big\{\delta,1-r_{\mathrm{G},b},\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}-r_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}\Big\}\right\},

\displaystyle\gamma^{(3)}_{\varepsilon}(\delta,r_{\mathrm{G},b},r_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},r_{\mathrm{E}})

\displaystyle:=\max_{r_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}\in\mathbb{R}}\min\left\{\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}r_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},{-}r_{\mathrm{E}}{-}2r_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}{+}2\alpha_{\sigma}\min\Big\{\delta,1{-}r_{\mathrm{G},b},\frac{1{-}\varepsilon}{2}{-}r_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}\Big\}{-}(1{+}\varepsilon)\delta\right\}.

By elementary calculus, we see that

\displaystyle\gamma^{(2)}_{\varepsilon}(\delta,r_{\mathrm{G},b},r_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},r_{\mathrm{E}})

\displaystyle=\frac{\beta_{\mathrm{C},b}}{\beta_{\mathrm{C},b}+1}\Big(-r_{\mathrm{E}}+\alpha_{b}\min\Big\{\delta,1-r_{\mathrm{G},b},\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}-r_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}\Big\}\Big),

\displaystyle\gamma^{(3)}_{\varepsilon}(\delta,r_{\mathrm{G},b},r_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},r_{\mathrm{E}})

\displaystyle=\frac{\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}}{\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}+2}\Big(-r_{\mathrm{E}}+2\alpha_{\sigma}\min\Big\{\delta,1-r_{\mathrm{G},b},\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}-r_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}\Big\}-(1+\varepsilon)\delta\Big).

Hence,

\displaystyle\gamma^{(1)}_{\varepsilon}(\delta,r_{\mathrm{G},b},r_{\mathrm{G},\sigma})

\displaystyle=\max_{r_{\mathrm{E}}\in\mathbb{R}}\min\left\{\beta_{\mathrm{E}}r_{\mathrm{E}},\frac{\beta_{\mathrm{C},b}}{\beta_{\mathrm{C},b}+1}\Big(-r_{\mathrm{E}}+\alpha_{b}\min\Big\{\delta,1-r_{\mathrm{G},b},\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}-r_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}\Big\}\Big),\right.

\displaystyle\left.\hskip 56.9055pt\frac{\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}}{\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}+2}\Big(-r_{\mathrm{E}}+2\alpha_{\sigma}\min\Big\{\delta,1-r_{\mathrm{G},b},\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}-r_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}\Big\}-(1+\varepsilon)\delta\Big)\right\}.

In order to compute the above term, we use the following elementary fact; for any
\kappa_{0},\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}>0
and
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
,

\max_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\min\Big\{\kappa_{0}x,-\kappa_{1}x+\theta_{1},-\kappa_{2}x+\theta_{2}\Big\}=\min\left\{\frac{\kappa_{0}}{\kappa_{0}+\kappa_{1}}\theta_{1},\frac{\kappa_{0}}{\kappa_{0}+\kappa_{2}}\theta_{2}\right\}.

(5.42)

From the above fact, we get

\displaystyle\gamma^{(1)}_{\varepsilon}(\delta,r_{\mathrm{G},b},r_{\mathrm{G},\sigma})

\displaystyle=\min\left\{\frac{1}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+\beta_{\mathrm{C},b}^{-1}}\alpha_{b}\min\Big\{\delta,1-r_{\mathrm{G},b},\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}-r_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}\Big\},\right.

\displaystyle\left.\hskip 56.9055pt\frac{1}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+2\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}^{-1}}\Big(2\alpha_{\sigma}\min\Big\{\delta,1-r_{\mathrm{G},b},\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}-r_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}\Big\}-(1+\varepsilon)\delta\Big)\right\}.

Inserting the above expression to (
5.41
), we obtain

\gamma_{\varepsilon}=\max_{\delta\in\mathbb{R}}\min\Big\{\beta_{\varepsilon}\delta,\gamma^{(4)}_{\varepsilon}(\delta),\gamma^{(5)}_{\varepsilon}(\delta)\Big\},

(5.43)

where

\beta_{\varepsilon}:=\min\left\{\beta_{0},\frac{\alpha_{b}}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+\beta_{\mathrm{C},b}^{-1}},\frac{2\alpha_{\sigma}-1-\varepsilon}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+2\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}^{-1}}\right\}>0

(5.44)

and

\displaystyle\gamma^{(4)}_{\varepsilon}(\delta):=\max_{r_{\mathrm{G},b}\in\mathbb{R}}\min\left\{\beta_{\mathrm{G},b}r_{\mathrm{G},b},\frac{\alpha_{b}(1-r_{\mathrm{G},b})}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+\beta_{\mathrm{C},b}^{-1}},\frac{2\alpha_{\sigma}(1-r_{\mathrm{G},b})-(1+\varepsilon)\delta}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+2\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}^{-1}}\right\},

\displaystyle\gamma^{(5)}_{\varepsilon}(\delta):=\max_{r_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}\in\mathbb{R}}\min\left\{\beta_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}r_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},\frac{\alpha_{b}\Big(\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}-r_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}\Big)}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+\beta_{\mathrm{C},b}^{-1}},\frac{2\alpha_{\sigma}\Big(\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}-r_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}\Big)-(1+\varepsilon)\delta}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+2\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}^{-1}}\right\}.

Again by using the elementary calculus (
5.42
), we have

\displaystyle\gamma^{(4)}_{\varepsilon}(\delta)=\min\left\{\frac{\alpha_{b}}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+\beta_{\mathrm{C},b}^{-1}+\beta_{\mathrm{G},b}^{-1}\alpha_{b}},\frac{2\alpha_{\sigma}-(1+\varepsilon)\delta}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+2\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}^{-1}+2\beta_{\mathrm{G},b}^{-1}\alpha_{\sigma}}\right\},

\displaystyle\gamma^{(5)}_{\varepsilon}(\delta)=\min\left\{\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}\cdot\frac{\alpha_{b}}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+\beta_{\mathrm{C},b}^{-1}+\beta_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}^{-1}\alpha_{b}},\frac{(1-\varepsilon)\alpha_{\sigma}-(1+\varepsilon)\delta}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+2\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}^{-1}+2\beta_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}^{-1}\alpha_{\sigma}}\right\}.

Inserting the above expressions to (
5.43
), we have

\gamma_{\varepsilon}=\min\left\{\frac{\alpha_{b}}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+\beta_{\mathrm{C},b}^{-1}+\beta_{\mathrm{G},b}^{-1}\alpha_{b}},\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}\cdot\frac{\alpha_{b}}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+\beta_{\mathrm{C},b}^{-1}+\beta_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}^{-1}\alpha_{b}},\gamma^{(6)}_{\varepsilon}\right\},

(5.45)

where

\gamma^{(6)}_{\varepsilon}:=\max_{\delta\in\mathbb{R}}\min\left\{\beta_{\varepsilon}\delta,\frac{2\alpha_{\sigma}-(1+\varepsilon)\delta}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+2\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}^{-1}+2\beta_{\mathrm{G},b}^{-1}\alpha_{\sigma}},\frac{(1-\varepsilon)\alpha_{\sigma}-(1+\varepsilon)\delta}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+2\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}^{-1}+2\beta_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}^{-1}\alpha_{\sigma}}\right\}.

Using the elementary calculus (
5.42
), we have

\displaystyle\gamma^{(6)}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\min\left\{\frac{2\alpha_{\sigma}}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+2\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}^{-1}+2\beta_{\mathrm{G},b}^{-1}\alpha_{\sigma}+(1+\varepsilon)\beta_{\varepsilon}^{-1}},\frac{(1-\varepsilon)\alpha_{\sigma}}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+2\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}^{-1}+2\beta_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}^{-1}\alpha_{\sigma}+(1+\varepsilon)\beta_{\varepsilon}^{-1}}\right\}.

Inserting the above expression to (
5.45
), we obtain

\begin{split}\gamma_{\varepsilon}&=\min\left\{\frac{\alpha_{b}}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+\beta_{\mathrm{C},b}^{-1}+\beta_{\mathrm{G},b}^{-1}\alpha_{b}},\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}\cdot\frac{\alpha_{b}}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+\beta_{\mathrm{C},b}^{-1}+\beta_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}^{-1}\alpha_{b}},\right.\\
&\left.\hskip 42.67912pt\frac{2\alpha_{\sigma}}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+2\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}^{-1}+2\beta_{\mathrm{G},b}^{-1}\alpha_{\sigma}+(1+\varepsilon)\beta_{\varepsilon}^{-1}},\frac{(1-\varepsilon)\alpha_{\sigma}}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+2\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}^{-1}+2\beta_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}^{-1}\alpha_{\sigma}+(1+\varepsilon)\beta_{\varepsilon}^{-1}}\right\}.\end{split}

(5.46)

To summarize the above arguments, the estimate (
5.39
) holds for any
\varepsilon\in(0,2\alpha_{\sigma}-1)
,
T\in(0,\infty)
and
\pi\in\Pi_{T}
, where the exponent
\gamma_{\varepsilon}>0
is defined as the maximal value of the optimization problem (
5.40
) and given by the expression (
5.46
), and the number
\beta_{\varepsilon}>0
is given by (
5.44
). Also, notice that
\beta_{\varepsilon}
and
\gamma_{\varepsilon}
are monotonically decreasing with respect to
\varepsilon\in(0,2\alpha_{\sigma}-1)
(that is, monotonically increasing as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
), and

\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,2\alpha_{\sigma}-1)}\beta_{\varepsilon}=\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\beta_{\varepsilon}=\beta_{*},\ \ \sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,2\alpha_{\sigma}-1)}\gamma_{\varepsilon}=\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\gamma_{\varepsilon}=\gamma_{*},

where
\beta_{*}
and
\gamma_{*}
are given by (
3.7
) and (
3.6
), respectively. Moreover, the convergences above are uniform with respect to
\vec{\beta}=(\beta_{0},\beta_{\mathrm{G},b},\beta_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},\beta_{\mathrm{C},b},\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},\beta_{\mathrm{E}})
. Therefore, for any
\gamma\in(0,\gamma_{*})
, we can take
\varepsilon\in(0,2\alpha_{\sigma}-1)
, which depends only on
\alpha_{b}
,
\alpha_{\sigma}
and
\gamma_{*}-\gamma
, such that
\gamma<\gamma_{\varepsilon}\leq\gamma_{*}
. Hence, defining
\mathcal{K}_{T,\gamma}:=\widehat{\mathcal{K}}_{T,\varepsilon}
for such an
\varepsilon
, we obtain the conclusion of (iii). This completes the proof.
∎

6
Applications to examples

In this section, we apply our general results (
Theorem

3.1
and
Corollary

3.3
) to some concrete examples of Markovian and non-Markovian models appearing in physics, chemistry, mathematical finance, economics, population biology, and so on, and provide precise weak error estimates for them.
To be specific, we pick up the following ten examples which have different features and difficulties from each other:

•

Section

6.1
: Stochastic Lorenz model

•

Section

6.2
: Stochastic Brusselator in the well-stirred case

•

Section

6.3
: Aït–Sahalia-type interest rate model with delay

•

Section

6.4
: Stochastic delay differential neoclassical growth model

•

Section

6.5
: Reflected Ornstein–Uhlenbeck process

•

Section

6.6
: Stochastic Duffing–van der Pol oscillator

•

Section

6.7
: Wright–Fisher diffusion with seed bank

•

Section

6.8
: Multi-dimensional polynomial diffusion

•

Section

6.9
: Volatility process in the
3/2
-stochastic volatility model

•

Section

6.10
: Dyson’s Brownian motion

For the first six examples, we can use
Corollary

3.3
(i) directly since, as we will see below, all of them satisfy the required moment condition (
3.8
). The last four examples are more complicated; by means of a careful moment estimate related to the “rare event” in (
3.5
), we apply
Theorem

3.1
(iii) and obtain a weak convergence rate depending on the parameters for each model. In all examples in this section, it is the first to obtain convergence rates for functional type weak approximations by means of the linear interpolation of the (standard) Euler–Maruyama scheme.

6.1
Stochastic Lorenz model

The Lorenz model is one of the most famous nonlinear models of chaos and has been extensively studied in a wide range of areas since the original study by Lorenz
[
52
]
on a simplified mathematical model of atmospheric convection.
Here, as a special case of the model considered in
[
45
]
, we consider the stochastic Lorenz model with additive noise, which is described by the following three dimensional coupled nonlinear system:

\begin{split}&\mathrm{d}X_{1}(t)=\kappa_{1}\Big\{X_{2}(t)-X_{1}(t)\Big\}\,\mathrm{d}t+\theta_{1}\,\mathrm{d}W_{1}(t),\\
&\mathrm{d}X_{2}(t)=\Big\{\kappa_{2}X_{1}(t)-X_{2}(t)-X_{1}(t)X_{3}(t)\Big\}\,\mathrm{d}t+\theta_{2}\,\mathrm{d}W_{2}(t),\\
&\mathrm{d}X_{3}(t)=\Big\{X_{1}(t)X_{2}(t)-\kappa_{3}X_{3}(t)\Big\}\,\mathrm{d}t+\theta_{3}\,\mathrm{d}W_{3}(t),\ \ t\in[0,\infty),\end{split}

(6.1)

where
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\kappa_{3},\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\theta_{3}\in\mathbb{R}
are given parameters. The above system can be seen as a Markovian SDE (
4.6
) with
d=n=3
,
D=\mathbb{R}^{3}
and coefficients
\bar{b}:\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
and
\bar{\sigma}:\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}
defined by

\bar{b}(\bar{x})=\begin{pmatrix}\kappa_{1}(\bar{x}_{2}-\bar{x}_{1})\\
\kappa_{2}\bar{x}_{1}-\bar{x}_{2}-\bar{x}_{1}\bar{x}_{3}\\
\bar{x}_{1}\bar{x}_{2}-\kappa_{3}\bar{x}_{3}\end{pmatrix},\ \ \bar{\sigma}(\bar{x})=\begin{pmatrix}\theta_{1}&0&0\\
0&\theta_{2}&0\\
0&0&\theta_{3}\end{pmatrix},

for
\bar{x}=(\bar{x}_{1},\bar{x}_{2},\bar{x}_{3})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. It is known that the system (
6.1
) admits a unique strong solution
X=(X_{1},X_{2},X_{3})^{\top}
for any initial condition
X(0)=\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
(see
[
45
, Theorem 4.4]
).

In view of the numerical study for the stochastic Lorenz model (
6.1
), there is a difficulty that the drift coefficient
\bar{b}
is super-linearly growing. This indicates that the standard Euler–Maruyama scheme corresponding to (
6.1
) might diverge in the
L^{p}
sense for any
p\in(0,\infty)
(see
[
38
]
and
Section

4.3
).
In order to overcome this difficulty, Hutzenthaler and Jentzen
[
37
]
study an appropriately modified Euler–Maruyama scheme called increment-tamed Euler–Maruyama scheme and show its converge to the solution
X=(X_{1},X_{2},X_{3})^{\top}
of the Markovian SDE (
6.1
) at each fixed time
T\in(0,\infty)
in the
L^{p}
sense for any
p\in(0,\infty)
.
However, they do not obtain any convergence rate for it.
Compared with
[
37
]
, we consider functional type weak convergence of the linear interpolation of the standard Euler–Maruyama scheme (
4.8
) and give a convergence rate in terms of the Lévy–Prokhorov metric.

By the same arguments as in
Section

4.4.1
, we see that the system (
6.1
) satisfies conditions (G’), (C’) (with
\alpha_{b}=\alpha_{\sigma}=1
) and (E’) in
Corollary

3.3
under the non-degeneracy condition
\theta_{1}\theta_{2}\theta_{3}\neq 0
. Furthermore, by
[
45
, Corollary 4.5 (ii)]
, the solution
X
with fixed initial condition
X(0)=\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
satisfies
\mathbb{E}\big[\|X_{T}\|_{\infty}^{p}\big]<\infty
for any
T\in(0,\infty)
and
p\in[1,\infty)
(which follows also from
Lemma

B.1
(i)). Therefore, the moment condition (
3.8
) in
Corollary

3.3
(i) holds, and we immediately obtain the following result.

Theorem 6.1
.

Fix
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\kappa_{3},\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\theta_{3}\in\mathbb{R}
, and assume that
\theta_{1}\theta_{2}\theta_{3}\neq 0
. Let
(X,W,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
be the weak solution of the SDE (
6.1
) on
D=\mathbb{R}^{3}
with initial condition
X(0)=\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
For each
T\in(0,\infty)
and
\pi\in\Pi_{T}
, let
X^{\pi}
be the Euler–Maruyama scheme defined on
(\Omega^{\pi},\mathcal{F}^{\pi},\mathbb{P}^{\pi})
and given by (
4.8
) with initial condition
X^{\pi}(t_{0})=\xi
.
Then, for any
\gamma\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
, there exists a constant
C_{\gamma}\in(0,\infty)
such that

d_{\mathrm{LP}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T}),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}])\big)\leq C_{\gamma}|\pi|^{\gamma}

for any
\pi\in\Pi_{T}
.

6.2
Stochastic Brusselator in the well-stirred case

The Brusselator introduced by Prigogine and Lefever
[
62
]
is one of theoretical models to describe the evolution of the concentration of the reactants in chemistry. In the well-stirred case, Dawson
[
23
]
proposed a stochastic Brusselator (see also
[
68
]
), which is described by the following two dimensional coupled nonlinear system with multiplicative noise:

\begin{split}&\mathrm{d}X_{1}(t)=\Big\{\kappa_{1}-(\kappa_{2}+1)X_{1}(t)+X_{2}(t)X_{1}(t)^{2}\Big\}\,\mathrm{d}t+\theta_{1}(X_{1}(t))\,\mathrm{d}W_{1}(t),\\
&\mathrm{d}X_{2}(t)=\Big\{\kappa_{2}X_{1}(t)-X_{2}(t)X_{1}(t)^{2}\Big\}\,\mathrm{d}t+\theta_{2}(X_{2}(t))\,\mathrm{d}W_{2}(t),\ \ t\in[0,\infty),\end{split}

(6.2)

where
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\in(0,\infty)
are fixed constants, and
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
are globally Lipschitz continuous functions with
\theta_{1}(0)=\theta_{2}(0)=0
and
\sup_{x\in[0,\infty)}|\theta_{2}(x)|<\infty
. The above system can be seen as a Markovian SDE (
4.6
) with
d=n=2
,
D=(0,\infty)^{2}
and coefficients
\bar{b}:D\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
\bar{\sigma}:D\to\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}
defined by

\bar{b}(\bar{x})=\begin{pmatrix}\kappa_{1}-(\kappa_{2}+1)\bar{x}_{1}+\bar{x}_{2}\bar{x}_{1}^{2}\\
\kappa_{2}\bar{x}_{1}-\bar{x}_{2}\bar{x}_{1}^{2}\end{pmatrix},\ \ \bar{\sigma}(\bar{x})=\begin{pmatrix}\theta_{1}(\bar{x}_{1})&0\\
0&\theta_{2}(\bar{x}_{2})\end{pmatrix},

for
\bar{x}=(\bar{x}_{1},\bar{x}_{2})^{\top}\in D
. By
[
68
, Theorem 2.1 a)]
, for each initial condition
X(0)=\xi\in[0,\infty)^{2}
, there exists a unique strong solution
X=(X_{1},X_{2})^{\top}
to the SDE (
6.2
) such that, a.s.,
X_{1}(t)\geq 0
and
X_{2}(t)\geq 0
for any
t\in[0,\infty)
. Furthermore,
Lemma

6.2
below shows that the solution never hits the boundary of
D=(0,\infty)^{2}
if
\xi\in D
.

As in the case of the stochastic Lorenz model (
6.1
), due to the super-linear terms in the drift coefficient, the standard Euler–Maruyama scheme of the above SDE might diverge in the
L^{p}
sense for any
p\in(0,\infty)
(see
[
38
]
and
Section

4.3
).
Hutzenthaler and Jentzen
[
37
]
then consider the increment-tamed Euler–Maruyama scheme for (
6.2
) and show its convergence to the true solution
X
at each fixed time
T\in(0,\infty)
in the
L^{p}
sense for any
p\in(0,\infty)
.
However, they do not obtain any convergence rate for it. Compared with
[
37
]
, we consider functional type weak convergence of the linear interpolation of the standard Euler–Maruyama scheme (
4.8
).

We will apply
Corollary

3.3
(i) to this setting. To do so, we need to check the moment condition (
3.8
).
Notice that the coefficients of the SDE (
6.2
) satisfy neither (
B.1
) nor (
B.2
) in
Lemma

B.1
. Nevertheless, the following lemma ensures that the desired moment estimate (
3.8
) holds in this setting.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\in(0,\infty)
be fixed constants, and let
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
be two globally Lipschitz continuous functions with
\theta_{1}(0)=\theta_{2}(0)=0
and
\sup_{x\in[0,\infty)}|\theta_{2}(x)|<\infty
. Then, there exists a unique strong solution
X=(X_{1},X_{2})^{\top}
of the SDE (
6.2
) on
D=(0,\infty)^{2}
with initial condition
X(0)=\xi\in D
. Furthermore, for any
T\in(0,\infty)
and
p\in[1,\infty)
, it holds that

\mathbb{E}\big[\|X_{T}\|_{\infty}^{p}+\|X^{-1}_{T}\|_{\infty}^{p}\big]<\infty,

where
X^{-1}(\cdot):=(X_{1}(\cdot)^{-1},X_{2}(\cdot)^{-1})^{\top}
.

Proof.

By
[
68
, Theorem 2.1 a)]
, there exists a unique strong solution
X
to the SDE (
6.2
) such that, a.s.,
X_{1}(t)\geq 0
and
X_{2}(t)\geq 0
for any
t\in[0,\infty)
. Let
T\in(0,\infty)
and
p\in[1,\infty)
be fixed. In this proof, we denote by
C
a positive constant depending only on
p,T,\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}
and the Lipschitz constants of
\theta_{1}
and
\theta_{2}
, which varies from line to line.

We first show that
\mathbb{E}[\|X_{T}\|^{p}_{\infty}]<\infty
. To do so, it suffices to show that
\mathbb{E}[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}(X_{1}(t)+X_{2}(t))^{p}]<\infty
. Observe that

\displaystyle X_{1}(t)+X_{2}(t)

\displaystyle=\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}+\int^{t}_{0}\big\{\kappa_{1}-X_{1}(s)\big\}\,\mathrm{d}s+\int^{t}_{0}\theta_{1}(X_{1}(s))\,\mathrm{d}W_{1}(s)+\int^{t}_{0}\theta_{2}(X_{2}(s))\,\mathrm{d}W_{2}(s)

for any
t\in[0,T]
a.s. For each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, define
\tau_{N}:=\inf\{t\geq 0\,|\,X_{1}(t)+X_{2}(t)\geq N\}
and
a_{N}(t):=\mathbb{E}[\sup_{s\in[0,t\wedge\tau_{N}]}(X_{1}(s)+X_{2}(s))^{p}]
for
t\in[0,T]
. Noting that
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
are globally Lipschitz continuous, by using the Burkholder–Davis–Gundy inequality, we get

a_{N}(t)\leq C\left\{1+|\xi|^{p}+\int^{t}_{0}a_{N}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\right\}

for any
t\in[0,T]
. By Gronwall’s inequality, we get
a_{N}(T)\leq C\{1+|\xi|^{p}\}
. Letting
N\to\infty
, Fatou’s lemma yields
\mathbb{E}[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}(X_{1}(t)+X_{2}(t))^{p}]\leq C(1+|\xi|^{p})
, and hence
\mathbb{E}[\|X_{T}\|^{p}_{\infty}]<\infty
.

Next, we show that
\mathbb{P}(\text{$X_{1}(t)>0$ for any $t\in[0,T]$})=1
and that

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}X_{1}(t)^{-p}\right]\leq C\big(1+\xi_{1}^{-p}\big).

(6.3)

For each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, define
\varsigma_{1,N}:=\inf\{t\geq 0\,|\,X_{1}(t)\leq 1/N\}
. Applying Itô’s formula to
X_{1}(t\wedge\varsigma_{1,N})^{-p}
, we obtain

\displaystyle X_{1}(t\wedge\varsigma_{1,N})^{-p}

\displaystyle=\xi_{1}^{-p}+p\int^{t\wedge\varsigma_{1,N}}_{0}F_{p}(X_{1}(s),X_{2}(s))\,\mathrm{d}s-p\int^{t\wedge\varsigma_{1,N}}_{0}X_{1}(s)^{-p-1}\theta_{1}(X_{1}(s))\,\mathrm{d}W_{1}(s)

for any
t\in[0,T]
a.s., where
F_{p}:(0,\infty)\times[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
is defined by

F_{p}(x_{1},x_{2}):=-\kappa_{1}x_{1}^{-p-1}+(\kappa_{2}+1)x_{1}^{-p}-x_{2}x_{1}^{-p+1}+\frac{p+1}{2}x_{1}^{-p-2}|\theta_{1}(x_{1})|^{2}

for
(x_{1},x_{2})\in(0,\infty)\times[0,\infty)
. Since
\theta_{1}:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
is globally Lipschitz continuous and satisfies
\theta_{1}(0)=0
, we have
|\theta_{1}(x_{1})|\leq Cx_{1}
for any
x_{1}\in(0,\infty)
. From this and the positivity of the constant
\kappa_{1}
, we see that the function
F_{p}
is bounded from above on
(0,\infty)\times[0,\infty)
. Thus, by the Burkholder–Davis–Gundy inequality and Young’s inequality, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\in[0,t\wedge\varsigma_{1,N}]}X_{1}(s)^{-p}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\xi_{1}^{-p}+C+C\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int^{t\wedge\varsigma_{1,N}}_{0}X_{1}(s)^{-2p-2}|\theta_{1}(X_{1}(s))|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\xi_{1}^{-p}+C+C\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int^{t\wedge\varsigma_{1,N}}_{0}X_{1}(s)^{-2p}\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\xi_{1}^{-p}+C+C\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int^{t\wedge\varsigma_{1,N}}_{0}X_{1}(s)^{-p}\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}\left(\sup_{s\in[0,t\wedge\varsigma_{1,N}]}X_{1}(s)^{-p}\right)^{1/2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\xi_{1}^{-p}+C+C\int^{t}_{0}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{r\in[0,s\wedge\varsigma_{1,N}]}X_{1}(r)^{-p}\right]\,\mathrm{d}s+\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\in[0,t\wedge\varsigma_{1,N}]}X_{1}(s)^{-p}\right],

and hence

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\in[0,t\wedge\varsigma_{1,N}]}X_{1}(s)^{-p}\right]\leq C\left\{1+\xi_{1}^{-p}+\int^{t}_{0}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{r\in[0,s\wedge\varsigma_{1,N}]}X_{1}(r)^{-p}\right]\,\mathrm{d}s\right\}

for any
t\in[0,T]
. By Gronwall’s inequality, we get

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\in[0,T\wedge\varsigma_{1,N}]}X_{1}(s)^{-p}\right]\leq C\big(1+\xi_{1}^{-p}\big).

This implies that
\mathbb{P}(\varsigma_{1,N}>T)\to 1
as
N\to\infty
,
and hence,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.,
X_{1}(t)>0
for any
t\in[0,T]
. Also, by letting
N\to\infty
in the above estimate and using Fatou’s lemma, we obtain (
6.3
).

Lastly, we show that
\mathbb{P}(\text{$X_{2}(t)>0$ for any $t\in[0,T]$})=1
and that

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\in[0,T]}X_{2}(s)^{-p}\right]\leq C\big(1+|\xi^{-1}|^{p}+|\xi|^{p+2}\big).

(6.4)

Define
\varsigma_{2,N}:=\inf\{t\geq 0\,|\,X_{2}(t)\leq 1/N\}
for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Applying Itô’s formula to
X_{2}(t\wedge\varsigma_{2,N})^{-p}
, we have

\displaystyle X_{2}(t\wedge\varsigma_{2,N})^{-p}

\displaystyle=\xi_{2}^{-p}+p\int^{t\wedge\varsigma_{2,N}}_{0}G_{p}(X_{1}(s),X_{2}(s))\,\mathrm{d}s-p\int^{t\wedge\varsigma_{2,N}}_{0}X_{2}(s)^{-p-1}\theta_{2}(X_{2}(s))\,\mathrm{d}W_{2}(s)

for any
t\in[0,T]
a.s., where
G_{p}:(0,\infty)\times(0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
is defined by

G_{p}(x_{1},x_{2}):=-\kappa_{2}x_{1}x_{2}^{-p-1}+x_{2}^{-p}x_{1}^{2}+\frac{p+1}{2}x_{2}^{-p-2}|\theta_{2}(x_{2})|^{2}

for
(x_{1},x_{2})\in(0,\infty)\times(0,\infty)
. Since
\kappa_{2}>0
and
|\theta_{2}(x_{2})|\leq Cx_{2}
for any
x_{2}\in(0,\infty)
, by using Young’s inequality, we see that
G_{p}(x_{1},x_{2})\leq C(x_{1}^{p+2}+x_{1}^{-p})
for any
(x_{1},x_{2})\in(0,\infty)\times(0,\infty)
. In the above arguments, we have shown that

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}X_{1}(t)^{p+2}\right]\leq C(1+|\xi|^{p+2})\ \ \text{and}\ \ \mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}X_{1}(t)^{-p}\right]\leq C(1+\xi_{1}^{-p}).

Therefore, by the same argument using the Burkholder–Davis–Gundy inequality as above, we get

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\in[0,T\wedge\varsigma_{2,N}]}X_{2}(s)^{-p}\right]\leq C\big(1+|\xi^{-1}|^{p}+|\xi|^{p+2}\big).

This implies that
\mathbb{P}(\varsigma_{2,N}>T)\to 1
as
N\to\infty
,
and hence,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.,
X_{2}(t)>0
for any
t\in[0,T]
. Also, by letting
N\to\infty
in the above estimate and using Fatou’s lemma, we obtain (
6.4
). This completes the proof.
∎

The following result is an immediate consequence of
Lemma

6.2
and
Corollary

3.3
(i). Here, we require that the functions
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
are non-degenerate on
(0,\infty)
in a suitable sense as specified below.

Theorem 6.3
.

Let
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\in(0,\infty)
be fixed constants, and let
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
be two globally Lipschitz continuous functions with
\theta_{1}(0)=\theta_{2}(0)=0
and
\sup_{x\in[0,\infty)}|\theta_{2}(x)|<\infty
. Assume that there exist constants
C,r\in(0,\infty)
such that

|\theta_{1}(x)|\wedge|\theta_{2}(x)|\geq\frac{1}{C(1+x^{r}+x^{-r})}

for any
x\in(0,\infty)
. Let
(X,W,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
be the weak solution of the SDE (
6.2
) on
D=(0,\infty)^{2}
with initial condition
X(0)=\xi\in D
.
For each
T\in(0,\infty)
and
\pi\in\Pi_{T}
, let
X^{\pi}
be the Euler–Maruyama scheme defined on
(\Omega^{\pi},\mathcal{F}^{\pi},\mathbb{P}^{\pi})
and given by (
4.8
) with initial condition
X^{\pi}(t_{0})=\xi
.
Then, for any
\gamma\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
, there exists a constant
C_{\gamma}\in(0,\infty)
such that

d_{\mathrm{LP}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T}),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}])\big)\leq C_{\gamma}|\pi|^{\gamma}

for any
\pi\in\Pi_{T}
.

6.3
Aït–Sahalia-type interest rate model with delay

Consider the following Aït–Sahalia-type interest rate model with delay:

\begin{cases}\mathrm{d}X(t)=\big\{\kappa_{-1}X(t)^{-1}-\kappa_{0}+\kappa_{1}X(t)-\kappa_{2}X(t)^{\rho}\big\}\,\mathrm{d}t+\eta(X(t-\tau))X(t)^{\theta}\,\mathrm{d}W(t),\ t\in[0,\infty),\\
X(t)=\xi(t),\ \ t\in[-\tau,0],\end{cases}

(6.5)

where
\xi:[-\tau,0]\to(0,\infty)
is a given continuous function,
\kappa_{-1},\kappa_{0},\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\in(0,\infty)
and
\rho,\theta\in(1,\infty)
are parameters,
\eta:(0,\infty)\to(0,\infty)
is a measurable function, and
\tau\in(0,\infty)
is a constant which represents the length of delay. The term
\eta(X(t-\tau))
represents the past-level-dependent volatility function. Observe that the equation (
6.5
) has strongly nonlinear terms
X(t)^{-1}
,
X(t)^{\rho}
and
X(t)^{\theta}
in both drift and diffusion coefficients as well as the delay term
\eta(X(t-\tau))
in the diffusion coefficient. The equation (
6.5
) can be seen as an SDDE (
4.9
) on
D=(0,\infty)
with
d=n=1
and coefficients
\bar{b},\bar{\sigma}:D\times D\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

\bar{b}(x,y)=\kappa_{-1}x^{-1}-\kappa_{0}+\kappa_{1}x-\kappa_{2}x^{\rho},\ \ \bar{\sigma}(x,y)=\eta(y)x^{\theta},\ \ x,y\in D.

Hence, the equation (
6.5
) fits into our framework; see
Section

4.4.2
.

The standard Aït–Sahalia model (that is, (
6.5
) with
\eta
being constant) has been applied in the field of mathematical finance
[
2
]
as an interest rate model.
Coffie and Mao
[
20
]
study the Aït–Sahalia model with delay (
6.5
) and prove that, under the assumptions that
\eta
is bounded and measurable and that
1+\rho>2\theta
, the equation has a unique strong solution
X
such that, a.s.,
X(t)\in D
for any
t\in[0,\infty)
(see
[
20
, Theorem 2.3]
).

Due to the strong nonlinear terms as well as the delay term, existing results on the standard Euler–Maruyama scheme cannot be applied to (
6.5
). In order to overcome this difficulty, in the standard Aït–Sahalia model without delay, Szpruch, Mao, Higham and Pan
[
72
]
and Neuenkirch and Szpruch
[
58
]
consider backward(-type) Euler–Maruyama schemes, and Chassagneux, Jacquier and Mihaylov
[
18
]
consider a truncated Euler–Maruyama scheme. In the case with delay, Coffie and Mao
[
20
]
consider a truncated Euler–Maruyama scheme.

We will apply
Corollary

3.3
(i) to this setting.
To do so, we need to check the moment condition (
3.8
).

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
\kappa_{-1},\kappa_{0},\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\in(0,\infty)
,
\rho,\theta\in(1,\infty)
and
\tau\in(0,\infty)
be fixed constants, let
\eta:(0,\infty)\to(0,\infty)
be a bounded measurable function, and let
\xi:[-\tau,0]\to(0,\infty)
be a given continuous function. Assume that
1+\rho>2\theta
. Then, there exists a unique strong solution
X
of the SDDE (
6.5
) on
D=(0,\infty)
. Furthermore, for any
T\in(0,\infty)
and
p\in[1,\infty)
, it holds that

\mathbb{E}\big[\|X_{T}\|_{\infty}^{p}+\|X^{-1}_{T}\|_{\infty}^{p}\big]<\infty.

Proof.

By
[
20
, Theorem 2.3]
, there exists a unique strong solution
X
of the SDDE (
6.5
) on
D=(0,\infty)
. Furthermore,
[
20
, Lemmas 2.4 and 2.5]
show that

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}X(t)^{p}\right]+\sup_{t\in[0,\infty)}\mathbb{E}\big[X(t)^{p}\big]+\sup_{t\in[0,\infty)}\mathbb{E}\big[X(t)^{-p}\big]<\infty\ \text{for any $T\in(0,\infty)$ and $p\in[1,\infty)$}.\end{split}

(6.6)

Thus, it remains to show that

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}X(t)^{-p}\right]<\infty

(6.7)

for any
T\in(0,\infty)
and
p\in[1,\infty)
.

Let
T\in(0,\infty)
and
p\in[1,\infty)
be fixed. Then, by Itô’s formula, we get

X(t)^{-p}=\xi(0)^{-p}+p\int^{t}_{0}F_{p}(X(s),X(s-\tau))\,\mathrm{d}s-p\int^{t}_{0}\eta(X(s-\tau))X(s)^{-p-1+\theta}\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}

(6.8)

for
t\in[0,T]
, where

F_{p}(x,y):=-\kappa_{-1}x^{-p-2}+\kappa_{0}x^{-p-1}-\kappa_{1}x^{-p}+\kappa_{2}x^{-p-1+\rho}+\frac{p+1}{2}\eta(y)^{2}x^{-p-2+2\theta}

for
(x,y)\in(0,\infty)\times(0,\infty)
. Applying the Burkholder–Davis–Gundy inequality to (
6.8
) and noting that
\eta
is bounded, we obtain (
6.7
) from the estimate (
6.6
). This completes the proof.
∎

By
Lemma

6.4
and
Corollary

3.3
(i), we immediately obtain the following result.

Theorem 6.5
.

Let
\kappa_{-1},\kappa_{0},\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\in(0,\infty)
,
\rho,\theta\in(1,\infty)
and
\tau\in(0,\infty)
be fixed constants, let
\eta:(0,\infty)\to(0,\infty)
be a bounded measurable function, and let
\xi:[-\tau,0]\to(0,\infty)
be a given continuous function. Assume that

1+\rho>2\theta
. Furthermore, assume that there exist constants
C,r\in(0,\infty)
and
\alpha_{\eta}\in(\frac{1}{2},1]
such that

|\xi(t)-\xi(s)|\leq C(t-s)^{1/2}

for any
-\tau\leq s\leq t\leq 0
, and

|\eta(x)-\eta(y)|\leq C\left(1+x^{r}+x^{-r}+y^{r}+y^{-r}\right)|x-y|^{\alpha_{\eta}},\ \ \eta(x)\geq\frac{1}{C\left(1+x^{r}+x^{-r}\right)},

for any
x,y\in(0,\infty)
. Let
(X,W,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
be the weak solution of the SDDE (
6.5
) on
D=(0,\infty)
.
For each
T\in(0,\infty)
and
\pi\in\Pi_{T}
, let
X^{\pi}
be the Euler–Maruyama scheme defined on
(\Omega^{\pi},\mathcal{F}^{\pi},\mathbb{P}^{\pi})
and given by (
2.8
) with initial condition
X^{\pi}(t_{0})=\xi(0)
.
Then, for any
\gamma\in(0,\alpha_{\eta}-\frac{1}{2})
, there exists a constant
C_{\gamma}\in(0,\infty)
such that

d_{\mathrm{LP}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T}),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}])\big)\leq C_{\gamma}|\pi|^{\gamma}

for any
\pi\in\Pi_{T}
.

6.4
Stochastic delay differential neoclassical growth model

The examination of economic growth models is one of the most frequently discussed issues in mathematical economics. Here, we consider the following
n
-connected stochastic delay differential neoclassical growth model:

\begin{cases}\mathrm{d}X_{i}(t)=\left\{-a_{i}X_{i}(t)+\sum_{j\neq i}b_{i,j}X_{j}(t)+c_{i}X_{i}(t-\tau_{i})^{\gamma_{i}}e^{-\delta_{i}X_{i}(t-\tau_{i})}\right\}\,\mathrm{d}t+\theta_{i}X_{i}(t)\,\mathrm{d}W_{i}(t),\ \ t\in[0,\infty),\\
X_{i}(t)=\xi_{i}(t),\ \ t\in[-\tau_{i},0],\ \ i\in\{1,\dots,n\},\end{cases}

(6.9)

where
a_{i},b_{i,j},c_{i},\gamma_{i},\delta_{i},\tau_{i},\theta_{i}\in(0,\infty)
are given parameters, and
\xi_{i}:[-\tau_{i},0]\to(0,\infty)
is a given continuous function for each
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
. The stochastic delay differential neoclassical growth model (
6.9
) is studied in
[
75
]
for the case of
n=1
, in
[
69
]
for the case of
n=2
and in
[
5
]
for the case of
n=3
. In the model (
6.9
),
X_{i}(t)
stands for the capital per labor at time
t
in the patch
i
,
a_{i}
is the sum of labor growth rate and capital depreciation rate multiplied by average saving rate,
b_{i,j}
is the dispersal coefficient of the capital from patch
j
to patch
i
,
\theta_{i}
denotes noise intensity, and the term
c_{i}X_{i}(t-\tau_{i})^{\gamma_{i}}e^{-\delta_{i}X_{i}(t-\tau_{i})}
describes the delayed reproduction function for patch
i
.
For more detailed economic interpretations, see for example
[
5
,
69
,
75
]
.
When
\gamma_{i}=1
, the model (
6.9
) reduces to the famous Nicholson’s blowflies model
[
77
]
.

The stochastic delay differential neoclassical growth model (
6.9
) can be seen as an SFDE (
1.1
) with data
(D,\mu_{0},b,\sigma)=((0,\infty)^{n},\delta_{\xi(0)},b,\sigma)
, where
b:\Lambda[\{\xi(0)\},(0,\infty)^{n}]\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\sigma:\Lambda[\{\xi(0)\},(0,\infty)^{n}]\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
are given by

\displaystyle b_{i}(t,x)=-a_{i}x_{i}(t)+\sum_{j\neq i}b_{i,j}x_{j}(t)+\bar{b}_{i}(\xi_{i}(t-\tau_{i}))\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[0,\tau_{i})}(t)+\bar{b}_{i}(x_{i}(t-\tau_{i}))\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\tau_{i},\infty)}(t),\ \ i\in\{1,\dots,n\},

\displaystyle\sigma_{i,j}(t,x)=\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{i=j}\theta_{i}x_{i}(t),\ \ i,j\in\{1,\dots,n\},

for
(t,x)\in\Lambda[\{\xi(0)\},(0,\infty)^{n}]
, where
\bar{b}_{i}:(0,\infty)\to(0,\infty)
is defined by

\bar{b}_{i}(\bar{x})=c_{i}\bar{x}^{\gamma_{i}}e^{-\delta_{i}\bar{x}},\ \ \bar{x}\in(0,\infty).

To the best of our knowledge, there is no existing work on numerical approximations for the stochastic delay differential neoclassical growth model (
6.9
).

Notice that (
6.9
) has multiple delay terms. Nevertheless, similar arguments as in
Section

4.4.2
are applicable to this setting. In particular, we can easily show that conditions (G’), (C’) and (E’) in
Corollary

3.3
hold with
D=(0,\infty)^{n}
. We will apply
Corollary

3.3
(i) to this setting.
To do so, we need to check the moment condition (
3.8
).

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
a_{i},b_{i,j},c_{i},\gamma_{i},\delta_{i},\tau_{i},\theta_{i}\in(0,\infty)
with
i,j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
be fixed, and let
\xi_{i}:[-\tau_{i},0]\to(0,\infty)
be a continuous function for each
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
. Then, there exists a unique strong solution
X=(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})^{\top}
of the SDDE (
6.9
) on
D=(0,\infty)^{n}
. Furthermore, for any
p\in[2,\infty)
, there exists a constant
C_{p}\in(0,\infty)
, which depends only on
a_{i},b_{i,j},c_{i},\gamma_{i},\delta_{i},\theta_{i}
with
i,j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
as well as
p
, such that

\mathbb{E}\big[\|X_{T}\|_{\infty}^{p}\big]\leq C_{p}e^{C_{p}T}\big(1+|\xi(0)|^{p}\big)\ \ \text{and}\ \ \mathbb{E}\big[\|X^{-1}_{T}\|_{\infty}^{p}\big]\leq C_{p}e^{C_{p}T}\big(1+|\xi(0)^{-1}|^{p}\big).

(6.10)

for any
T\in(0,\infty)
.

Proof.

Since the coefficients of the SDDE (
6.9
) are locally Lipschitz continuous on
D=(0,\infty)^{n}
, by a standard truncation argument, we see that there exists a pathwise unique maximal local solution
X=(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})^{\top}
; see
[
54
, Theorem 3.1]
.

For each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, define the stopping time

\tau_{N}:=\inf\left\{t\geq 0\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}X(t)\notin\left(\frac{1}{N},N\right)^{n}\right\}

and the progressively measurable set

\Lambda_{N}:=\Lambda\left[\{\xi(0)\},\left(\frac{1}{N},N\right)^{n}\right].

Notice that
(X_{\tau_{N}},W,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
is a weak solution of the SFDE with data
((0,\infty)^{n},\delta_{\xi(0)},\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{\Lambda_{N}}b,\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{\Lambda_{N}}\sigma)
. Noting that the function
y\mapsto y^{\gamma}e^{-\delta y}
is bounded on
(0,\infty)
for any constants
\gamma,\delta>0
, straightforward calculations show that the coefficients
\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{\Lambda_{N}}b
and
\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{\Lambda_{N}}\sigma
satisfy the condition in
Lemma

B.1
(i). Also, thanks to
b_{i,j},c_{i}>0
for each
i,j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, we see that the condition in
Lemma

B.1
(ii) holds as well. Furthermore, the corresponding constant
\widehat{C}
appearing in
Lemma

B.1
depends only on
a_{i},b_{i,j},c_{i},\gamma_{i},\delta_{i},\theta_{i}
with
i,j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
but not on
N
. Hence,
Lemma

B.1
(i) and (ii) yield

\mathbb{E}\big[\|X_{T\wedge\tau_{N}}\|_{\infty}^{p}\big]\leq C_{p}e^{C_{p}T}\big(1+|\xi(0)|^{p}\big)\ \ \text{and}\ \ \mathbb{E}\big[\|X^{-1}_{T\wedge\tau_{N}}\|_{\infty}^{p}\big]\leq C_{p}e^{C_{p}T}\big(1+|\xi(0)^{-1}|^{p}\big)

(6.11)

for any
T\in(0,\infty)
,
p\in[2,\infty)
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Here, the positive constant
C_{p}
depends only on
a_{i},b_{i,j},c_{i},\gamma_{i},\delta_{i},\theta_{i}
with
i,j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
and
p
. The estimate (
6.11
) shows that
\lim_{N\to\infty}\tau_{N}=\infty
a.s. Moreover, letting
N\to\infty
in (
6.11
) and using Fatou’s lemma, we get the desired estimate (
6.10
). This completes the proof.
∎

By
Lemma

6.6
and
Corollary

3.3
(i), we immediately obtain the following result.

Theorem 6.7
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
a_{i},b_{i,j},c_{i},\gamma_{i},\delta_{i},\tau_{i},\theta_{i}\in(0,\infty)
with
i,j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
be fixed. For each
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, let
\xi_{i}:[-\tau_{i},0]\to(0,\infty)
be a continuous function such that

|\xi_{i}(t)-\xi_{i}(s)|\leq C(t-s)^{1/2}

for any
-\tau_{i}\leq s\leq t\leq 0
for some constant
C\in(0,\infty)
. Let
(X,W,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
be the weak solution of the SDDE (
6.9
) on
D=(0,\infty)^{n}
.
For each
T\in(0,\infty)
and
\pi\in\Pi_{T}
, let
X^{\pi}
be the Euler–Maruyama scheme defined on
(\Omega^{\pi},\mathcal{F}^{\pi},\mathbb{P}^{\pi})
and given by (
2.8
) with initial condition
X^{\pi}(t_{0})=\xi(0)
.
Then, for any
\gamma\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
, there exists a constant
C_{\gamma}\in(0,\infty)
such that

d_{\mathrm{LP}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T}),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}])\big)\leq C_{\gamma}|\pi|^{\gamma}

for any
\pi\in\Pi_{T}
.

6.5
Reflected Ornstein–Uhlenbeck process

The reflected Ornstein–Uhlenbeck process is a mean-reversion model having a reflection and plays a crucial role in the field of queueing models with reneging or balking.
Here, as in
[
63
,
76
]
, we consider the reflected Ornstein–Uhlenbeck process on
[0,\infty)
which is defined as the solution of the following reflected SDE:

\mathrm{d}\check{X}(t)=(\kappa_{1}-\kappa_{2}\check{X}(t))\,\mathrm{d}t+\theta\,\mathrm{d}W(t)+\mathrm{d}\Phi_{\check{X}}(t),\ \ t\in[0,\infty),\ \ \check{X}(0)=\xi\in[0,\infty),

(6.12)

where
\kappa_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\kappa_{2},\theta\in(0,\infty)
are given parameters. By
[
78
, Theorem 3.1]
, there exists a unique strong solution to the reflected SDE (
6.12
).
In the context of numerical approximations of reflected SDEs, Lépingle
[
51
]
studies the rate of convergence in the
L^{2}
-sup norm on
[0,T]
by means of the Euler–Maruyama-type scheme (interpolated by the Brownian path).

As mentioned in Section
4.4.3
, the reflected SDE (
6.12
) can be transformed to an SFDE involving the Skorokhod map
\Gamma
given by (
4.13
). The corresponding SFDE is of the following form:

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}X(t)=\left\{\kappa_{1}-\kappa_{2}\Big(X(t)-\min_{s\in[0,t]}(X(s)\wedge 0)\Big)\right\}\,\mathrm{d}t+\theta\,\mathrm{d}W(t),\ \ t\in[0,\infty),\ \ X(0)=\xi.

(6.13)

The above can be seen as an SFDE (
1.1
) with data
(\mathbb{R},\delta_{\xi},b,\sigma)
, where the coefficients
b,\sigma:\Lambda[\{\xi\},\mathbb{R}]\to\mathbb{R}
are given by

b(t,x)=\kappa_{1}-\kappa_{2}\left(x(t)-\min_{s\in[0,t]}(x(s)\wedge 0)\right),\ \ \sigma(t,x)=\theta,

for
(t,x)\in\Lambda[\{\xi\},\mathbb{R}]
. Clearly, the data of the SFDE (
6.13
) satisfies conditions (G’), (C’) and (E’) in
Corollary

3.3
. Furthermore, by using
Lemma

B.1
(i), we see that the solution
X
satisfies
\mathbb{E}[\|X_{T}\|_{\infty}^{p}]<\infty
for any
T\in(0,\infty)
and
p\in[2,\infty)
. Therefore, the moment condition (
3.8
) in
Corollary

3.3
(i) holds, and we immediately obtain the following result.

Theorem 6.8
.

Let
\kappa_{1}\in\mathbb{R},\kappa_{2},\theta\in(0,\infty)
be fixed parameters, and let
(X,W,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
be the weak solution of the SFDE (
6.13
) with initial condition
X(0)=\xi\in[0,\infty)
.
For each
T\in(0,\infty)
and
\pi\in\Pi_{T}
, let
X^{\pi}
be the Euler–Maruyama scheme defined on
(\Omega^{\pi},\mathcal{F}^{\pi},\mathbb{P}^{\pi})
and given by (
4.19
) with initial condition
X^{\pi}(t_{0})=\xi
.
Then, for any
\gamma\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
, there exists a constant
C_{\gamma}\in(0,\infty)
such that

d_{\mathrm{LP}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T}),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}])\big)\leq C_{\gamma}|\pi|^{\gamma}

for any
\pi\in\Pi_{T}
.

6.6
Stochastic Duffing–van der Pol oscillator

The Duffing–van der Pol oscillator is important in the theory of stability and bifurcations of nonlinear dynamical systems (see for example
[
35
]
).
As in
[
67
]
, we consider the system incorporating an affine-linear noise term.
To be specific, let
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\kappa_{3},\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\theta_{3}\in\mathbb{R}
be fixed parameters with
\kappa_{3}\geq 0
, and consider the stochastic Duffing–van der Pol oscillator formally described by

\ddot{x}-\kappa_{1}x+\kappa_{2}\dot{x}+\kappa_{3}\dot{x}x^{2}+x^{3}=\theta_{1}x\dot{W}_{1}+\theta_{2}\dot{x}\dot{W}_{2}+\theta_{3}\dot{W}_{3},\ \ (x(0),\dot{x}(0))=(\xi,\dot{\xi}),

where
\xi,\dot{\xi}\in\mathbb{R}
are initial conditions, and
\dot{W}_{1},\dot{W}_{2},\dot{W}_{3}
are three independent one-dimensional white noises. The above is rewritten as the
2
-dimensional Markovian SDE for
(x,\dot{x})
:

\begin{cases}\mathrm{d}x(t)=\dot{x}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t,\ \ t\in[0,\infty)\\
\mathrm{d}\dot{x}(t)=\left\{\kappa_{1}x(t)-\kappa_{2}\dot{x}(t)-\kappa_{3}\dot{x}(t)x(t)^{2}-x(t)^{3}\right\}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
\hskip 56.9055pt+\theta_{1}x(t)\,\mathrm{d}W_{1}(t)+\theta_{2}\dot{x}(t)\,\mathrm{d}W_{2}(t)+\theta_{3}\,\mathrm{d}W_{3}(t),\ \ t\in[0,\infty),\\
(x(0),\dot{x}(0))=(\xi,\dot{\xi}).\end{cases}

(6.14)

In view of numerical analysis, the SDE (
6.14
) has at least two difficulties, namely, the super-linearity of the drift coefficient and degeneracy of the diffusion coefficient. On the one hand, due to the super-linearity of the drift coefficient, the standard Euler–Maruyama scheme of the above SDE might diverge in the
L^{p}
sense for any
p\in(0,\infty)
(see
[
38
]
). Hutzenthaler and Jentzen
[
37
]
then consider an appropriately modified Euler–Maruyama scheme for (
6.14
) and show its convergence to the true solution
(x,\dot{x})^{\top}
at each fixed time
T\in(0,\infty)
in the
L^{p}
sense for any
p\in(0,\infty)
. However, they do not obtain any convergence rate for it. On the other hand, due to the degeneracy of the diffusion coefficient, our main results cannot be applied to the SDE (
6.14
) directly.

In order to overcome the second difficulty mentioned above, as in
Section

4.4.4
, we consider the equivalent stochastic integro-differential equation (
4.21
) for
X=\dot{x}
which is of the following form:

\begin{cases}\mathrm{d}X(t)=\left\{\kappa_{1}\left(\xi+\int^{t}_{0}X(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\right)-\kappa_{2}X(t)-\kappa_{3}X(t)\left(\xi+\int^{t}_{0}X(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{2}-\left(\xi+\int^{t}_{0}X(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{3}\right\}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
\hskip 42.67912pt+\theta_{1}\left(\xi+\int^{t}_{0}X(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\right)\,\mathrm{d}W_{1}(t)+\theta_{2}X(t)\,\mathrm{d}W_{2}(t)+\theta_{3}\mathrm{d}W_{3}(t),\ \ t\in[0,\infty),\\
X(0)=\dot{\xi}.\end{cases}

(6.15)

This transformed equation can be seen as an SFDE (
1.1
) with data
(D,\mu_{0},b,\sigma)=(\mathbb{R},\delta_{\dot{\xi}},b,\sigma)
, where the coefficients
b:\Lambda[\{\dot{\xi}\},\mathbb{R}]\to\mathbb{R}
and
\sigma:\Lambda[\{\dot{\xi}\},\mathbb{R}]\to\mathbb{R}^{1\times 3}
are given by

b(t,\dot{x})=\kappa_{1}\left(\xi+\int^{t}_{0}\dot{x}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\right)-\kappa_{2}\dot{x}(t)-\kappa_{3}\dot{x}(t)\left(\xi+\int^{t}_{0}\dot{x}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{2}-\left(\xi+\int^{t}_{0}\dot{x}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{3}

and

\sigma(t,\dot{x})=\begin{pmatrix}\displaystyle\theta_{1}\left(\xi+\int^{t}_{0}\dot{x}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\right)&\theta_{2}\dot{x}(t)&\theta_{3}\end{pmatrix}

for
(t,\dot{x})\in\Lambda[\{\dot{\xi}\},\mathbb{R}]
. Observe that the diffusion coefficient
\sigma
satisfies condition (E’) in
Corollary

3.3
if
\theta_{3}\neq 0
. Also, the arguments in
Section

4.4.4
show that conditions (G’) and (C’) in
Corollary

3.3
hold as well. We will apply
Corollary

3.3
(i) to the stochastic integro-differential equation (
6.15
) and show the functional type weak convergence of the Euler–Maruyama scheme (
4.23
) to the law of the solution
X
. To do so, we need to check the moment condition (
3.8
).
The following moment estimate is based on
[
37
, Corollary 3.17 and Section 4.3]
.

Lemma 6.9
.

Let
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\kappa_{3},\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\theta_{3}\in\mathbb{R}
with
\kappa_{3}\geq 0
be fixed parameters. Then, for any
\xi,\dot{\xi}\in\mathbb{R}
, there exists a unique strong solution
X
to the stochastic integro-differential equation (
6.15
) with initial condition
X(0)=\dot{\xi}
on
D=\mathbb{R}
. Furthermore, for any
T\in(0,\infty)
and
p\in[1,\infty)
, it holds that

\mathbb{E}\big[\|X_{T}\|^{p}_{\infty}\big]<\infty.

Proof.

As discussed in
Section

4.4.4
, the Markovian SDE (
6.14
) and the stochastic integro-differential equation (
6.15
) are in one-to-one correspondence via
(x,\dot{x})=(\xi+\int^{\cdot}_{0}X(s)\,\mathrm{d}s,X)
. By the arguments in
[
37
, Section 4.3]
, we see that there exists a pathwise unique global solution
(x,\dot{x})
of the SDE (
6.14
), and hence the same is true for the stochastic integro-differential equation (
6.15
). Furthermore,
[
37
, Section 4.3]
shows that

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}\big[|x(t)|^{p}+|\dot{x}(t)|^{p}\big]<\infty\ \ \text{for any $T\in(0,\infty)$ and $p\in[1,\infty)$}.

(6.16)

Let
T\in(0,\infty)
and
p\in[1,\infty)
be fixed. Observe that, by the second equation in (
6.14
),

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|\dot{x}(t)|^{p}

\displaystyle\leq 3^{p-1}|\dot{\xi}|^{p}+3^{p-1}\left(\int^{T}_{0}\Big|\kappa_{1}x(s)-\kappa_{2}\dot{x}(s)-\kappa_{3}\dot{x}(s)x(s)^{2}-x(s)^{3}\Big|\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{p}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+3^{p-1}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\int^{t}_{0}\theta_{1}x(s)\,\mathrm{d}W_{1}(s)+\int^{t}_{0}\theta_{2}\dot{x}(s)\,\mathrm{d}W_{2}(s)+\int^{t}_{0}\theta_{3}\,\mathrm{d}W_{3}(s)\right|^{p}.

Then, using the Burkholder–Davis–Gundy inequality and (
6.16
), we obtain
\mathbb{E}[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|\dot{x}(t)|^{p}]<\infty
, and hence
\mathbb{E}[\|X_{T}\|_{\infty}^{p}]<\infty
. This completes the proof.
∎

By
Lemma

6.9
and
Corollary

3.3
(i), we immediately obtain the following result.

Theorem 6.10
.

Let
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\kappa_{3},\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\theta_{3}\in\mathbb{R}
with
\kappa_{3}\geq 0
be fixed parameters, and assume that
\theta_{3}\neq 0
. Fix
\xi,\dot{\xi}\in\mathbb{R}
, and let
(X,W,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
be the weak solution of the stochastic integro-differential equation (
6.15
) with initial condition
X(0)=\dot{\xi}
on
D=\mathbb{R}
.
For each
T\in(0,\infty)
and
\pi\in\Pi_{T}
, let
X^{\pi}
be the Euler–Maruyama scheme defined on
(\Omega^{\pi},\mathcal{F}^{\pi},\mathbb{P}^{\pi})
and given by (
4.23
) with initial condition
X^{\pi}(t_{0})=\dot{\xi}
.
Then, for any
\gamma\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
, there exist constants
C_{\gamma}\in(0,\infty)
such that

d_{\mathrm{LP}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T}),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}])\big)\leq C_{\gamma}|\pi|^{\gamma}

for any
\pi\in\Pi_{T}
.

6.7
Wright–Fisher diffusion with seed bank

The Wright–Fisher diffusion is a classical probabilistic object in population genetics, which describes the scaling limit of the fraction of the neutral allele in a large haploid population.
Recently, Blath et al.
[
13
]
derived the Wright–Fisher diffusion with seed bank, which incorporates the so-called strong seed-bank effect to the classical Wright–Fisher model.
Here, as in
[
12
]
, we consider the Wright–Fisher diffusion with seed bank including mutation, which is defined as the
[0,1]^{2}
-valued strong Markov process
(X(t),Y(t))^{\top}
solving the following SDE:

\begin{cases}\mathrm{d}X(t)=\big\{\!-\kappa_{1}X(t)+\kappa_{2}(1-X(t))+\kappa_{3}(Y(t)-X(t))\big\}\,\mathrm{d}t+\theta\sqrt{X(t)(1-X(t))}\,\mathrm{d}W(t),\\
\mathrm{d}Y(t)=\big\{\!-\kappa_{1}^{\prime}Y(t)+\kappa_{2}^{\prime}(1-Y(t))+\kappa_{3}^{\prime}(X(t)-Y(t))\big\}\,\mathrm{d}t,\ \ t\in[0,\infty),\\
(X(0),Y(0))^{\top}=(\xi,\eta)^{\top}\in[0,1]^{2},\\
\end{cases}

(6.17)

where
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\kappa_{3},\kappa_{1}^{\prime},\kappa_{2}^{\prime},\kappa_{3}^{\prime}\in[0,\infty)
and
\theta\in(0,\infty)
are given parameters.
Notice that, when
\kappa_{3}=0
, the first line of the above system is reduced to the one-dimensional SDE for the classical Wright–Fisher diffusion. By
[
70
, Theorem 3.2]
, there exists a unique strong solution
(X,Y)^{\top}
to the SDE (
6.17
) such that, a.s.,
(X(t),Y(t))^{\top}\in[0,1]^{2}
for any
t\in[0,\infty)
. Furthermore,
[
12
, Theorem 3.1]
shows that the first component
X
of the solution started from
\xi\in(0,1)
will never hit
0
or
1
if and only if
2(\kappa_{1}\wedge\kappa_{2})\geq\theta^{2}
.

Euler–Maruyama-type approximations for the classical Wright–Fisher diffusion (that is, (
6.17
) with
\kappa_{3}=0
) have been studied in several papers; Neuenkirch and Szpruch
[
58
]
provide a rate of convergence in the
L^{p}
sense by means of the Lamperti-backward Euler–Maruyama scheme for
p\in[2,\frac{4(\kappa_{1}\wedge\kappa_{2})}{3\theta^{2}}]
under the assumption that
2(\kappa_{1}\wedge\kappa_{2})\geq 3\theta^{2}
, and Mickel and Neuenkirch
[
55
]
provide a rate of convergence in the
L^{1}
sense by means of the truncated type Euler–Maruyama scheme under the assumption that
\kappa_{1}\wedge\kappa_{2}>0
(in this case, the Wright–Fisher diffusion can hit
0
and
1
). In the general case of the SDE (
6.17
) with
\kappa_{3}\neq 0
, the pathwise uniqueness result
[
70
, Theorem 3.2]
and
[
43
, Theorem D]
imply that the (standard) Euler–Maruyama scheme (interpolated by the Brownian path) converges in the
L^{2}
-sup sense, but these results do not provide any convergence rates.

Notice that the system (
6.17
) is a degenerate
2
-dimensional Markovian SDE driven by
1
-dimensional Brownian motion
W
. The degeneracy prevents us from applying our main result to the system (
6.17
). However, using the trick in
[
12
]
, which is similar to the arguments discussed in
Section

4.4.4
for the case of stochastic oscillator models, we can convert the system (
6.17
) to an SFDE which fits into our framework. Indeed, since the second line of (
6.17
) is a linear differential equation, the variation of constants formula shows that the second component
Y
of the solution is represented in terms of the first component
X
:

Y(t)=\eta e^{-\kappa^{\prime}t}+\int^{t}_{0}e^{-\kappa^{\prime}(t-s)}\big(\kappa_{2}^{\prime}+\kappa_{3}^{\prime}X(s)\big)\,\mathrm{d}s,\ \ t\in[0,\infty),

(6.18)

where
\kappa^{\prime}:=\kappa_{1}^{\prime}+\kappa_{2}^{\prime}+\kappa_{3}^{\prime}
. Inserting this expression to the first equation in (
6.17
), we get the following stochastic integro-differential equation for
X
:

\begin{split}\mathrm{d}X(t)&=\kappa_{3}\left\{\eta e^{-\kappa^{\prime}t}+\int^{t}_{0}e^{-\kappa^{\prime}(t-s)}\big(\kappa_{2}^{\prime}+\kappa_{3}^{\prime}X(s)\big)\,\mathrm{d}s\right\}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt+\big\{-(\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{3})X(t)+\kappa_{2}(1-X(t))\big\}\,\mathrm{d}t+\theta\sqrt{X(t)(1-X(t))}\,\mathrm{d}W(t),\ t\in[0,\infty),\end{split}

(6.19)

with initial condition
X(0)=\xi
. Notice that
(X,Y)
solves the system (
6.17
) if and only if
X
solves (
6.19
) and
Y
is given by (
6.18
). The delay term in (
6.19
) reveals the underlying age structure of the model; see
[
12
]
. Observe that the equation (
6.19
) with initial condition
X(0)=\xi\in(0,1)
can be seen as an SFDE (
1.1
) with data
(D,\mu_{0},b,\sigma)=((0,1),\delta_{\xi},b,\sigma)
, where the coefficients
b,\sigma:\Lambda[\{\xi\},(0,1)]\to\mathbb{R}
are given by

\begin{split}&b(t,x)=\kappa_{3}\left\{\eta e^{-\kappa^{\prime}t}+\int^{t}_{0}e^{-\kappa^{\prime}(t-s)}\big(\kappa^{\prime}_{2}+\kappa_{3}^{\prime}x(s)\big)\,\mathrm{d}s\right\}-(\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{3})x(t)+\kappa_{2}(1-x(t)),\\
&\sigma(t,x)=\theta\sqrt{x(t)(1-x(t))},\end{split}

(6.20)

for
(t,x)\in\Lambda[\{\xi\},(0,1)]
. The coefficients
b
and
\sigma
defined by (
6.20
) are progressively measurable and satisfy
Assumption

1
with the choices of the exponents
\alpha_{b}=\alpha_{\sigma}=1
, the sets

\begin{split}&D_{\mathrm{G},b;T}(R)=D_{\mathrm{G},\sigma;T}(R)=D_{\mathrm{C},b;T}(R)=D=(0,1),\\
&D_{\mathrm{C},\sigma;T}(R)=D_{\mathrm{E};T}(R)=\left(\frac{1}{R},1-\frac{1}{R}\right),\ \ R\in[1,\infty),\end{split}

(6.21)

and some constants
K_{\mathrm{G},b;T},K_{\mathrm{G},\sigma;T},K_{\mathrm{C},b;T},K_{\mathrm{C},\sigma;T},K_{\mathrm{E};T}
depending only on
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\kappa_{3}
and
\theta
.

As mentioned above, if the parameters satisfy
2(\kappa_{1}\wedge\kappa_{2})\geq\theta^{2}
and
\xi\in D=(0,1)
, then the SDE (
6.17
), and hence also the stochastic integro-differential equation (
6.19
), has a unique solution
X
such that, a.s.,
X(t)\in D
for any
t\in[0,\infty)
; see
[
12
, Theorem 3.1]
. Hence, under the conditions that
2(\kappa_{1}\wedge\kappa_{2})\geq\theta^{2}
and
\xi\in D
,
Theorem

3.1
(i) implies that

\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}]\to X_{T}\ \ \text{weakly on $\mathcal{C}_{T}$ as $|\pi|\downarrow 0$ along $\pi\in\Pi_{T}$ for any $T\in(0,\infty)$},

(6.22)

where
X^{\pi}=(X^{\pi}(t_{k}))^{m}_{k=0}
is the Euler–Maruyama scheme given by (
2.8
) with initial condition
X^{\pi}(t_{0})=\xi
. In order to get a weak convergence order with respect to the Lévy–Prokhorov metric, we use
Theorem

3.1
(iii). To do so, we need to determine the parameters
\vec{\beta}=(\beta_{0},\beta_{\mathrm{G},b},\beta_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},\beta_{\mathrm{C},b},\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},\beta_{\mathrm{E}})\in(0,\infty)^{6}
appearing in the assumption (
3.5
) on the probability of the “rare event”. The following moment estimates play crucial roles for such a purpose.

Lemma 6.11
.

Let
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\kappa_{3},\kappa_{1}^{\prime},\kappa_{2}^{\prime},\kappa_{3}^{\prime}\in[0,\infty)
and
\theta\in(0,\infty)
be given parameters with
2(\kappa_{1}\wedge\kappa_{2})\geq\theta^{2}
. Fix
\eta\in[0,1]
, and let
(X,W,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
be the weak solution of the stochastic integro-differential equation (
6.19
) on
D=(0,1)
with initial condition
X(0)=\xi\in D
.

(i)

Assume that
2\kappa_{2}>\theta^{2}
. Then, for any
p\in(0,\frac{2\kappa_{2}}{\theta^{2}}-1)
, there exists a constant
C_{p}\in(0,\infty)
, which depends only on
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\kappa_{3},\theta
and
p
, such that, for any
T\in(0,\infty)
,

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}X(t)^{-p}+\int^{T}_{0}X(t)^{-p-1}\,\mathrm{d}t\right]\leq C_{p}e^{C_{p}T}\xi^{-p}.

(ii)

Assume that
2\kappa_{1}>\theta^{2}
. Then, for any
p\in(0,\frac{2\kappa_{1}}{\theta^{2}}-1)
, there exists a constant
C_{p}\in(0,\infty)
, which depends only on
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\kappa_{3},\theta
and
p
, such that, for any
T\in(0,\infty)
,

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\big(1-X(t)\big)^{-p}+\int^{T}_{0}\big(1-X(t)\big)^{-p-1}\,\mathrm{d}t\right]\leq C_{p}e^{C_{p}T}\big(1-\xi\big)^{-p}.

Proof.

Notice that the process
1-X
solves the stochastic integro-differential equation (
6.19
) with the parameters
(\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\kappa_{3},\kappa^{\prime}_{1},\kappa^{\prime}_{2},\kappa_{3}^{\prime},\theta,\xi,\eta)
replaced by

(\kappa_{2},\kappa_{1},\kappa_{3},\kappa^{\prime}_{2},\kappa^{\prime}_{1},\kappa_{3}^{\prime},\theta,1-\xi,1-\eta).

Thus, assertions (i) and (ii) are parallel, and we only need to show (i).

Assume that
2\kappa_{2}>\theta^{2}
, and let
p\in(0,\frac{2\kappa_{2}}{\theta^{2}}-1)
be fixed. In this proof, we denote by
C_{p}
a positive constant which depends only on
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\kappa_{3},\theta
and
p
and varies from line to line. By Itô’s formula, it holds that

\begin{split}&X(t)^{-p}+p\left\{\kappa_{2}-\frac{(p+1)\theta^{2}}{2}\right\}\int^{t}_{0}X(s)^{-p-1}\,\mathrm{d}s+p\kappa_{3}\int^{t}_{0}X(s)^{-p-1}Y(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&=\xi^{-p}+p\left\{\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{2}+\kappa_{3}-\frac{(p+1)\theta^{2}}{2}\right\}\int^{t}_{0}X(s)^{-p}\,\mathrm{d}s-p\theta\int^{t}_{0}X(s)^{-p-1/2}\sqrt{1-X(s)}\,\mathrm{d}W(s)\end{split}

(6.23)

for any
t\in[0,\infty)

\mathbb{P}
-a.s., where
Y
is defined by (
6.18
). Notice that the three terms in the left-hand side of (
6.23
) are nonnegative.
For any
N\in\mathbb{N}
, define a stopping time
\tau_{N}
by
\tau_{N}:=\inf\{t\geq 0\,|\,X(t)\leq 1/N\}
. Since
\mathbb{P}(\text{$X(t)\in D$ for any $t\in[0,\infty)$})=1
, we have
\tau_{N}\to\infty
as
N\to\infty
,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. Let
T\in(0,\infty)
, and take an arbitrary
T_{1}\in[0,T]
. On the one hand, letting
t=T_{1}\wedge\tau_{N}
and taking expectations in (
6.23
), we see that

\displaystyle p\left\{\kappa_{2}-\frac{(p+1)\theta^{2}}{2}\right\}\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T_{1}\wedge\tau_{N}}_{0}X(s)^{-p-1}\,\mathrm{d}s\right]

\displaystyle\leq\xi^{-p}+p\left\{\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{2}+\kappa_{3}-\frac{(p+1)\theta^{2}}{2}\right\}\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T_{1}\wedge\tau_{N}}_{0}X(s)^{-p}\,\mathrm{d}s\right].

Since
\kappa_{2}-\frac{(p+1)\theta^{2}}{2}>0
, we get

\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T_{1}\wedge\tau_{N}}_{0}X(s)^{-p-1}\,\mathrm{d}s\right]\leq C_{p}\left\{\xi^{-p}+\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T_{1}\wedge\tau_{N}}_{0}X(s)^{-p}\,\mathrm{d}s\right]\right\}.

(6.24)

On the other hand, taking the supremum with respect to
t\in[0,T_{1}\wedge\tau_{N}]
and then taking expectations in (
6.23
), by the Burkholder–Davis–Gundy inequality, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T_{1}]}X(t\wedge\tau_{N})^{-p}\right]

\displaystyle\leq C_{p}\left\{\xi^{-p}+\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T_{1}\wedge\tau_{N}}_{0}X(s)^{-p}\,\mathrm{d}s\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int^{T_{1}\wedge\tau_{N}}_{0}X(s)^{-2p-1}\big(1-X(s)\big)\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}\right]\right\}.

From this, together with the estimate

\displaystyle C_{p}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int^{T_{1}\wedge\tau_{N}}_{0}X(s)^{-2p-1}\big(1-X(s)\big)\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq C_{p}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sup_{s\in[0,T_{1}]}X(s\wedge\tau_{N})^{-p}\right)^{1/2}\left(\int^{T_{1}\wedge\tau_{N}}_{0}X(s)^{-p-1}\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\in[0,T_{1}]}X(s\wedge\tau_{N})^{-p}\right]+C_{p}\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T_{1}\wedge\tau_{N}}_{0}X(s)^{-p-1}\,\mathrm{d}s\right],

we obtain

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T_{1}]}X(t\wedge\tau_{N})^{-p}\right]&\leq C_{p}\left\{\xi^{-p}+\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T_{1}\wedge\tau_{N}}_{0}X(s)^{-p}\,\mathrm{d}s\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T_{1}\wedge\tau_{N}}_{0}X(s)^{-p-1}\,\mathrm{d}s\right]\right\}.\end{split}

(6.25)

By (
6.24
) and (
6.25
), we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T_{1}]}X(t\wedge\tau_{N})^{-p}\right]

\displaystyle\leq C_{p}\left\{\xi^{-p}+\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T_{1}\wedge\tau_{N}}_{0}X(s)^{-p}\,\mathrm{d}s\right]\right\}

\displaystyle\leq C_{p}\left\{\xi^{-p}+\int^{T_{1}}_{0}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,s]}X(t\wedge\tau_{N})^{-p}\right]\,\mathrm{d}s\right\}.

Noting that
T_{1}\in[0,T]
is arbitrary, Gronwall’s inequality yields that

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}X(t\wedge\tau_{N})^{-p}\right]\leq C_{p}e^{C_{p}T}\xi^{-p}.

From this estimate and (
6.24
) with
T_{1}=T
, letting
N\to\infty
and using Fatou’s lemma, we obtain the desired estimate in assertion (i). This completes the proof.
∎

Combining the above estimates with
Theorem

3.1
(iii), we get the following convergence order for the weak approximation (
6.22
) under the slightly stronger condition
2(\kappa_{1}\wedge\kappa_{2})>\theta^{2}
.

Theorem 6.12
.

Let
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\kappa_{3},\kappa_{1}^{\prime},\kappa_{2}^{\prime},\kappa_{3}^{\prime}\in[0,\infty)
and
\theta\in(0,\infty)
be given parameters with
2(\kappa_{1}\wedge\kappa_{2})>\theta^{2}
. Fix
\eta\in[0,1]
, and let
(X,W,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
be the weak solution of the stochastic integro-differential equation (
6.19
) on
D=(0,1)
with initial condition
X(0)=\xi\in D
.
For each
T\in(0,\infty)
and
\pi\in\Pi_{T}
, let
X^{\pi}
be the Euler–Maruyama scheme defined on
(\Omega^{\pi},\mathcal{F}^{\pi},\mathbb{P}^{\pi})
and given by (
2.8
) with initial condition
X^{\pi}(t_{0})=\xi
.
Then, for any

\gamma\in\left(0,\frac{1}{4}\cdot\frac{2(\kappa_{1}\wedge\kappa_{2})-\theta^{2}}{(\kappa_{1}\wedge\kappa_{2})+\theta^{2}}\right),

there exists a constant
C_{\gamma}\in(0,\infty)
such that

d_{\mathrm{LP}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T}),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}])\big)\leq C_{\gamma}|\pi|^{\gamma}

for any
\pi\in\Pi_{T}
.

Proof.

Recall that the coefficients of the stochastic integro-differential equation (
6.19
) satisfy
Assumption

1
with
\alpha_{b}=\alpha_{\sigma}=1
, the sets
D_{\mathrm{G},b;T},D_{\mathrm{G},\sigma;T},D_{\mathrm{C},b;T},D_{\mathrm{C},\sigma;T},D_{\mathrm{E};T}\subset D=(0,1)
given by (
6.21
), and some constants
K_{\mathrm{G},b;T},K_{\mathrm{G},\sigma;T},K_{\mathrm{C},b;T},K_{\mathrm{C},\sigma;T},K_{\mathrm{E};T}
depending only on
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\kappa_{3}
and
\theta
. In order to apply
Theorem

3.1
(iii), we check the condition (
3.5
). Observe that, for any
\Delta\in(0,1]
and
\vec{R}=(R_{\mathrm{G},b},R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{C},b},R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{E}})\in[1,\infty)^{5}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{t\in[0,T]}\mathrm{dist}\!\left(X(t),\mathbb{R}\setminus D_{T}(\vec{R})\right)\leq\Delta\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(\text{$X(t)\leq\frac{1}{R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}\wedge R_{\mathrm{E}}}+\Delta$ or $X(t)\geq 1-\frac{1}{R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}\wedge R_{\mathrm{E}}}-\Delta$ for some $t\in[0,T]$}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}X(t)^{-1}\geq\left(\frac{1}{R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}\wedge R_{\mathrm{E}}}+\Delta\right)^{-1}\right)+\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\big(1-X(t)\big)^{-1}\geq\left(\frac{1}{R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}\wedge R_{\mathrm{E}}}+\Delta\right)^{-1}\right),

and thus, by Markov’s inequality and
Lemma

6.11
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{t\in[0,T]}\mathrm{dist}\!\left(X(t),\mathbb{R}\setminus D_{T}(\vec{R})\right)\leq\Delta\right)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{1}{R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}\wedge R_{\mathrm{E}}}+\Delta\right)^{p}\left\{\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}X(t)^{-p}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\big(1-X(t)\big)^{-p}\right]\right\}

\displaystyle\leq C_{p}\max\left\{\Delta^{p},R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}^{-p},R_{\mathrm{E}}^{-p}\right\},

for any
p\in(0,\frac{2(\kappa_{1}\wedge\kappa_{2})}{\theta^{2}}-1)
. Here,
C_{p}\in(0,\infty)
is a constant which does not depend on
\Delta
or
\vec{R}
. The above estimate shows that (
3.5
) holds for any
\vec{\beta}=(\beta_{0},\beta_{\mathrm{G},b},\beta_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},\beta_{\mathrm{C},b},\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},\beta_{\mathrm{E}})
with

\beta_{\mathrm{G},b},\beta_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},\beta_{\mathrm{C},b}\in(0,\infty),\ \beta_{0},\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},\beta_{\mathrm{E}}\in\left(0,\frac{2(\kappa_{1}\wedge\kappa_{2})}{\theta^{2}}-1\right).

Concerning the constants
\beta_{*}
and
\gamma_{*}
defined by (
3.7
) and (
3.6
) , we have

\begin{split}\beta_{*}&=\min\left\{\beta_{0},\frac{1}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+\beta_{\mathrm{C},b}^{-1}},\frac{1}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+2\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}^{-1}}\right\}\to\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{2(\kappa_{1}\wedge\kappa_{2})-\theta^{2}}{(\kappa_{1}\wedge\kappa_{2})+\theta^{2}}\end{split}

and

\begin{split}\gamma_{*}&=\min\left\{\frac{1}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+\beta_{\mathrm{C},b}^{-1}+\beta_{\mathrm{G},b}^{-1}},\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+\beta_{\mathrm{C},b}^{-1}+\beta_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}^{-1}},\right.\\
&\hskip 42.67912pt\left.\frac{2}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+2\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}^{-1}+2\beta_{\mathrm{G},b}^{-1}+\beta_{*}^{-1}},\frac{1}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+2\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}^{-1}+2\beta_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}^{-1}+\beta_{*}^{-1}}\right\}\\
&\to\frac{1}{4}\cdot\frac{2(\kappa_{1}\wedge\kappa_{2})-\theta^{2}}{(\kappa_{1}\wedge\kappa_{2})+\theta^{2}}\end{split}

as
\beta_{\mathrm{G},b},\beta_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},\beta_{\mathrm{C},b}\to\infty
and
\beta_{0},\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},\beta_{\mathrm{E}}\to\frac{2(\kappa_{1}\wedge\kappa_{2})}{\theta^{2}}-1
. Therefore, noting
Remark

3.2
, by
Theorem

3.1
(iii), we get the desired estimate. This completes the proof.
∎

6.8
Multi-dimensional polynomial diffusion

The standard Wright–Fisher diffusion (that is, (
6.19
) with
\kappa_{3}=0
) is a kind of one-dimensional polynomial diffusions. Now we consider the following
n
-dimensional polynomial diffusion with
n\geq 2
:

\mathrm{d}X(t)=-\kappa X(t)\,\mathrm{d}t+\theta\,\sqrt{1-|X(t)|^{2}}I_{n\times n}\,\mathrm{d}W(t),\ \ t\in[0,\infty),\ \ X(0)=\xi,

(6.26)

where
\kappa,\theta\in(0,\infty)
are given parameters, and
W
denotes an
n
-dimensional Brownian motion. The solution
X
can be seen as a multi-dimensional extension of the Jacobi process. The above SDE was first investigated in
[
71
]
and then applied in the field of mathematical finance in
[
27
]
.
It is known that the SDE (
6.26
) has a unique strong solution
X
on the closed unit ball
\overline{D}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\,|\,|x|\leq 1\}
when the parameters satisfy
\kappa\geq(\sqrt{2}-1)\theta^{2}
for any initial condition
\xi\in\overline{D}
(see
[
49
, Theorem 4.6]
).
Moreover, it is also known that, when
\xi\in D=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\,|\,|x|<1\}
,
\mathbb{P}(\text{$X(t)\in D$ for any $t\in[0,\infty)$})=1
if and only if
\kappa\geq\theta^{2}
(see
[
71
, Proposition 2]
and
[
49
, Proposition 2.2]
).

The pathwise uniqueness result
[
49
, Theorem 4.6]
and
[
43
, Theorem D]
imply that the (standard) Euler–Maruyama scheme (interpolated by the Brownian path) for the SDE (
6.26
) converges in the
L^{2}
-sup sense when the parameters satisfy
\kappa\geq(\sqrt{2}-1)\theta^{2}
, but these results do not provide any convergence rates.
Numerical approximations for the polynomial diffusion (
6.26
) are studied in
[
57
]
, where the authors provide a convergence rate in the
L^{2}
sense for the semi-implicit Euler–Maruyama scheme under the condition
\kappa>3\theta^{2}
.

Let
\kappa\geq\theta^{2}
and
\xi\in D=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\,|\,|x|<1\}
. As discussed in
Section

4.4.1
, the
n
-dimensional Markovian SDE (
6.26
) on
D
fits into the framework of the present paper. More precisely, the data satisfies
Assumption

1
with the choices of the exponents
\alpha_{b}=\alpha_{\sigma}=1
, the sets

\begin{split}&D_{\mathrm{G},b;T}(R)=D_{\mathrm{G},\sigma;T}(R)=D_{\mathrm{C},b;T}(R)=D,\\
&D_{\mathrm{C},\sigma;T}(R)=D_{\mathrm{E};T}(R)=\left\{x\in D\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}|x|<\left(1-\frac{1}{R^{2}}\right)^{1/2}\right\},\ \ R\in[1,\infty),\end{split}

(6.27)

and some constants
K_{\mathrm{G},b;T},K_{\mathrm{G},\sigma;T},K_{\mathrm{C},b;T},K_{\mathrm{C},\sigma;T},K_{\mathrm{E};T}
depending only on
n
,
\kappa
and
\theta
.
Hence,
Theorem

3.1
(i) shows that

\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}]\to X_{T}\ \ \text{weakly on $\mathcal{C}_{T}$ as $|\pi|\downarrow 0$ along $\pi\in\Pi_{T}$ for any $T\in(0,\infty)$},

(6.28)

where
X^{\pi}=(X^{\pi}(t_{k}))^{m}_{k=0}
is the standard Euler–Maruyama scheme given by (
4.8
) with initial condition
X^{\pi}(t_{0})=\xi
. In order to get a weak convergence order with respect to the Lévy–Prokhorov metric, we use
Theorem

3.1
(iii). As before, a moment estimate corresponding to the “rare event” appearing in (
3.5
) plays a crucial role in determining the parameters
\vec{\beta}=(\beta_{0},\beta_{\mathrm{G},b},\beta_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},\beta_{\mathrm{C},b},\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},\beta_{\mathrm{E}})\in(0,\infty)^{6}
. Here, we require a slightly stronger condition
\kappa>\theta^{2}
.

Lemma 6.13
.

Let
\kappa,\theta\in(0,\infty)
satisfy
\kappa>\theta^{2}
, and let
(X,W,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
be the weak solution of the SDE (
6.26
) on
D=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\,|\,|x|<1\}
with initial condition
X(0)=\xi\in D
. Then, for any
p\in(0,\frac{\kappa}{\theta^{2}}-1)
, there exists a constant
C_{p}\in(0,\infty)
, which depends only on
n,\kappa,\theta
and
p
such that, for any
T\in(0,\infty)
,

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\big(1-|X(t)|^{2}\big)^{-p}+\int^{T}_{0}\big(1-|X(t)|^{2}\big)^{-p-1}\,\mathrm{d}t\right]\leq C_{p}e^{C_{p}T}\big(1-|\xi|^{2}\big)^{-p}.

Proof.

Define
\widetilde{X}:=|X|^{2}
. As in the proof of
[
57
, Proposition 2.4]
, we see that
\widetilde{X}
solves the SDE:

\begin{cases}\mathrm{d}\widetilde{X}(t)=\big\{\!-2\kappa\widetilde{X}(t)+n\theta^{2}(1-\widetilde{X}(t))\big\}\,\mathrm{d}t+2\theta\sqrt{\widetilde{X}(t)(1-\widetilde{X}(t))}\,\mathrm{d}\widetilde{W}(t),\ \ t\in[0,\infty),\\
\widetilde{X}(0)=|\xi|^{2},\end{cases}

for some one-dimensional Brownian motion
\widetilde{W}
. The above is a (classical) Wright–Fisher diffusion (
6.19
) with
\kappa_{3}=0
and parameters
(\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\theta)
replaced by
(2\kappa,n\theta^{2},2\theta)
. Therefore, by
Lemma

6.11
(ii), we get the desired estimate.
2
2

2

Although
\widetilde{X}(0)=0
when
\xi=0
, the proof of
Lemma

6.11
(ii) applies unchanged: it uses Itô’s formula for
(1-\widetilde{X})^{-p}
and stopping times that localize only near the boundary
1
, so no strict positivity of
\widetilde{X}
is required.

∎

By using
Lemma

6.13
and
Theorem

3.1
(iii), we can obtain the following convergence order for the weak approximation (
6.28
) under the slightly stronger condition
\kappa>\theta^{2}
.

Theorem 6.14
.

Let
\kappa,\theta\in(0,\infty)
satisfy
\kappa>\theta^{2}
, and let
(X,W,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
be the weak solution of the multi-dimensional polynomial diffusion (
6.26
) on
D=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\,|\,|x|<1\}
with initial condition
X(0)=\xi\in D
.
For each
T\in(0,\infty)
and
\pi\in\Pi_{T}
, let
X^{\pi}
be the Euler–Maruyama scheme defined on
(\Omega^{\pi},\mathcal{F}^{\pi},\mathbb{P}^{\pi})
and given by (
4.8
) with initial condition
X^{\pi}(t_{0})=\xi
.
Then, for any

\gamma\in\left(0,\frac{\kappa-\theta^{2}}{2\kappa+\theta^{2}}\right),

there exists a constant
C_{\gamma}\in(0,\infty)
such that

d_{\mathrm{LP}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T}),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}])\big)\leq C_{\gamma}|\pi|^{\gamma}

for any
\pi\in\Pi_{T}
.

Proof.

Noting (
6.27
), for any
\Delta\in(0,1]
and
\vec{R}=(R_{\mathrm{G},b},R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{C},b},R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{E}})\in[1,\infty)^{5}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{t\in[0,T]}\mathrm{dist}\!\left(X(t),\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus D_{T}(\vec{R})\right)\leq\Delta\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(\text{$|X(t)|\geq\left(1-\frac{1}{R^{2}_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}\wedge R^{2}_{\mathrm{E}}}\right)^{1/2}-\Delta$ for some $t\in[0,T]$}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\text{$1-|X(t)|^{2}\leq\frac{1}{R^{2}_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}\wedge R^{2}_{\mathrm{E}}}+3\Delta$ for some $t\in[0,T]$}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\big(1-|X(t)|^{2}\big)^{-1}\geq\left(\frac{1}{R^{2}_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}\wedge R^{2}_{\mathrm{E}}}+3\Delta\right)^{-1}\right),

and thus, by Markov’s inequality and
Lemma

6.13
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{t\in[0,T]}\mathrm{dist}\!\left(X(t),\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus D_{T}(\vec{R})\right)\leq\Delta\right)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{1}{R^{2}_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}\wedge R^{2}_{\mathrm{E}}}+3\Delta\right)^{p}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\big(1-|X(t)|^{2}\big)^{-p}\right]

\displaystyle\leq C_{p}\max\left\{\Delta^{p},R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}^{-2p},R_{\mathrm{E}}^{-2p}\right\},

for any
p\in(0,\frac{\kappa}{\theta^{2}}-1)
. Here,
C_{p}\in(0,\infty)
is a constant which does not depend on
\Delta
or
\vec{R}
. The above estimate shows that (
3.5
) holds for any
\vec{\beta}=(\beta_{0},\beta_{\mathrm{G},b},\beta_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},\beta_{\mathrm{C},b},\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},\beta_{\mathrm{E}})
with
