\beta_{0}\in\left(0,\frac{\kappa}{\theta^{2}}-1\right),\ \beta_{\mathrm{G},b},\beta_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},\beta_{\mathrm{C},b}\in(0,\infty),\ \beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},\beta_{\mathrm{E}}\in\left(0,\frac{2\kappa}{\theta^{2}}-2\right).

Concerning the constants
\beta_{*}
and
\gamma_{*}
defined by (
3.7
) and (
3.6
), we have

\begin{split}\beta_{*}&=\min\left\{\beta_{0},\frac{1}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+\beta_{\mathrm{C},b}^{-1}},\frac{1}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+2\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}^{-1}}\right\}\to\frac{2(\kappa-\theta^{2})}{2\kappa+\theta^{2}}\end{split}

and

\begin{split}\gamma_{*}&=\min\left\{\frac{1}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+\beta_{\mathrm{C},b}^{-1}+\beta_{\mathrm{G},b}^{-1}},\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+\beta_{\mathrm{C},b}^{-1}+\beta_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}^{-1}},\right.\\
&\hskip 42.67912pt\left.\frac{2}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+2\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}^{-1}+2\beta_{\mathrm{G},b}^{-1}+\beta_{*}^{-1}},\frac{1}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+2\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}^{-1}+2\beta_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}^{-1}+\beta_{*}^{-1}}\right\}\\
&\to\frac{\kappa-\theta^{2}}{2\kappa+\theta^{2}}\end{split}

as
\beta_{0}\to\frac{\kappa}{\theta^{2}}-1
,
\beta_{\mathrm{G},b},\beta_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},\beta_{\mathrm{C},b}\to\infty
and
\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},\beta_{\mathrm{E}}\to\frac{2\kappa}{\theta^{2}}-2
. Therefore, noting
Remark

3.2
, by
Theorem

3.1
(iii), we get the desired estimate. This completes the proof.
∎

6.9
Volatility process in the
3/2
-stochastic volatility model

We consider the following one-dimensional Markovian SDE on
D=(0,\infty)
:

\mathrm{d}X(t)=\kappa_{1}X(t)(\kappa_{2}-X(t))\,\mathrm{d}t+\theta X(t)^{3/2}\,\mathrm{d}W(t),\ \ t\in[0,\infty),\ \ X(0)=\xi\in D,

(6.29)

where
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\theta\in(0,\infty)
are given parameters. The above SDE describes the dynamics of the volatility process in the so-called
3/2
-stochastic volatility model
[
33
]
.
By Feller’s test (cf.
[
44
, Chapter 5, Theorem 5.29]
), we can show that the SDE (
6.29
) admits a unique strong solution
X
satisfying
\mathbb{P}(\text{$X(t)\in D$ for any $t\in[0,\infty)$})=1
if and only if the parameters satisfy
2\kappa_{1}+\theta^{2}\geq 0
. In the literature on numerical approximations of the SDE (
6.29
), Neuenkirch and Szpruch
[
58
, Proposition 3.2]
provide a convergence rate in
L^{p}
by means of the Lamperti-backward Euler–Maruyama scheme for
p\in[1,(\kappa_{1}+\theta^{2})/(3\theta^{2}))
under the assumption that
\kappa_{1}-2\theta^{2}>0
, and Sabanis
[
65
, Theorem 2 and Appendix]
provide a convergence rate in
L^{p}
by means of the tamed Euler–Maruyama scheme for
p\in[2,(2\kappa_{1}+\theta^{2})/(3\theta^{2})]
under the assumption that
2\kappa_{1}-5\theta^{2}>0
.

Let
2\kappa_{1}+\theta^{2}\geq 0
. As discussed in
Section

4.4.1
, the one-dimensional Markovian SDE (
6.29
) on
D=(0,\infty)
fits into the framework of the present paper. More precisely, the data satisfies
Assumption

1
with the choices of the exponents
\alpha_{b}=\alpha_{\sigma}=1
, the sets

\begin{split}D_{\mathrm{G},b;T}(R)&=(0,2R^{1/2}),\ D_{\mathrm{G},\sigma;T}(R)=(0,2R^{2/3}),\\
D_{\mathrm{C},b;T}(R)&=(0,2R),\ D_{\mathrm{C},\sigma;T}(R)=(0,2R^{2}),\ D_{\mathrm{E};T}(R)=\left(\frac{1}{R^{2/3}},\infty\right),\ \ R\in[1,\infty),\end{split}

(6.30)

and some constants
K_{\mathrm{G},b;T},K_{\mathrm{G},\sigma;T},K_{\mathrm{C},b;T},K_{\mathrm{C},\sigma;T},K_{\mathrm{E};T}
depending only on
\kappa_{1}
,
\kappa_{2}
and
\theta
; the multiplication by
2
in (
6.30
) is just for a technical reason to make the computation in the proof of
Theorem

6.16
below simple.

Since
\mathbb{P}(\text{$X(t)\in D$ for any $t\in[0,\infty)$})=1
,
Theorem

3.1
(i) shows that

\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}]\to X_{T}\ \ \text{weakly on $\mathcal{C}_{T}$ as $|\pi|\downarrow 0$ along $\pi\in\Pi_{T}$ for any $T\in(0,\infty)$},

(6.31)

where
X^{\pi}=(X^{\pi}(t_{k}))^{m}_{k=0}
is the standard Euler–Maruyama scheme given by (
4.8
) with initial condition
X^{\pi}(t_{0})=\xi
. In order to get a weak convergence order with respect to the Lévy–Prokhorov metric, we use
Theorem

3.1
(iii). To do so, we first investigate the following moment estimates corresponding to the “rare event” appearing in (
3.5
). Here, we require a slightly stronger condition
2\kappa_{1}+\theta^{2}>0
.

Lemma 6.15
.

Let
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\theta\in(0,\infty)
satisfy
2\kappa_{1}+\theta^{2}>0
, and let
(X,W,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
be the weak solution of the SDE (
6.29
) on
D=(0,\infty)
with initial condition
X(0)=\xi\in D
. Let
T\in(0,\infty)
be fixed. Then the following hold:

(i)

For any
p\in(0,\frac{2\kappa_{1}}{\theta^{2}}+1)
, there exists a constant
C_{p}\in(0,\infty)
, which depends only on
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\theta
and
p
, such that

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}X(t)^{p}+\int^{T}_{0}X(t)^{p+1}\,\mathrm{d}t\right]\leq C_{p}e^{C_{p}T}\xi^{p}.

(ii)

For any
p\in[2,\infty)
, there exists a constant
C_{p}\in(0,\infty)
, which depends only on
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\theta
and
p
, such that

\mathbb{E}\big[\|X^{-1}_{T}\|_{\infty}^{p}\big]\leq C_{p}e^{C_{p}T}\big(1+\xi^{-p}\big).

Proof.

Assertion (ii) follows from
Lemma

B.1
(ii). We show assertion (i).

Let
T\in(0,\infty)
and
p\in(0,\frac{2\kappa_{1}}{\theta^{2}}+1)
be fixed. We denote by
C_{p}
a positive constant which depends only on
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\theta
and
p
and varies from line to line. By Itô’s formula, we have

\begin{split}&X(t)^{p}+p\left\{\kappa_{1}-\frac{(p-1)\theta^{2}}{2}\right\}\int^{t}_{0}X(s)^{p+1}\,\mathrm{d}s=\xi^{p}+p\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}\int^{t}_{0}X(s)^{p}\,\mathrm{d}s+p\theta\int^{t}_{0}X(s)^{p+1/2}\,\mathrm{d}W(s)\end{split}

(6.32)

for any
t\in[0,\infty)

\mathbb{P}
-a.s.
For each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, define a stopping time
\tau_{N}
by
\tau_{N}:=\inf\{t\geq 0\,|\,X(t)\geq N\}
.
Notice that
\tau_{N}\to\infty
as
N\to\infty

\mathbb{P}
-a.s. Take an arbitrary
T_{1}\in[0,T]
. On the one hand, letting
t=T_{1}\wedge\tau_{N}
and taking expectations in (
6.32
), we see that

p\left\{\kappa_{1}-\frac{(p-1)\theta^{2}}{2}\right\}\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T_{1}\wedge\tau_{N}}_{0}X(s)^{p+1}\,\mathrm{d}s\right]\leq\xi^{p}+p\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T_{1}\wedge\tau_{N}}_{0}X(s)^{p}\,\mathrm{d}s\right].

Since
\kappa_{1}-\frac{(p-1)\theta^{2}}{2}>0
, we get

\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T_{1}\wedge\tau_{N}}_{0}X(s)^{p+1}\,\mathrm{d}s\right]\leq C_{p}\left\{\xi^{p}+\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T_{1}\wedge\tau_{N}}_{0}X(s)^{p}\,\mathrm{d}s\right]\right\}.

(6.33)

On the other hand, considering the supremum with respect to
t\in[0,T_{1}\wedge\tau_{N}]
and taking expectations in (
6.32
), by the Burkholder–Davis–Gundy inequality, we see that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T_{1}]}X(t\wedge\tau_{N})^{p}\right]\leq C_{p}\left\{\xi^{p}+\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T_{1}\wedge\tau_{N}}_{0}X(s)^{p}\,\mathrm{d}s\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int^{T_{1}\wedge\tau_{N}}_{0}X(s)^{2p+1}\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}\right]\right\}.

From this, together with the estimate

\displaystyle C_{p}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int^{T_{1}\wedge\tau_{N}}_{0}X(s)^{2p+1}\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq C_{p}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\in[0,T_{1}]}X(s\wedge\tau_{N})^{p/2}\left(\int^{T_{1}\wedge\tau_{N}}_{0}X(s)^{p+1}\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\in[0,T_{1}]}X(s\wedge\tau_{N})^{p}\right]+C_{p}\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T_{1}\wedge\tau_{N}}_{0}X(s)^{p+1}\,\mathrm{d}s\right],

we obtain

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T_{1}]}X(t\wedge\tau_{N})^{p}\right]&\leq C_{p}\left\{\xi^{p}+\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T_{1}\wedge\tau_{N}}_{0}X(s)^{p}\,\mathrm{d}s\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T_{1}\wedge\tau_{N}}_{0}X(s)^{p+1}\,\mathrm{d}s\right]\right\}.\end{split}

(6.34)

By (
6.33
) and (
6.34
), we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T_{1}]}X(t\wedge\tau_{N})^{p}\right]

\displaystyle\leq C_{p}\left\{\xi^{p}+\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T_{1}\wedge\tau_{N}}_{0}X(s)^{p}\,\mathrm{d}s\right]\right\}\leq C_{p}\left\{\xi^{p}+\int^{T_{1}}_{0}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,s]}X(t\wedge\tau_{N})^{p}\right]\,\mathrm{d}s\right\}.

Noting that
T_{1}\in[0,T]
is arbitrary, Gronwall’s inequality yields

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}X(t\wedge\tau_{N})^{p}\right]\leq C_{p}e^{C_{p}T}\xi^{p}.

From this estimate and (
6.33
) with
T_{1}=T
, letting
N\to\infty
and using Fatou’s lemma, we obtain the desired estimate in assertion (i).
∎

By
Lemma

6.15
and
Theorem

3.1
(iii), we can obtain the following convergence order for the weak approximation (
6.31
) under the slightly stronger condition
2\kappa_{1}+\theta^{2}>0
.

Theorem 6.16
.

Let
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\theta\in(0,\infty)
satisfy
2\kappa_{1}+\theta^{2}>0
, and let
(X,W,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
be the weak solution of the SDE (
6.29
) on
D=(0,\infty)
with initial condition
X(0)=\xi\in D
.
For each
T\in(0,\infty)
and
\pi\in\Pi_{T}
, let
X^{\pi}
be the Euler–Maruyama scheme defined on
(\Omega^{\pi},\mathcal{F}^{\pi},\mathbb{P}^{\pi})
and given by (
4.8
) with initial condition
X^{\pi}(t_{0})=\xi
.
Then, for any

\gamma\in\left(0,\frac{2\kappa_{1}+\theta^{2}}{4\kappa_{1}+7\theta^{2}}\right),

there exists a constant
C_{\gamma}\in(0,\infty)
such that

d_{\mathrm{LP}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T}),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}])\big)\leq C_{\gamma}|\pi|^{\gamma}

for any
\pi\in\Pi_{T}
.

Proof.

Recall the choices of the subsets in (
6.30
). For any
\Delta\in(0,1]
and
\vec{R}=(R_{\mathrm{G},b},R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{C},b},R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{E}})\in[1,\infty)^{5}
, noting that
\Delta\leq 1\leq\min\left\{R^{1/2}_{\mathrm{G},b},R^{2/3}_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{C},b},R^{2}_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}\right\}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{t\in[0,T]}\mathrm{dist}\!\left(X(t),\mathbb{R}\setminus D_{T}(\vec{R})\right)\leq\Delta\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(\text{$X(t)\geq 2\min\left\{R^{1/2}_{\mathrm{G},b},R^{2/3}_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{C},b},R^{2}_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}\right\}-\Delta$}\vphantom{\frac{1}{R^{2/3}_{\mathrm{E}}}}\ \text{or $X(t)\leq\frac{1}{R^{2/3}_{\mathrm{E}}}+\Delta$ for some $t\in[0,T]$}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}X(t)\geq\min\left\{R^{1/2}_{\mathrm{G},b},R^{2/3}_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{C},b},R^{2}_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}\right\}\right)+\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}X(t)^{-1}\geq\left(\frac{1}{R^{2/3}_{\mathrm{E}}}+\Delta\right)^{-1}\right),

and thus, by Markov’s inequality and
Lemma

6.15
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{t\in[0,T]}\mathrm{dist}\!\left(X(t),\mathbb{R}\setminus D_{T}(\vec{R})\right)\leq\Delta\right)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\min\left\{R^{1/2}_{\mathrm{G},b},R^{2/3}_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{C},b},R^{2}_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}\right\}\right)^{-p}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}X(t)^{p}\right]+\left(\frac{1}{R^{2/3}_{\mathrm{E}}}+\Delta\right)^{q}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}X(t)^{-q}\right]

\displaystyle\leq C_{p,q}\max\left\{\Delta^{q},R_{\mathrm{G},b}^{-p/2},R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}^{-2p/3},R_{\mathrm{C},b}^{-p},R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}^{-2p},R_{\mathrm{E}}^{-2q/3}\right\},

for any
p\in(0,\frac{2\kappa_{1}}{\theta^{2}}+1)
and
q\in(0,\infty)
. Here,
C_{p,q}\in(0,\infty)
is a constant which does not depend on
\Delta
or
\vec{R}
. The above estimate shows that (
3.5
) holds for any
\vec{\beta}=(\beta_{0},\beta_{\mathrm{G},b},\beta_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},\beta_{\mathrm{C},b},\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},\beta_{\mathrm{E}})
with

\displaystyle\beta_{0},\beta_{\mathrm{E}}\in(0,\infty),\ \beta_{\mathrm{G},b}\in\left(0,\frac{\kappa_{1}}{\theta^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}\right),\ \beta_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}\in\left(0,\frac{4\kappa_{1}}{3\theta^{2}}+\frac{2}{3}\right),

\displaystyle\beta_{\mathrm{C},b}\in\left(0,\frac{2\kappa_{1}}{\theta^{2}}+1\right),\ \beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}\in\left(0,\frac{4\kappa_{1}}{\theta^{2}}+2\right).

Concerning the constants
\beta_{*}
and
\gamma_{*}
defined by (
3.7
) and (
3.6
), we have

\begin{split}\beta_{*}&=\min\left\{\beta_{0},\frac{1}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+\beta_{\mathrm{C},b}^{-1}},\frac{1}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+2\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}^{-1}}\right\}\to\frac{2\kappa_{1}+\theta^{2}}{2(\kappa_{1}+\theta^{2})},\end{split}

and

\begin{split}\gamma_{*}&=\min\left\{\frac{1}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+\beta_{\mathrm{C},b}^{-1}+\beta_{\mathrm{G},b}^{-1}},\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+\beta_{\mathrm{C},b}^{-1}+\beta_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}^{-1}},\right.\\
&\hskip 42.67912pt\left.\frac{2}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+2\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}^{-1}+2\beta_{\mathrm{G},b}^{-1}+\beta_{*}^{-1}},\frac{1}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+2\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}^{-1}+2\beta_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}^{-1}+\beta_{*}^{-1}}\right\}\\
&\to\frac{2\kappa_{1}+\theta^{2}}{4\kappa_{1}+7\theta^{2}}\end{split}

as
\beta_{0},\beta_{\mathrm{E}}\to\infty
,
\beta_{\mathrm{G},b}\to\frac{\kappa_{1}}{\theta^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}
,
\beta_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}\to\frac{4\kappa_{1}}{3\theta^{2}}+\frac{2}{3}
,
\beta_{\mathrm{C},b}\to\frac{2\kappa_{1}}{\theta^{2}}+1
and
\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}\to\frac{4\kappa_{1}}{\theta^{2}}+2
. Therefore, by
Theorem

3.1
(iii), we get the desired estimate. This completes the proof.
∎

6.10
Dyson’s Brownian motion

Let
d=n\geq 2
, and consider the following SDE defined on
D=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}|\,x_{1}>x_{2}>\cdots>x_{n}\}
:

\mathrm{d}X_{i}(t)=\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{\kappa}{X_{i}(t)-X_{j}(t)}\,\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}W_{i}(t),\ \ t\in[0,\infty),\ \ i\in\{1,\dots,n\},\ \ X(0)=\xi\in D,

(6.35)

where
\kappa
is a fixed positive constant. The SDE (
6.35
) has a unique strong solution
X=(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})^{\top}
satisfying
\mathbb{P}(\text{$X(t)\in D$ for any $t\in[0,\infty)$})=1
if and only if
\kappa\geq 1/2
. Indeed, the “if part” (that is, the sufficiency of
\kappa\geq 1/2
) is shown in
[
17
, Theorem 3.1 and Proposition 4.1]
and
[
64
, Lemma 1]
, and the “only if part” (that is, the necessity of
\kappa\geq 1/2
) can be shown by comparing the dynamics of the process
X_{i,i+1}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(X_{i}-X_{i+1})
(see (
6.38
) below) with the Bessel process for each
i\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
. The solution
X
of the SDE (
6.35
) is called Dyson’s Brownian motion, which arises in mathematical physics as a non-colliding particle system
[
26
]
and in random matrix theory as dynamics of the eigenvalues of some matrix-valued Brownian motions
[
6
,
26
]
.

Numerical approximations for Dyson’s Brownian motion are studied in
[
24
,
60
,
61
]
, where the authors provide convergence rates in the
L^{p}
-sup sense for some
p\geq 2
(which depends on
\kappa
) by means of the backward/truncated Euler–Maruyama schemes assuming that
\kappa
is sufficiently large; for example,
[
24
, Theorem 4.2]
requires that
\kappa>9/2
.

Let
\kappa\geq 1/2
. As discussed in
Section

4.4.1
, the Markovian SDE (
6.35
) on
D=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}|\,x_{1}>x_{2}>\cdots>x_{n}\}
fits into the framework of the present paper. More precisely, the data satisfies
Assumption

1
with the choices of the exponents
\alpha_{b}=\alpha_{\sigma}=1
, the sets

\begin{split}D_{\mathrm{G},b;T}(R)=\left\{x\in D\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}x_{i}-x_{j}>\frac{1}{R}\ \text{for any $1\leq i<j\leq n$}\right\},\\
D_{\mathrm{C},b;T}(R)=\left\{x\in D\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}x_{i}-x_{j}>\frac{1}{R^{1/2}}\ \text{for any $1\leq i<j\leq n$}\right\},\\
D_{\mathrm{G},\sigma;T}(R)=D_{\mathrm{C},\sigma;T}(R)=D_{\mathrm{E};T}(R)=D,\ \ R\in[1,\infty),\end{split}

(6.36)

and some constants
K_{\mathrm{G},b;T},K_{\mathrm{G},\sigma;T},K_{\mathrm{C},b;T},K_{\mathrm{C},\sigma;T},K_{\mathrm{E};T}
depending only on
\kappa
and
n
.

Since
\mathbb{P}(\text{$X(t)\in D$ for any $t\in[0,\infty)$})=1
,
Theorem

3.1
(i) shows that

\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}]\to X_{T}\ \ \text{weakly on $\mathcal{C}_{T}$ as $|\pi|\downarrow 0$ along $\pi\in\Pi_{T}$ for any $T\in(0,\infty)$},

(6.37)

where
X^{\pi}=(X^{\pi}(t_{k}))^{m}_{k=0}
is the standard Euler–Maruyama scheme given by (
4.8
) with initial condition
X^{\pi}(t_{0})=\xi
. In order to get a weak convergence order with respect to the Lévy–Prokhorov metric, we use
Theorem

3.1
(iii). To do so, we first investigate moment estimates corresponding to the “rare event” appearing in (
3.5
). In the literature, it has been shown that

\max_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}\left[\big(X_{i}(t)-X_{j}(t)\big)^{-p}\right]<\infty

for any
p\in(0,2\kappa-1)
under the condition
\kappa>1/2
, and

\max_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\big(X_{i}(t)-X_{j}(t)\big)^{-p}\right]<\infty

for any
p\in(0,2\kappa-3)
under the condition
\kappa>3/2
(see
[
24
, Theorem 2.7]
). Notice that there is a gap between the above two estimates in terms of the parameters
\kappa
and
p
.
The following lemma fills the gap and improves the above results.

Lemma 6.17
.

Assume that
\kappa>1/2
, and let
(X,W,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
be the weak solution of the SDE (
6.35
) on
D=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}|\,x_{1}>x_{2}>\cdots>x_{n}\}
with initial condition
X(0)=\xi\in D
. Then, for any
p\in(0,2\kappa-1)
, there exists a constant
C_{p}\in(0,\infty)
, which depends only on
\kappa,n
and
p
, such that

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,\infty)}\big(X_{i}(t)-X_{j}(t)\big)^{-p}+\int^{\infty}_{0}\big(X_{i}(t)-X_{j}(t)\big)^{-p-2}\,\mathrm{d}t\right]\leq C_{p}\max_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\big(\xi_{i}-\xi_{j}\big)^{-p}.

Proof.

For each
1\leq i<j\leq n
, we set
X_{i,j}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(X_{i}-X_{j})
and
W_{i,j}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(W_{i}-W_{j})
.
Notice that
W_{i,j}
is a one-dimensional standard Brownian motion and that,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.,
X_{i,j}(t)>0
for any
t\in[0,\infty)
. Moreover,
X_{i,j}
evolves as

\begin{split}\mathrm{d}X_{i,j}(t)&=\mathrm{d}W_{i,j}(t)+\kappa X_{i,j}(t)^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}t-\frac{\kappa}{2}\left\{\sum_{k\leq i-1}X_{k,i}(t)^{-1}+\sum_{\ell\geq j+1}X_{j,\ell}(t)^{-1}\right\}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt+\frac{\kappa}{2}\left\{\sum_{k\leq j-1,k\neq i}X_{k,j}(t)^{-1}+\sum_{\ell\geq i+1,\ell\neq j}X_{i,\ell}(t)^{-1}\right\}\,\mathrm{d}t,\ \ t\in[0,\infty),\end{split}

(6.38)

with initial condition
X_{i,j}(0)=\xi_{i,j}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\xi_{i}-\xi_{j})>0
. In the above expression, the summation with respect to an empty set of indexes is understood to be zero. Let
p\in(0,2\kappa-1)
be fixed. We show that

\max_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,\infty)}X_{i,j}(t)^{-p}+\int^{\infty}_{0}X_{i,j}(t)^{-p-2}\,\mathrm{d}t\right]\leq C_{p}\max_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\xi_{i,j}^{-p}

(6.39)

for some constant
C_{p}\in(0,\infty)
which depends only on
\kappa,n
and
p
. To do so, we first show that there exist some positive constants
a_{1},\dots,a_{n-1}
, which depend only on
\kappa,n
and
p
, such that

\begin{split}&\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}a_{j-i}X_{i,j}(t)^{-p}+p\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\int_{0}^{t}X_{i,j}(s)^{-p-2}\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&\leq\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}a_{j-i}\xi_{i,j}^{-p}-p\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}a_{j-i}\int_{0}^{t}X_{i,j}(s)^{-p-1}\,\mathrm{d}W_{i,j}(s)\end{split}

(6.40)

for any
t\in[0,\infty)

\mathbb{P}
-a.s.

By Itô’s formula, for each
1\leq i<j\leq n
, we have

\displaystyle X_{i,j}(t)^{-p}

\displaystyle=\xi_{i,j}^{-p}-p\int_{0}^{t}X_{i,j}(s)^{-p-1}\,\mathrm{d}W_{i,j}(s)-p\left(\kappa-\frac{p+1}{2}\right)\int_{0}^{t}X_{i,j}(s)^{-p-2}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{p\kappa}{2}\int_{0}^{t}X_{i,j}(s)^{-p-1}\left\{\sum_{k\leq i-1}X_{k,i}(s)^{-1}+\sum_{\ell\geq j+1}X_{j,\ell}(s)^{-1}\right\}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{p\kappa}{2}\int_{0}^{t}X_{i,j}(s)^{-p-1}\left\{\sum_{k\leq j-1,k\neq i}X_{k,j}(s)^{-1}+\sum_{\ell\geq i+1,\ell\neq j}X_{i,\ell}(s)^{-1}\right\}\,\mathrm{d}s,

and hence

\displaystyle X_{i,j}(t)^{-p}+p\left(\kappa-\frac{p+1}{2}\right)\int^{t}_{0}X_{i,j}(s)^{-p-2}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\xi_{i,j}^{-p}-p\int^{t}_{0}X_{i,j}(s)^{-p-1}\,\mathrm{d}W_{i,j}(s)+\frac{p\kappa}{2}\int^{t}_{0}X_{i,j}(s)^{-p-1}\left\{\sum_{k\leq i-1}X_{k,i}(s)^{-1}+\sum_{\ell\geq j+1}X_{j,\ell}(s)^{-1}\right\}\,\mathrm{d}s.

Let
a_{1},\dots,a_{n-1}
be positive constants, which will be determined later. Multiplying
a_{j-i}
to both sides of the above inequality and then summing up over
1\leq i<j\leq n
, we get

\begin{split}&\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}a_{j-i}X_{i,j}(t)^{-p}+p\left(\kappa-\frac{p+1}{2}\right)\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}a_{j-i}\int^{t}_{0}X_{i,j}(s)^{-p-2}\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&\leq\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}a_{j-i}\xi_{i,j}^{-p}-p\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}a_{j-i}\int^{t}_{0}X_{i,j}(s)^{-p-1}\,\mathrm{d}W_{i,j}(s)\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt+\frac{p\kappa}{2}\int^{t}_{0}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}a_{j-i}X_{i,j}(s)^{-p-1}\left\{\sum_{k\leq i-1}X_{k,i}(s)^{-1}+\sum_{\ell\geq j+1}X_{j,\ell}(s)^{-1}\right\}\,\mathrm{d}s.\end{split}

(6.41)

We now consider the integrand of the last term in the right hand side of (
6.41
). Observe that

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}a_{j-i}X_{i,j}(s)^{-p-1}\left\{\sum_{k\leq i-1}X_{k,i}(s)^{-1}+\sum_{\ell\geq j+1}X_{j,\ell}(s)^{-1}\right\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq i<j<k\leq n}\Big\{a_{j-i}X_{i,j}(s)^{-p-1}X_{j,k}(s)^{-1}+a_{k-j}X_{i,j}(s)^{-1}X_{j,k}(s)^{-p-1}\Big\}.

We fix
1\leq i<j<k\leq n
. On the one hand, if

X_{i,j}(s)\geq(a_{j-i}+a_{k-j}+1)X_{j,k}(s)\ \text{or}\ X_{j,k}(s)\geq(a_{j-i}+a_{k-j}+1)X_{i,j}(s),

then it holds that

\displaystyle a_{j-i}X_{i,j}(s)^{-p-1}X_{j,k}(s)^{-1}+a_{k-j}X_{i,j}(s)^{-1}X_{j,k}(s)^{-p-1}\leq X_{i,j}(s)^{-p-2}+X_{j,k}(s)^{-p-2}.

On the other hand, if

X_{i,j}(s)<(a_{j-i}+a_{k-j}+1)X_{j,k}(s)\ \text{and}\ X_{j,k}(s)<(a_{j-i}+a_{k-j}+1)X_{i,j}(s),

then we have

\displaystyle X_{i,k}(s)=X_{i,j}(s)+X_{j,k}(s)<(a_{j-i}+a_{k-j}+2)(X_{i,j}(s)\wedge X_{j,k}(s)),

and hence it holds that

\displaystyle a_{j-i}X_{i,j}(s)^{-p-1}X_{j,k}(s)^{-1}+a_{k-j}X_{i,j}(s)^{-1}X_{j,k}(s)^{-p-1}<\big(a_{j-i}+a_{k-j}+2\big)^{p+3}X_{i,k}(s)^{-p-2}.

Therefore, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}a_{j-i}X_{i,j}(s)^{-p-1}\left\{\sum_{k\leq i-1}X_{k,i}(s)^{-1}+\sum_{\ell\geq j+1}X_{j,\ell}(s)^{-1}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{1\leq i<j<k\leq n}\Big\{X_{i,j}(s)^{-p-2}+X_{j,k}(s)^{-p-2}+\big(a_{j-i}+a_{k-j}+2\big)^{p+3}X_{i,k}(s)^{-p-2}\Big\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\left\{n-j+i-1+\sum_{1\leq\ell\leq j-i-1}\big(a_{\ell}+a_{j-i-\ell}+2\big)^{p+3}\right\}X_{i,j}(s)^{-p-2}.

From the above estimate, together with (
6.41
), we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}a_{j-i}X_{i,j}(t)^{-p}+p\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\widehat{a}_{j-i}\int_{0}^{t}X_{i,j}(s)^{-p-2}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}a_{j-i}\xi_{i,j}^{-p}-p\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}a_{j-i}\int_{0}^{t}X_{i,j}(s)^{-p-1}\,\mathrm{d}W_{i,j}(s),

where
\widehat{a}_{1},\dots,\widehat{a}_{n-1}
are defined by

\widehat{a}_{m}:=a_{m}\left(\kappa-\frac{p+1}{2}\right)-\frac{\kappa}{2}\left\{n-m-1+\sum_{1\leq\ell\leq m-1}\big(a_{\ell}+a_{m-\ell}+2\big)^{p+3}\right\}

for
m\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
. Notice that the summation term in the expression of
\widehat{a}_{m}
is equal to zero for
m=1
and independent of
a_{m},\dots,a_{n-1}
for
2\leq m\leq n-1
. Thus, noting that
\kappa-\frac{p+1}{2}>0
, we can set positive constants
a_{1},\dots,a_{n-1}
inductively such that
\widehat{a}_{m}=1
for any
m\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
. Then, we get the estimate (
6.40
). Notice that the constants
a_{1},\dots,a_{n-1}
depend only on
\kappa,n
and
p
.

For
N\in\mathbb{N}
, define a stopping time
\tau_{N}
by

\tau_{N}:=\inf\left\{t\geq 0\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}X_{i,j}(t)\leq 1/N\,\text{for some}\,1\leq i<j\leq n\right\}.

Notice that
\tau_{N}\to\infty
as
N\to\infty

\mathbb{P}
-a.s. By (
6.40
), we have

\max_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{\tau_{N}}X_{i,j}(s)^{-p-2}\,\mathrm{d}s\right]\leq C_{p}\max_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\xi_{i,j}^{-p}.

(6.42)

Here and in the rest of this proof,
C_{p}\in(0,\infty)
denotes a constant which depends only on
\kappa,n
and
p
and varies from line to line. Taking supremum with respect to
t\in[0,\tau_{N}]
and then taking the expectations in (
6.40
), we have

\displaystyle a_{j^{\prime}-i^{\prime}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,\tau_{N}]}X_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}(t)^{-p}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}a_{j-i}\xi_{i,j}^{-p}+p\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,\tau_{N}]}\left|\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}a_{j-i}\int^{t}_{0}X_{i,j}(s)^{-p-1}\,\mathrm{d}W_{i,j}(s)\right|\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}a_{j-i}\xi_{i,j}^{-p}+p\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}a_{j-i}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,\tau_{N}]}\left|\int^{t}_{0}X_{i,j}(s)^{-p-1}\,\mathrm{d}W_{i,j}(s)\right|\right]

for any
1\leq i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}\leq n
, and thus,

\begin{split}\max_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,\tau_{N}]}X_{i,j}(t)^{-p}\right]&\leq C_{p}\max_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\xi_{i,j}^{-p}+C_{p}\max_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,\tau_{N}]}\left|\int^{t}_{0}X_{i,j}(s)^{-p-1}\,\mathrm{d}W_{i,j}(s)\right|\right].\end{split}

(6.43)

Concerning the second term above, by using the Burkholder–Davis–Gundy inequality and Young’s inequality, we get

\displaystyle C_{p}\max_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,\tau_{N}]}\left|\int^{t}_{0}X_{i,j}(s)^{-p-1}\,\mathrm{d}W_{i,j}(s)\right|\right]

\displaystyle\leq C_{p}\max_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int^{\tau_{N}}_{0}X_{i,j}(s)^{-2p-2}\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq C_{p}\max_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,\tau_{N}]}X_{i,j}(t)^{-p/2}\left(\int^{\tau_{N}}_{0}X_{i,j}(s)^{-p-2}\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\max_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,\tau_{N}]}X_{i,j}(t)^{-p}\right]+C_{p}\max_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{\tau_{N}}_{0}X_{i,j}(s)^{-p-2}\,\mathrm{d}s\right]

(6.44)

By (
6.42
), (
6.43
) and (
6.44
), we obtain

\max_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,\tau_{N}]}X_{i,j}(t)^{-p}+\int^{\tau_{N}}_{0}X_{i,j}(s)^{-p-2}\,\mathrm{d}s\right]\leq C_{p}\max_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\xi_{i,j}^{-p}.

By taking the limit
N\to\infty
and using Fatou’s lemma, we obtain the desired estimate (
6.39
). This completes the proof.
∎

By
Lemma

6.17
and
Theorem

3.1
(iii), we can obtain the following convergence order for the weak approximation (
6.37
) under the slightly stronger condition
\kappa>1/2
.

Theorem 6.18
.

Assume that
\kappa>1/2
, and let
(X,W,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
be the weak solution of the SDE (
6.35
) on
D=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}|\,x_{1}>x_{2}>\cdots>x_{n}\}
with initial condition
X(0)=\xi\in D
.
For each
T\in(0,\infty)
and
\pi\in\Pi_{T}
, let
X^{\pi}
be the Euler–Maruyama scheme defined on
(\Omega^{\pi},\mathcal{F}^{\pi},\mathbb{P}^{\pi})
and given by (
4.8
) with initial condition
X^{\pi}(t_{0})=\xi
.
Then, for any

\gamma\in\left(0,\frac{2\kappa-1}{2(2\kappa+1)}\right),

there exists a constant
C_{\gamma}\in(0,\infty)
such that

d_{\mathrm{LP}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(X_{T}),\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\pi}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[X^{\pi}])\big)\leq C_{\gamma}|\pi|^{\gamma}

for any
\pi\in\Pi_{T}
.

Proof.

Noting (
6.36
), for any
\Delta\in(0,1]
and
\vec{R}=(R_{\mathrm{G},b},R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{C},b},R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{E}})\in[1,\infty)^{5}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{t\in[0,T]}\mathrm{dist}\!\left(X(t),\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus D_{T}(\vec{R})\right)\leq\Delta\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(X_{i}(t)-X_{j}(t)\leq\frac{1}{R_{\mathrm{G},b}\wedge R^{1/2}_{\mathrm{C},b}}+\sqrt{2}\Delta\ \ \text{for some $1\leq i<j\leq n$ and $t\in[0,T]$}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\big(X_{i}(t)-X_{j}(t)\big)^{-1}\geq\left(\frac{1}{R_{\mathrm{G},b}\wedge R^{1/2}_{\mathrm{C},b}}+\sqrt{2}\Delta\right)^{-1}\right),

and thus, by Markov’s inequality and
Lemma

6.17
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{t\in[0,T]}\mathrm{dist}\!\left(X(t),\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus D_{T}(\vec{R})\right)\leq\Delta\right)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{1}{R_{\mathrm{G},b}\wedge R^{1/2}_{\mathrm{C},b}}+\sqrt{2}\Delta\right)^{p}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\big(X_{i}(t)-X_{j}(t)\big)^{-p}\right]

\displaystyle\leq C_{p}\max\left\{\Delta^{p},R_{\mathrm{G},b}^{-p},R_{\mathrm{C},b}^{-p/2}\right\},

for any
p\in(0,2\kappa-1)
. Here,
C_{p}\in(0,\infty)
is a constant which does not depend on
\Delta
or
\vec{R}
. The above estimate shows that (
3.5
) holds for any
\vec{\beta}=(\beta_{0},\beta_{\mathrm{G},b},\beta_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},\beta_{\mathrm{C},b},\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},\beta_{\mathrm{E}})
with

\beta_{0},\beta_{\mathrm{G},b}\in(0,2\kappa-1),\ \beta_{\mathrm{C},b}\in\left(0,\kappa-\frac{1}{2}\right),\ \beta_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},\beta_{\mathrm{E}}\in(0,\infty).

Concerning the constants
\beta_{*}
and
\gamma_{*}
defined by (
3.7
) and (
3.6
), we have

\begin{split}\beta_{*}&=\min\left\{\beta_{0},\frac{1}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+\beta_{\mathrm{C},b}^{-1}},\frac{1}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+2\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}^{-1}}\right\}\to\frac{2\kappa-1}{2\kappa+1}\end{split}

and

\begin{split}\gamma_{*}&=\min\left\{\frac{1}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+\beta_{\mathrm{C},b}^{-1}+\beta_{\mathrm{G},b}^{-1}},\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+\beta_{\mathrm{C},b}^{-1}+\beta_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}^{-1}},\right.\\
&\hskip 42.67912pt\left.\frac{2}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+2\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}^{-1}+2\beta_{\mathrm{G},b}^{-1}+\beta_{*}^{-1}},\frac{1}{1+\beta_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}+2\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma}^{-1}+2\beta_{\mathrm{G},\sigma}^{-1}+\beta_{*}^{-1}}\right\}\\
&\to\frac{2\kappa-1}{2(2\kappa+1)}\end{split}

as
\beta_{0},\beta_{\mathrm{G},b}\to 2\kappa-1
,
\beta_{\mathrm{C},b}\to\kappa-\frac{1}{2}
and
\beta_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},\beta_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},\beta_{\mathrm{E}}\to\infty
. Therefore, noting
Remark

3.2
, by
Theorem

3.1
(iii), we get the desired estimate. This completes the proof.
∎

Appendix

Appendix A
Quantitative estimate for Lévy–Prokhorov metric under transformation

The following lemma provides a quantitative estimate for the Lévy–Prokhorov metric under uniformly continuous transformation. This ensures that quantitative estimates for the weak approximation of the transformed SFDEs in
Section

4.4.3
and
Section

4.4.4
are carried over to those of the original reflected SDEs and stochastic oscillator models.

Lemma A.1
.

Let
(S,d_{S})
and
(T,d_{T})
be complete separable metric spaces, and let
f:S\to T
be a uniformly continuous map. Let
\rho_{f}:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
be a strictly increasing, continuous function with
\rho_{f}(0)=0
and
\rho_{f}(a)\geq a
for any
a\in[0,1]
such that

d_{T}\big(f(s_{1}),f(s_{2})\big)\leq\rho_{f}\big(d_{S}(s_{1},s_{2})\big)

for all
s_{1},s_{2}\in S
. Then, it holds that

d_{\mathrm{LP},T}\big(\mu_{1}\circ f^{-1},\mu_{2}\circ f^{-1}\big)\leq\rho_{f}\big(d_{\mathrm{LP},S}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\big)

(A.1)

for all
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in\mathcal{P}(S)
, where
d_{\mathrm{LP},S}:\mathcal{P}(S)\times\mathcal{P}(S)\to[0,1]
and
d_{\mathrm{LP},T}:\mathcal{P}(T)\times\mathcal{P}(T)\to[0,1]
denote the Lévy–Prokhorov metrics on
\mathcal{P}(S)
and
\mathcal{P}(T)
corresponding to
(S,d_{S})
and
(T,d_{T})
, respectively.

Proof.

Let
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in\mathcal{P}(S)
be given. Since the left-hand side of (
A.1
) is not greater than
1
, we may assume that
\rho_{f}(d_{\mathrm{LP},S}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}))<1
. Let
\rho_{f}(d_{\mathrm{LP},S}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}))<\varepsilon<1
. Since
\rho_{f}
is strictly increasing and satisfies
\rho_{f}(a)\geq a
for any
a\in[0,1]
, we have
d_{\mathrm{LP},S}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})<\rho_{f}^{-1}(\varepsilon)\leq\varepsilon
. Hence, by the coupling representation (
2.2
) of the Lévy–Prokhorov metric, there exists a coupling
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{P}(S\times S)
between the probability measures
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in\mathcal{P}(S)
such that

\mathfrak{m}\left(\left\{(s_{1},s_{2})\in S\times S\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}d_{S}(s_{1},s_{2})>\rho_{f}^{-1}(\varepsilon)\right\}\right)<\rho_{f}^{-1}(\varepsilon)\leq\varepsilon.

Define a continuous map
F:S\times S\to T\times T
by
F(s_{1},s_{2}):=(f(s_{1}),f(s_{2}))
for each
(s_{1},s_{2})\in S\times S
, and consider the probability measure
\mathfrak{m}\circ F^{-1}\in\mathcal{P}(T\times T)
. Clearly,
\mathfrak{m}\circ F^{-1}
is a coupling between the probability measures
\mu_{1}\circ f^{-1},\mu_{2}\circ f^{-1}\in\mathcal{P}(T)
. Furthermore,

\displaystyle\big(\mathfrak{m}\circ F^{-1}\big)\left(\left\{(t_{1},t_{2})\in T\times T\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}d_{T}(t_{1},t_{2})>\varepsilon\right\}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{m}\left(\left\{(s_{1},s_{2})\in S\times S\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}d_{T}\big(f(s_{1}),f(s_{2})\big)>\varepsilon\right\}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathfrak{m}\left(\left\{(s_{1},s_{2})\in S\times S\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\rho_{f}\big(d_{S}(s_{1},s_{2})\big)>\varepsilon\right\}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{m}\left(\left\{(s_{1},s_{2})\in S\times S\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}d_{S}(s_{1},s_{2})>\rho_{f}^{-1}(\varepsilon)\right\}\right)

\displaystyle<\varepsilon.

Again by noting (
2.2
), the above observations show that

d_{\mathrm{LP},T}\big(\mu_{1}\circ f^{-1},\mu_{2}\circ f^{-1}\big)\leq\varepsilon.

Since
\rho_{f}(d_{\mathrm{LP},S}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}))<\varepsilon<1
is arbitrary, we obtain the desired estimate (
A.1
).
∎

Appendix B
A priori moment estimates for weak solutions of SFDEs

The following lemma provides a standard a priori moment estimates for weak solutions of SFDEs under the one-sided linear-growth type conditions.

Lemma B.1
.

Let data
(D,\mu_{0},b,\sigma)
be given. Suppose that we are given a weak solution
(X,W,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
to the SFDE (
1.1
) associated with
(D,\mu_{0},b,\sigma)
.

(i)

Suppose that there exists a constant
\widehat{C}\in(0,\infty)
such that

\langle x(t),b(t,x)\rangle\leq\widehat{C}\big(1+\|x_{t}\|_{\infty}^{2}\big)\ \ \text{and}\ \ |\sigma(t,x)|^{2}\leq\widehat{C}\big(1+\|x_{t}\|_{\infty}^{2}\big)

(B.1)

for any
(t,x)\in\Lambda[\mathrm{supp}\,\mu_{0},D]
and that
M_{p}(\mu_{0}):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\xi|^{p}\,\mu_{0}(\mathrm{d}\xi)<\infty
for some
p\in[2,\infty)
. Then, there exists a constant
C_{p}\in(0,\infty)
, which depends only on
\widehat{C}
and
p
, such that

\mathbb{E}\big[\|X_{T}\|_{\infty}^{p}\big]\leq C_{p}e^{C_{p}T}\Big(1+M_{p}(\mu_{0})\Big)

for any
T\in(0,\infty)
.

(ii)

Let
D=(0,\infty)^{n}
. Suppose that there exists a constant
\widehat{C}\in(0,\infty)
such that

\sum^{n}_{i=1}x_{i}(t)^{-3}b_{i}(t,x)\geq-\widehat{C}\big(1+\|x_{t}^{-1}\|_{\infty}^{2}\big)\ \ \text{and}\ \ \sum^{n}_{i=1}x_{i}(t)^{-4}\sum^{d}_{j=1}\sigma_{i,j}(t,x)^{2}\leq\widehat{C}\big(1+\|x_{t}^{-1}\|_{\infty}^{2}\big)

(B.2)

for any
(t,x)\in\Lambda[\mathrm{supp}\,\mu_{0},(0,\infty)^{n}]
and that
\check{M}_{p}(\mu_{0}):=\int_{(0,\infty)^{n}}|\xi^{-1}|^{p}\,\mu_{0}(\mathrm{d}\xi)<\infty
for some
p\in[2,\infty)
. Then, there exists a constant
C_{p}\in(0,\infty)
, which depends only on
\widehat{C}
and
p
, such that

\mathbb{E}\big[\|X_{T}^{-1}\|_{\infty}^{p}\big]\leq C_{p}e^{C_{p}T}\Big(1+\check{M}_{p}(\mu_{0})\Big)

for any
T\in(0,\infty)
.

Proof.

First, we show assertion (i). Let
p\in[2,\infty)
be fixed. In this proof, we denote by
C_{p}
a positive constant depending only on
\widehat{C}
and
p
which may vary from line to line. Using Itô’s formula, we have

\displaystyle|X(t)|^{p}

\displaystyle=|X(0)|^{p}+\int^{t}_{0}\Big\{p|X(s)|^{p-2}\langle X(s),b(s,X)\rangle+\frac{p}{2}|X(s)|^{p-2}|\sigma(s,X)|^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt+\frac{p(p-2)}{2}|X(s)|^{p-4}|\sigma(s,X)^{\top}X(s)|^{2}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}}(X(s))\Big\}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\int^{t}_{0}p|X(s)|^{p-2}\big\langle X(s),\sigma(s,X)\,\mathrm{d}W(s)\big\rangle,\ \ t\in[0,\infty).

By the assumption (
B.1
), we obtain

\begin{split}&|X(t)|^{p}\leq|X(0)|^{p}+C_{p}t+C_{p}\int^{t}_{0}\|X_{s}\|_{\infty}^{p}\,\mathrm{d}s+p\int^{t}_{0}|X(s)|^{p-2}\big\langle X(s),\sigma(s,X)\,\mathrm{d}W(s)\big\rangle,\ t\in[0,\infty).\end{split}

(B.3)

For each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, define
\tau_{N}:=\inf\{t\geq 0\,|\,|X(t)|\geq N\}
and
a_{N}(t):=\mathbb{E}[\|X_{t\wedge\tau_{N}}\|_{\infty}^{p}]
,
t\in[0,\infty)
. Each
\tau_{N}
is a stopping time such that
\tau_{N}\to\infty

\mathbb{P}
-a.s. Thanks to (
B.3
), by the Burkholder–Davis–Gundy inequality, second estimate in (
B.1
) and Young’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle a_{N}(t)

\displaystyle\leq M_{p}(\mu_{0})+C_{p}t+C_{p}\int^{t}_{0}a_{N}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s+C_{p}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int^{t\wedge\tau_{N}}_{0}|X(s)|^{2p-2}(1+\|X_{s}\|_{\infty}^{2})\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq M_{p}(\mu_{0})+C_{p}t+C_{p}\int^{t}_{0}a_{N}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s+C_{p}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int^{t}_{0}\|X_{s\wedge\tau_{N}}\|_{\infty}^{p}\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}\big(1+\|X_{t\wedge\tau_{N}}\|_{\infty}^{p}\big)^{1/2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq M_{p}(\mu_{0})+C_{p}t+C_{p}\int^{t}_{0}a_{N}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s+\frac{1}{2}\big(1+a_{N}(t)\big),

and hence

a_{N}(t)\leq 1+2M_{p}(\mu_{0})+C_{p}t+C_{p}\int^{t}_{0}a_{N}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s

for any
t\in[0,\infty)
. Hence, Gronwall’s inequality yields

a_{N}(T)\leq C_{p}e^{C_{p}T}\big(1+M_{p}(\mu_{0})\big)

for any
T\in(0,\infty)
. Then, letting
N\to\infty
and using Fatou’s lemma, we obtain the desired estimate in assertion (i).

Next, we show assertion (ii). By using Itô’s formula, we see that
(X^{-1},W,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
is a weak solution of the SFDE (
1.1
) with data
((0,\infty)^{n},\check{\mu}_{0},\check{b},\check{\sigma})
, where
\check{\mu}_{0}:=\mu_{0}\circ(\xi\mapsto\xi^{-1})^{-1}
, and

\displaystyle\check{b}_{i}(t,x):=-x_{i}(t)^{2}b_{i}(t,x^{-1})+x_{i}(t)^{3}\sum^{d}_{j=1}\sigma_{i,j}(t,x^{-1})^{2},\ \ i\in\{1,\dots,n\},

\displaystyle\check{\sigma}_{i,j}(t,x):=-x_{i}(t)^{2}\sigma_{i,j}(t,x^{-1}),\ \ i\in\{1,\dots,n\},\ \ j\in\{1,\dots,d\},

for
(t,x)\in\Lambda[\mathrm{supp}\,\check{\mu}_{0},(0,\infty)^{n}]
. Clearly, the assumption (
B.2
) implies that
\check{b}
and
\check{\sigma}
satisfy (
B.1
). Hence, applying assertion (i) to
X^{-1}
instead of
X
, we obtain the conclusion of (ii). This completes the proof.
∎

Appendix C
Existence and uniqueness of the controlled Euler–Maruyama scheme

In this section, we show existence and uniqueness of the controlled Euler–Maruyama scheme
\widehat{X}^{\pi}
defined as the solution of the non-standard SFDE (
5.1
). To do so, we need the following standard lemma.

Lemma C.1
.

Let
x,y:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
be right continuous functions, and define

\tau_{x}:=\inf\left\{t\geq 0\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}x(t)=0\right\}\ \ \text{and}\ \ \tau_{y}:=\inf\left\{t\geq 0\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}y(t)=0\right\}.

If
\tau_{x}\wedge\tau_{y}<\infty
and
x(\tau_{x}\wedge\tau_{y})=y(\tau_{x}\wedge\tau_{y})
, then
\tau_{x}=\tau_{y}
.

Proof.

Assume that
\tau_{x}<\tau_{y}
. Then, we have
x(\tau_{x})=x(\tau_{x}\wedge\tau_{y})=y(\tau_{x}\wedge\tau_{y})=y(\tau_{x})
. The definition of
\tau_{y}
and the assumption
\tau_{x}<\tau_{y}
yield that
y(\tau_{x})\neq 0
. However, the definition of
\tau_{x}
and the right-continuity of
x
yield that
x(\tau_{x})=0
, leading a contradiction. Hence, we have
\tau_{y}\leq\tau_{x}
. Similarly, we can show that
\tau_{x}\leq\tau_{y}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Using the above standard fact, we show existence and uniqueness of the solution of a general version of (
5.1
).

Lemma C.2
.

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
be a filtered probability space supporting a
d
-dimensional Brownian motion
W
relative to
\mathbb{F}
and an
\mathbb{R}^{n}
-valued
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-measurable random variable
\xi
. Let
\bar{b}:\Lambda\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\bar{\sigma}:\Lambda\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
be progressively measurable maps, and let
f:\Omega\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
g:\Omega\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
be
\mathbb{F}
-progressively measurable maps. Assume that
\bar{b},\bar{\sigma},f,g
satisfy the following properties:

•

There exists an increasing sequence
(t_{k})^{\infty}_{k=0}\subset[0,\infty)
with
t_{0}=0
and
\lim_{k\to\infty}t_{k}=\infty
such that
\bar{b}(t,y)=\sum^{\infty}_{k=0}\bar{b}(t_{k},y)\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[t_{k},t_{k+1})}(t)
and
\bar{\sigma}(t,y)=\sum^{\infty}_{k=0}\bar{\sigma}(t_{k},y)\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[t_{k},t_{k+1})}(t)
for any
(t,y)\in\Lambda
;

•

There exists a constant
L\in(0,\infty)
such that
|f(\omega,t,\eta_{1})-f(\omega,t,\eta_{2})|\leq L|\eta_{1}-\eta_{2}|
for any
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
(\omega,t)\in\Omega\times[0,\infty)
. Furthermore, it holds that
\int^{T}_{0}|f(\omega,t,0)|\mathrm{d}t<\infty
for any
T\in(0,\infty)
and
\omega\in\Omega
;

•

The map
g(\omega,\cdot,\cdot):[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
is continuous for any
\omega\in\Omega
.

Then, there exists a unique (up to
\mathbb{P}
-indistinguishability)
\mathbb{R}^{n}
-valued continuous
\mathbb{F}
-adapted process
Y
on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
such that

\begin{cases}\mathrm{d}Y(t)=\bar{b}(t,Y)\,\mathrm{d}t+\bar{\sigma}(t,Y)\,\mathrm{d}W(t)+f(t,Y(t))\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[0,\tau)}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t,\ \ t\in[0,\infty),\\
Y(0)=\xi,\ \ \tau=\inf\left\{t\geq 0\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}g(t,Y(t))=0\right\}.\end{cases}

(C.1)

Proof.

First, we show that there exists a unique
\mathbb{R}^{n}
-valued continuous
\mathbb{F}
-adapted process
\widetilde{Y}
on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
such that

\begin{cases}\mathrm{d}\widetilde{Y}(t)=\bar{b}(t,\widetilde{Y})\,\mathrm{d}t+\bar{\sigma}(t,\widetilde{Y})\,\mathrm{d}W(t)+f(t,\widetilde{Y}(t))\,\mathrm{d}t,\ \ t\in[0,\infty),\\
\widetilde{Y}(0)=\xi.\end{cases}

(C.2)

Noting the assumptions that

\bar{b}(t,y)=\sum^{\infty}_{k=0}\bar{b}(t_{k},y)\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[t_{k},t_{k+1})}(t)\ \ \text{and}\ \ \bar{\sigma}(t,y)=\sum^{\infty}_{k=0}\bar{\sigma}(t_{k},y)\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[t_{k},t_{k+1})}(t),

we can construct
(\widetilde{Y}(t))_{t\in(t_{k},t_{k+1}]}
for each
k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
by the step-by-step argument. Indeed, assuming that the stopped continuous
\mathbb{F}
-adapted process
\widetilde{Y}_{t_{k}}=\widetilde{Y}(t_{k}\wedge\cdot)
is uniquely constructed for some
k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
, then
(\widetilde{Y}(t))_{t\in(t_{k},t_{k+1}]}
is constructed as the unique solution of

\mathrm{d}\widetilde{Y}(t)=\bar{b}\big(t_{k},\widetilde{Y}\big)\,\mathrm{d}t+\bar{\sigma}\big(t_{k},\widetilde{Y}\big)\,\mathrm{d}W(t)+f\big(t,\widetilde{Y}(t)\big)\,\mathrm{d}t,\ \ t\in(t_{k},t_{k+1}],

(C.3)

with initial condition
\widetilde{Y}(t_{k})
, which is given by the assumption of the induction. By progressive measurability of
\bar{b}
and
\bar{\sigma}
, we have
\bar{b}(t_{k},\widetilde{Y})=\bar{b}(t_{k},\widetilde{Y}_{t_{k}})
and
\bar{\sigma}(t_{k},\widetilde{Y})=\bar{\sigma}(t_{k},\widetilde{Y}_{t_{k}})
, and they are determined by the assumption of the induction. Thanks to the Lipschitz continuity of
f
, the equation (
C.3
) is nothing but the standard SDE with Lipschitz coefficients, and hence it admits a unique continuous
\mathbb{F}
-adapted solution
(\widetilde{Y}(t))_{t\in[t_{k},t_{k+1}]}
. By induction, we see that (
C.2
) admits a unique continuous
\mathbb{F}
-adapted solution
\widetilde{Y}
.

Next, we define

\widetilde{\tau}:=\inf\left\{t\geq 0\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}g(t,\widetilde{Y}(t))=0\right\},

which is an
\mathbb{F}
-stopping time. Then, consider the following equation:

\begin{cases}\mathrm{d}Y(t)=\bar{b}(t,Y)\,\mathrm{d}t+\bar{\sigma}(t,Y)\,\mathrm{d}W(t)+f(t,\widetilde{Y}(t))\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[0,\widetilde{\tau})}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t,\ \ t\in[0,\infty),\\
Y(0)=\xi.\end{cases}

(C.4)

Noting that the term
f(t,\widetilde{Y}(t))\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[0,\widetilde{\tau})}(t)
is given, again by the step-by-step argument, we can construct
Y
as follows:

\displaystyle Y(t)=Y(t_{k})+\bar{b}(t_{k},Y)(t-t_{k})+\bar{\sigma}(t_{k},Y)(W(t)-W(t_{k}))+\int^{t}_{t_{k}}f\big(s,\widetilde{Y}(s)\big)\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[0,\widetilde{\tau})}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s,\ \ t\in(t_{k},t_{k+1}],

for each
k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
, with the initial condition
Y(0)=\xi
. We show that the process
Y
is a solution to the SFDE (
C.1
). To do so, define an
\mathbb{F}
-stopping time
\tau
by

\tau:=\inf\left\{t\geq 0\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}g(t,Y(t))=0\right\}.

First, we show that

Y(t\wedge\tau\wedge\widetilde{\tau})=\widetilde{Y}(t\wedge\tau\wedge\widetilde{\tau})\ \ \text{for any $t\in[0,\infty)$}.

(C.5)

To do so, we show that
Y_{t_{k}\wedge\tau\wedge\widetilde{\tau}}=\widetilde{Y}_{t_{k}\wedge\tau\wedge\widetilde{\tau}}
inductively with respect to
k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
. Clearly, the equality holds for
k=0
. Let
k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
be fixed, and assume that
Y_{t_{k}\wedge\tau\wedge\widetilde{\tau}}=\widetilde{Y}_{t_{k}\wedge\tau\wedge\widetilde{\tau}}
. Then, on the event
\{t_{k}<\tau\wedge\widetilde{\tau}\}\in\mathcal{F}_{t_{k}}
, we have, for any
t\in(t_{k},t_{k+1}\wedge\tau\wedge\widetilde{\tau}]
,

\displaystyle Y(t)

\displaystyle=Y(t_{k})+\bar{b}(t_{k},Y)(t-t_{k})+\bar{\sigma}(t_{k},Y)(W(t)-W(t_{k}))+\int^{t}_{t_{k}}f\big(s,\widetilde{Y}(s)\big)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\widetilde{Y}(t_{k})+\bar{b}\big(t_{k},\widetilde{Y}\big)(t-t_{k})+\bar{\sigma}\big(t_{k},\widetilde{Y}\big)(W(t)-W(t_{k}))+\int^{t}_{t_{k}}f\big(s,\widetilde{Y}(s)\big)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\widetilde{Y}(t).

Hence, we get
Y_{t_{k+1}\wedge\tau\wedge\widetilde{\tau}}=\widetilde{Y}_{t_{k+1}\wedge\tau\wedge\widetilde{\tau}}
. By induction, we see that
Y_{t_{k}\wedge\tau\wedge\widetilde{\tau}}=\widetilde{Y}_{t_{k}\wedge\tau\wedge\widetilde{\tau}}
holds for any
k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
, showing (
C.5
). In particular, it holds that
g(\tau\wedge\widetilde{\tau},Y(\tau\wedge\widetilde{\tau}))=g(\tau\wedge\widetilde{\tau},\widetilde{Y}(\tau\wedge\widetilde{\tau}))
on
\{\tau\wedge\widetilde{\tau}<\infty\}
. Thus, applying
Lemma

C.1
to
x(t)=g(t,Y(t))
and
y(t)=g(t,\widetilde{Y}(t))
, we see that
\tau=\widetilde{\tau}
. Combining this equality with (
C.4
) and (
C.5
), we see that
Y
satisfies (
C.1
).

Uniqueness follows from a similar argument as above. Here, we provide a sketch of the proof. Let
Y^{\prime}
be another solution of (
C.1
) with stopping time
\tau^{\prime}=\inf\{t\geq 0\,|\,g(t,Y^{\prime}(t))=0\}
. The step-by-step argument and the Lipschitz continuity of
f
yield that
Y_{t_{k}\wedge\tau\wedge\tau^{\prime}}=Y^{\prime}_{t_{k}\wedge\tau\wedge\tau^{\prime}}
for any
k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
a.s. Hence, we have
Y(t\wedge\tau\wedge\tau^{\prime})=Y^{\prime}(t\wedge\tau\wedge\tau^{\prime})
for any
t\in[0,\infty)
a.s., and in particular
g(\tau\wedge\tau^{\prime},Y(\tau\wedge\tau^{\prime}))=g(\tau\wedge\tau^{\prime},Y^{\prime}(\tau\wedge\tau^{\prime}))
on
\{\tau\wedge\tau^{\prime}<\infty\}
a.s. Applying
Lemma

C.1
to
x(t)=g(t,Y(t))
and
y(t)=g(t,Y^{\prime}(t))
, we see that
\tau=\tau^{\prime}
a.s. Hence,
f(t,Y(t))\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[0,\tau)}(t)=f(t,Y^{\prime}(t))\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[0,\tau^{\prime})}(t)
for any
t\in[0,\infty)
a.s. Using this equality, again by the step-by-step argument as above, we see that
Y=Y^{\prime}
a.s. This completes the proof.
∎

Remark C.3
.

Let
Assumption

1
hold, and suppose that we are given a weak solution
(X,W,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
of the original SFDE (
1.1
). Then, for each
T\in(0,\infty)
,
\pi\in\Pi_{T}
,
\lambda\in(0,\infty)
,
\Delta\in(0,1]
and
\vec{R}=(R_{\mathrm{G},b},R_{\mathrm{G},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{C},b},R_{\mathrm{C},\sigma},R_{\mathrm{E}})\in[1,\infty)^{5}
, applying
Lemma

C.2
to the
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-measurable initial condition
\xi(\omega)=X(\omega,0)
,
\omega\in\Omega
, the path-dependent coefficients

\bar{b}(t,y)=b(\pi(t),\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[y]),\ \bar{\sigma}(t,y)=\sigma(\pi(t),\mathfrak{p}^{\pi}[y]),\ \ (t,y)\in\Lambda,

and the
\mathbb{F}
-progressively measurable maps

\displaystyle f(\omega,t,\eta)=\frac{\lambda}{\Delta}(X(\omega,t)-\eta)\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[0,T\wedge\zeta(\omega))}(t),\ g(\omega,t,\eta)=\mathrm{dist}\left(\eta,\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B_{X(\omega,t)}(\Delta)\right),

\displaystyle\hskip 199.16928pt(\omega,t,\eta)\in\Omega\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{n},

we see that there exists a unique (up to
\mathbb{P}
-indistinguishability)
\mathbb{R}^{n}
-valued continuous and
\mathbb{F}
-adapted process
\widehat{X}^{\pi}=(\widehat{X}^{\pi}(t))_{t\in[0,\infty)}
on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
satisfying (
5.1
). This shows existence and uniqueness of the controlled Euler–Maruyama scheme.

Appendix D
Fundamental estimates for stochastic processes

In this section, we prove some fundamental estimates for stochastic processes used in the proof of
Proposition

5.3
. The following lemma is concerned with the tail-probability of the modulus of continuity of a continuous local martingale, which is used for the estimate of the term
P_{2}
appearing in (
5.8
).

Lemma D.1
.

Let
M=(M^{1},\dots,M^{n})^{\top}
be an
n
-dimensional continuous local martingale on a filtered probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
. For any
0<\delta\leq T<\infty
,
\kappa\in(0,\infty)
and
\theta\in(0,\infty)
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{i\in\{1,\dots,n\}}\varpi(T,\langle M^{i}\rangle;\delta)\leq\kappa\ \ \text{and}\ \ \varpi(T,M;\delta)\geq 2\sqrt{2n\kappa\left(\theta^{2}+\log\frac{T}{\delta}\right)}\right)\leq 4n\exp\left(-\theta^{2}\right).

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
M(0)=0
. Notice that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{i\in\{1,\dots,n\}}\varpi(T,\langle M^{i}\rangle;\delta)\leq\kappa\ \ \text{and}\ \ \varpi(T,M;\delta)\geq 2\sqrt{2n\kappa\left(\theta^{2}+\log\frac{T}{\delta}\right)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum^{n}_{i=1}\mathbb{P}\left(\varpi(T,\langle M^{i}\rangle;\delta)\leq\kappa\ \ \text{and}\ \ \varpi(T,M^{i};\delta)\geq 2\sqrt{2\kappa\left(\theta^{2}+\log\frac{T}{\delta}\right)}\right).

Thus, it suffices to show the lemma for
n=1
.

Let
M
be a one-dimensional continuous local martingale with
M(0)=0
. We show that

\mathbb{P}\left(\varpi(T,\langle M\rangle;\delta)\leq\kappa\ \ \text{and}\ \ \varpi(T,M;\delta)\geq 2\sqrt{2\kappa\left(\theta^{2}+\log\frac{T}{\delta}\right)}\right)\leq 4\exp\left(-\theta^{2}\right).

(D.1)

By the Dambis–Dubins–Schwarz theorem, enlarging the probability space (which we again denote by
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
) if necessary, we can find a one-dimensional Brownian motion
W
such that
M=W\circ\langle M\rangle
. Set
m:=\max\{j\in\mathbb{N}\,|\,j\leq\frac{T}{\delta}\}
. Notice that

\langle M\rangle(t)-\langle M\rangle(s)\leq\varpi(T,\langle M\rangle;\delta)\ \ \text{for any $0\leq s\leq t\leq T$ such that $t-s\leq\delta$},

and

\langle M\rangle(T)=\sum^{m-1}_{j=0}\big\{\langle M\rangle((j+1)\delta)-\langle M\rangle(j\delta)\big\}+\langle M\rangle(T)-\langle M\rangle(m\delta)\leq(m+1)\varpi(T,\langle M\rangle;\delta).

Hence, on the event
\{\varpi(T,\langle M\rangle;\delta)\leq\kappa\}
, we have

\displaystyle\varpi(T,M;\delta)

\displaystyle=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq s\leq t\leq T\\
t-s\leq\delta\end{subarray}}|M(t)-M(s)|

\displaystyle=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq s\leq t\leq T\\
t-s\leq\delta\end{subarray}}\big|W\big(\langle M\rangle(t)\big)-W\big(\langle M\rangle(s)\big)\big|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq s\leq t\leq(m+1)\kappa\\
t-s\leq\kappa\end{subarray}}|W(t)-W(s)|

\displaystyle\leq\max_{j\in\{0,\dots,m-1\}}\sup_{s,t\in[j\kappa,(j+2)\kappa]}|W(t)-W(s)|

\displaystyle\leq 2\max_{j\in\{0,\dots,m-1\}}\sup_{t\in[j\kappa,(j+2)\kappa]}|W(t)-W((j+1)\kappa)|

\displaystyle=2\sqrt{\kappa}\max_{j\in\{0,\dots,m-1\}}\left(\xi^{1}_{j}\vee\xi^{2}_{j}\right),

where we set

\displaystyle\xi^{1}_{j}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\kappa}}\sup_{t\in[j\kappa,(j+1)\kappa]}\big|W(t)-W((j+1)\kappa)\big|\ \ \text{and}

\displaystyle\xi^{2}_{j}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\kappa}}\sup_{t\in[(j+1)\kappa,(j+2)\kappa]}\big|W(t)-W((j+1)\kappa)\big|

for
j\in\{0,\dots,m-1\}
. Thus, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\varpi(T,\langle M\rangle;\delta)\leq\kappa\ \ \text{and}\ \ \varpi(T,M;\delta)\geq 2\sqrt{2\kappa\left(\theta^{2}+\log\frac{T}{\delta}\right)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{j\in\{0,\dots,m-1\}}\left(\xi^{1}_{j}\vee\xi^{2}_{j}\right)\geq\sqrt{2\left(\theta^{2}+\log\frac{T}{\delta}\right)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum^{m-1}_{j=0}\sum_{i\in\{1,2\}}\mathbb{P}\left(\xi^{i}_{j}\geq\sqrt{2\left(\theta^{2}+\log\frac{T}{\delta}\right)}\right).

By the time-homogeneity and the scaling property of the Brownian motion
W
, we see that
\xi^{i}_{j}\overset{d}{=}\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|W(t)|
for any
j\in\{0,\dots,m-1\}
and
i\in\{1,2\}
. Furthermore,

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|W(t)|=\max\left\{\sup_{t\in[0,1]}W(t),\sup_{t\in[0,1]}(-W(t))\right\},

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,1]}W(t)\overset{d}{=}\sup_{t\in[0,1]}(-W(t))\overset{d}{=}|W(1)|,\ \ \text{and}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(|W(1)|\geq r)\leq\exp(-r^{2}/2)\ \ \text{for any $r>0$}.

By these observations, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\varpi(T,\langle M\rangle;\delta)\leq\kappa\ \ \text{and}\ \ \varpi(T,M;\delta)\geq 2\sqrt{2\kappa\left(\theta^{2}+\log\frac{T}{\delta}\right)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2m\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|W(t)|\geq\sqrt{2\left(\theta^{2}+\log\frac{T}{\delta}\right)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 4m\mathbb{P}\left(|W(1)|\geq\sqrt{2\left(\theta^{2}+\log\frac{T}{\delta}\right)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 4m\frac{\delta}{T}\exp\left(-\theta^{2}\right).

Since
m=\max\{j\in\mathbb{N}\,|\,j\leq\frac{T}{\delta}\}\leq\frac{T}{\delta}
, the estimate (
D.1
) holds. This completes the proof.
∎

The following lemma, which is used for the estimate of the term
P_{3}
appearing in (
5.8
), is a slight refinement of
[
48
, Lemma B.1]
incorporating a logarithmic term.

Lemma D.2
.

Let
Z
be a one-dimensional nonnegative Itô process on a filtered probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
represented by

\mathrm{d}Z(t)=v(t)\,\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}N(t),\ \ t\in[0,\infty),

for some progressively measurable process
v
and one-dimensional continuous local martingale
N
with
N(0)=0
. Suppose that there exist constants
T\in(0,\infty)
,
\kappa\in(0,\infty)
,
A\in[0,\infty)
and
B\in(0,\infty)
and a nonnegative random variable
\varsigma
such that
\varsigma\leq T
and

v(t)\leq-\kappa Z(t)+A\ \ \text{and}\ \ \frac{\mathrm{d}\langle N\rangle(t)}{\mathrm{d}t}\leq B\ \ \text{for any $t\in[0,\varsigma)$ $\mathbb{P}$-a.s.}

Then, for any
\theta\in(0,\infty)
, it holds that

\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{t\in[0,\varsigma]}\big(Z(t)-e^{-\kappa t}Z(0)\big)\geq\frac{A}{\kappa}+\sqrt{\frac{2B\big(\theta^{2}+\log(1\vee(T\kappa))\big)}{\kappa}}\right)\leq 219\exp\left(-\theta^{2}\right).

(D.2)

Proof.

The arguments in the proof of Lemma B.1 in
[
48
]
show that

Z(t)=e^{-\kappa t}Z(0)+\int^{t}_{0}e^{-\kappa(t-s)}\big(v(s)+\kappa Z(s)\big)\,\mathrm{d}s+\int^{t}_{0}e^{-\kappa(t-s)}\,\mathrm{d}N(s),\ \ t\in[0,\infty),

and

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,\varsigma]}\int^{t}_{0}e^{-\kappa(t-s)}\big(v(s)+\kappa Z(s)\big)\,\mathrm{d}s\leq\frac{A}{\kappa},

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,\varsigma]}\int^{t}_{0}e^{-\kappa(t-s)}\,\mathrm{d}N(s)\leq B^{1/2}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\int^{t}_{0}e^{-\kappa(t-s)}\,\mathrm{d}W(s),

where
W
is a one-dimensional Brownian motion defined on an enlarged probability space. The estimate (
D.2
) then follows from
Lemma

D.3
below.
∎

The proof of the above lemma is based on the following estimate of the tail-probability of the supremum of a stochastic convolution (or the Ornstein–Uhlenbeck process), which is important in its own right.

Lemma D.3
.

Let
W
be a one-dimensional Brownian motion on a probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
. For any
T\in(0,\infty)
and
\kappa\in(0,\infty)
, it holds that

\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\kappa}{2}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\int^{t}_{0}e^{-\kappa(t-s)}\,\mathrm{d}W(s)\right|^{2}\right)\right]\leq 73(2+T\kappa).

(D.3)

In particular, for any
T\in(0,\infty)
,
\kappa\in(0,\infty)
and
\theta\in(0,\infty)
, it holds that

\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\int^{t}_{0}e^{-\kappa(t-s)}\,\mathrm{d}W(s)\right|\geq\sqrt{\frac{2\big(\theta^{2}+\log(1\vee(T\kappa))\big)}{\kappa}}\right)\leq 219\exp\left(-\theta^{2}\right).

(D.4)

Proof.

Define
X(t):=\int^{t}_{0}e^{-\kappa(t-s)}\,\mathrm{d}W(s)
,
t\in[0,\infty)
, and
f(x):=\exp(\frac{\kappa}{2}x^{2})
,
x\in\mathbb{R}
. We show that

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}f(X(t))\right]\leq 73(2+T\kappa).

(D.5)

Notice that
X
is an Ornstein–Uhlenbeck process such that

\mathrm{d}X(t)=-\kappa X(t)\,\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}W(t),\ \ t\in[0,\infty),\ \ X(0)=0.

Also, notice that
f(0)=1
,
f^{\prime}(x)=\kappa xf(x)
and
f^{\prime\prime}(x)=(\kappa+\kappa^{2}x^{2})f(x)
. Thus, Itô’s formula yields

\displaystyle f(X(t))

\displaystyle=1+\int^{t}_{0}\Big\{-\kappa^{2}X(s)^{2}f(X(s))+\frac{1}{2}\big(\kappa+\kappa^{2}X(s)^{2}\big)f(X(s))\Big\}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt+\int^{t}_{0}\kappa X(s)f(X(s))\,\mathrm{d}W(s),\ \ t\in[0,\infty).

Equivalently, it holds that

\displaystyle f(X(t))+\frac{\kappa^{2}}{4}\int^{t}_{0}X(s)^{2}f(X(s))\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=1+\frac{\kappa}{2}\int^{t}_{0}\left(1-\frac{\kappa}{2}X(s)^{2}\right)f(X(s))\,\mathrm{d}s+\kappa\int^{t}_{0}X(s)f(X(s))\,\mathrm{d}W(s),\ \ t\in[0,\infty).

Since
(1-\frac{\kappa}{2}x^{2})f(x)=(1-\frac{\kappa}{2}x^{2})\exp(\frac{\kappa}{2}x^{2})\leq 1
for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
, we obtain

f(X(t))+\frac{\kappa^{2}}{4}\int^{t}_{0}X(s)^{2}f(X(s))\,\mathrm{d}s\leq 1+\frac{t\kappa}{2}+\kappa\int^{t}_{0}X(s)f(X(s))\,\mathrm{d}W(s),\ \ t\in[0,\infty).

(D.6)

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
, and define
\tau_{N}:=\inf\{t\geq 0\,|\,|X(t)|\geq N\}
. Then
\tau_{N}
is a stopping time such that
\lim_{N\to\infty}\tau_{N}=\infty
a.s. Notice that the stopped stochastic integral
\int^{\cdot\wedge\tau_{N}}_{0}X(s)f(X(s))\,\mathrm{d}W(s)
is a martingale, and hence its expectation is zero. By taking the expectations in both sides of (
D.6
) with
t=T\wedge\tau_{N}
, we obtain

\frac{\kappa^{2}}{4}\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T\wedge\tau_{N}}_{0}X(s)^{2}f(X(s))\,\mathrm{d}s\right]\leq 1+\frac{T\kappa}{2}.

(D.7)

Furthermore, taking the supremum for
t\in[0,T\wedge\tau_{N}]
and then taking the expectations in both sides of (
D.6
), we have, by the Burkholder--Davis--Gundy inequality
3
3

3

Following the proof of
[
44
, Theorem 3.28]
, in the Burkholder–Davis–Gundy inequality
\mathbb{E}[\|M_{T}\|_{\infty}]\leq K_{1/2}\mathbb{E}[\langle M\rangle(T)^{1/2}]
for one-dimensional continuous local martingales starting from zero, we can take
K_{1/2}=6
.
,

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T\wedge\tau_{N}]}f(X(t))\right]\leq 1+\frac{T\kappa}{2}+6\kappa\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int^{T\wedge\tau_{N}}_{0}X(s)^{2}f(X(s))^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}\right].

Then, Young’s inequality yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T\wedge\tau_{N}]}f(X(t))\right]

\displaystyle\leq 1+\frac{T\kappa}{2}+6\kappa\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int^{T\wedge\tau_{N}}_{0}X(s)^{2}f(X(s))\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}\left(\sup_{s\in[0,T\wedge\tau_{N}]}f(X(s))\right)^{1/2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 1+\frac{T\kappa}{2}+18\kappa^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T\wedge\tau_{N}}_{0}X(s)^{2}f(X(s))\,\mathrm{d}s\right]+\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\in[0,T\wedge\tau_{N}]}f(X(s))\right].

Thanks to the stopping time
\tau_{N}
, we have
\mathbb{E}[\sup_{s\in[0,T\wedge\tau_{N}]}f(X(s))]<\infty
. Hence, the above estimate shows that

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T\wedge\tau_{N}]}f(X(t))\right]\leq 2+T\kappa+36\kappa^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T\wedge\tau_{N}}_{0}X(s)^{2}f(X(s))\,\mathrm{d}s\right].

Combining this estimate with (
D.7
), we obtain

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T\wedge\tau_{N}]}f(X(t))\right]\leq 73(2+T\kappa).

By taking the limit
N\to\infty
in this estimate and using Fatou’s lemma, we obtain (
D.5
) and equivalently (
D.3
).

As for the estimate (
D.4
), by using Markov’s inequality and (
D.3
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\int^{t}_{0}e^{-\kappa(t-s)}\,\mathrm{d}W(s)\right|\geq\sqrt{\frac{2\big(\theta^{2}+\log(1\vee(T\kappa))\big)}{\kappa}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(\exp\left(\frac{\kappa}{2}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\int^{t}_{0}e^{-\kappa(t-s)}\,\mathrm{d}W(s)\right|^{2}\right)\geq(1\vee(T\kappa))\exp\left(\theta^{2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\kappa}{2}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\int^{t}_{0}e^{-\kappa(t-s)}\,\mathrm{d}W(s)\right|^{2}\right)\right](1\vee(T\kappa))^{-1}\exp\left(-\theta^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 73(2+T\kappa)(1\vee(T\kappa))^{-1}\exp\left(-\theta^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 219\exp\left(-\theta^{2}\right).

This completes the proof.
∎
